On the asymptotic behavior at the kinetic time of a weakly interacting Fermi gas
Peter S. Madsen, Phan Thành Nam, Herbert Spohn, Minh-Binh Tran

Abstract
. This paper is devoted to the dynamics of a weakly interacting Fermi gas at the kinetic time regime t ∼ λ − 2 t\sim\lambda^{-2} where λ ≪ 1 \lambda\ll 1 is the strength of the interaction potential. We prove that if the initial state is close to equilibrium, then the two-point time correlation function of the many-body quantum dynamics can be computed effectively. In fact, we show that its leading order behavior is determined completely by the collisional frequency of the Boltzmann-Nordheim collision operator at equilibrium. This settles a prediction by Lukkarinen-Spohn [ 12 ] , and thus gives a justification of the quantum Boltzmann equation from many-body quantum mechanics.



1.
Introduction

Quantum kinetic theory has a long history, starting with the Boltzmann-Nordheim equation
[
14
]
, which describes the effective dynamics of weakly interacting quantum gases. The heuristic derivation of Nordheim via Fermi’s golden rule is indeed an ingenious guess, supported by an H-theorem and by the physically expected stationary momentum distributions.
A few years later, Uehling and Uhlenbeck
[
20
]
studied properties of this transport equation by linearizing it around equilibrium and deriving the long-time hydrodynamic approximation. Since then, the problem of deriving
irreversible kinetic equations from reversible microscopic dynamics has remained one of the central topics in
mathematical physics.

For around 100 years, there have been many attempts to make Nordheim’s argument rigorous. On the physics side, we mention in particular a series of heuristic and perturbative arguments, including the works of van Hove
[
21
]
, Prigogine
[
16
]
, Balescu
[
1
]
, and Hugenholtz
[
8
]
. From a more mathematical perspective, as argued by Erdős, Salmhofer, and Yau
[
5
]
, the emergence of quantum kinetic equations can be understood as a consequence of the quasi-free approximation on the kinetic time scale. However, turning these heuristic pictures into a mathematically controlled derivation is highly nontrivial.
The main difficulties are by now well understood: one has to isolate the leading resonant
interactions over long times, control the combinatorial complexity of the perturbation series,
and show that all subleading recollisions and interference patterns are negligible in the kinetic
limit. Addressing these issues requires methods that go substantially beyond naive perturbation theory.

This problem is closely related to the emergence of classical kinetic equations from the nonlinear Schrödinger equation in the weak-coupling regime. In
[
13
]
, Lukkarinen and Spohn investigated this problem for initial data close to equilibrium. They derived the asymptotic behavior of the solution on the kinetic time scale by means of a Duhamel expansion together with a careful graph analysis of the resulting high-dimensional oscillatory integrals. Related methods have also been used by Peierls
[
15
]
to derive kinetic equations from weakly nonlinear wave equations, and by Erdős, Salmhofer, and Yau
[
6
]
to study the long-time diffusion of a quantum particle; an earlier short-time result was obtained in
[
19
]
. As discussed in
[
12
]
, this approach is expected to be useful for the derivation of quantum kinetic equations, but the corresponding detailed analysis has not yet been carried out.

The purpose of this paper is to implement the program proposed in
[
12
]
for a weakly interacting Fermi gas.
We consider the many-body dynamics on the kinetic time scale

t\sim\lambda^{-2}
, where
\lambda\ll 1
denotes the strength of the interaction. Our initial state is assumed
to be close to thermal equilibrium, and we study the two-point time correlation function of
the Heisenberg evolution. The main result shows that, to leading order in the weak-coupling
limit, this correlation function is governed by the collisional frequency associated with the Boltzmann–Nordheim collision operator at equilibrium. In this sense,
the many-body quantum dynamics exhibits, at kinetic times, the damping predicted by the
effective quantum kinetic equation. This settles a prediction in
[
12
]
, and thus gives the first rigorous justification of
the quantum Boltzmann equation from many-body quantum mechanics, at least in this particular
scenario.

The assumptions of the paper are close to those used in the work of Lukkarinen and Spohn
on weakly nonlinear Schrödinger equations: we require
\ell^{1}
-clustering of the equilibrium
state together with dispersive, constructive-interference, and crossing estimates for the
dispersion relation. Under these assumptions, and in dimension
d\geq 4
, we obtain an
effective description of the equilibrium two-point function on a nontrivial kinetic time interval.
Although this still falls short of a full derivation of the nonlinear Boltzmann–Nordheim
equation, it gives rigorous evidence that the quantum many-body dynamics selects the same
collision mechanism as the one predicted by formal kinetic theory, at least in the regime of
small perturbations around equilibrium.

From the technical point of view, the general strategy of the present paper is to adopt the graphical methods developed in
[
13
]
in the context
of the classical discrete nonlinear Schrödinger equation. Similarly to the classical case, we
obtain an expansion in
\lambda
for the two-point time correlation function, which yields a series of
high-dimensional oscillatory integrals with zero radius of convergence. There are, however, two
differences: first, the emergence of a modified dispersion relation; and second, the need to
deal with a nonlocal interaction potential. These lead to additional challenges in the analysis.

It remains open to understand the full derivation of the nonlinear Boltzmann–Nordheim equation for arbitrary
non-equilibrium states. In the related problem in the classical setting, let us mention recent developments of Deng and Hani
[
2
,
3
]
, who derive the nonlinear kinetic equations from the kinetic-time behavior of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation. Moreover, in very recent work
[
4
]
, Deng, Hani, and Ma reported a spectacular breakthrough regarding kinetic limits. They
developed an ingenious “resummation scheme” and thereby proved convergence for arbitrary
kinetic times concerning the derivation of the classical Boltzmann equation for a dilute gas of
classical hard spheres. Hopefully, the impressive developments on classical systems in
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
will inspire further results in the quantum setting.

In the next section we describe the model, formulate the
Boltzmann–Nordheim prediction, state the assumptions, and present the main theorem.

2.
Setting and main result

We consider a system of fermions on the lattice
\Omega=\{0,\dotsc,L-1\}^{d}
(
d\geq 1
) interacting via a two-body potential
\lambda V(x-y)
.
The function
V
is fixed and the small coupling constant
\lambda\to 0^{+}
models the weakly interacting regime.
Here we impose periodic boundary conditions, which is natural to work in momentum space later.

Hamiltonian in Fock space

In the grand canonical ensemble, the system is described by the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\lambda}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathfrak{h}_{x_{k}}+\lambda\sum_{1\leq k<\ell\leq n}V(x_{k}-x_{\ell})\bigg)

on the fermionic Fock space

\mathcal{F}={}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\bigwedge^{n}\ell^{2}(\Omega)\bigg),

where
\mathfrak{h}
is a suitable one-body operator on
\ell^{2}(\Omega)
.
It is convenient to introduce the Fourier transform

\hat{f}(k)={}\sum_{x\in\Omega}f(x)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x},\quad k\in\Omega^{\ast}={}\bigg\{0,\frac{1}{L},\dotsc,\frac{L-1}{L}\bigg\}^{d}

and work instead exclusively in momentum space.
The set
\Omega^{\ast}
is a subset of the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=[0,1]^{d}
.
In this convention, the physical momentum is
p=2\pi k
, and the inverse Fourier transform is

f(x)={}\frac{1}{\lvert\Omega\rvert}\sum_{k\in\Omega^{\ast}}\hat{f}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot x},

where
\lvert\Omega\rvert=L^{d}
.
In the sequel, we also employ the following short hand notations

(2.1)

\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k={}\frac{1}{\lvert\Omega\rvert}\sum_{k\in\Omega^{\ast}}

and

(2.2)

\langle f,g\rangle={}\sum_{x\in\Omega}f(x)^{\ast}g(x),

as well as the standard Japanese bracket

(2.3)

\langle x\rangle=\sqrt{1+x^{2}},\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

In the momentum space, we will use the usual creation and annihilation operators
\hat{a}(k)^{\ast},\hat{a}(k)
, which satisfy the anti-commutation relations

(2.4)

\{\hat{a}(k),\hat{a}(k^{\prime})^{\ast}\}={}\delta_{L}(k-k^{\prime}),\quad\{\hat{a}(k),\hat{a}(k^{\prime})\}={}0,\quad\{\hat{a}(k)^{\ast},\hat{a}(k^{\prime})^{\ast}\}={}0,

where
\{A,B\}=AB+BA
, and
\delta_{L}:(\mathbb{Z}/L)^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is the discrete
\delta
-function

(2.5)

\delta_{L}(k)\coloneq L^{d}{\mathds{1}}(k\mod 1=0).

The Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
can then be rewritten as

\displaystyle H_{\lambda}=\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\omega(k)\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k)+\lambda

\displaystyle\iiiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{4}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

(2.6)

\displaystyle\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})\hat{a}(k_{1})^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{2})^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3})\hat{a}(k_{4}),

where
\omega(k)
is the dispersion relation (its properties will be specified later).

Two-point time correlation function

For any operator
A
on Fock space, the Heisenberg time evolution is defined by

A(t)\coloneq{}e^{itH_{\lambda}}Ae^{-itH_{\lambda}}.

This satisfies the equation

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}A(t)={}i[H_{\lambda},A(t)],\quad A(0)={}A.

In particular, for the annihilation operators we have inserting the expression (
2
) of
H_{\lambda}
and using (
2.4
),

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\hat{a}(k_{1},t)={}-i\omega(k_{1})\hat{a}(k_{1},t)-i\lambda

\displaystyle\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

(2.7)

\displaystyle\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t),

and similarly for the creation operators.

We are interested in the two-point time correlation function
\langle\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\rangle
.
We will restrict the attention to the initial Gibbs state
Z^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{T}H_{\lambda}}
, namely

\langle A\rangle_{\lambda}\coloneq{}\frac{1}{Z}{\rm Tr}\,(e^{-\frac{1}{T}H_{\lambda}}A),\quad Z={}{\rm Tr}\,e^{-\frac{1}{T}H_{\lambda}},

where
T>0
is the temperature.
Here we ignore the chemical potential for simplicity.
The result remains valid for a general chemical potential
\mu\neq 0
, which only changes the equilibrium background.
Moreover, we work in finite volume throughout and take the limit
L\to\infty
only in the final step (the volume dependence of the estimates is important, of course).

Lemma 2.1
.

(1)

If
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle
is a translation- and gauge invariant state on
\Omega
, then there exists a function
W_{L}^{\lambda}:\Omega^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
k,k^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\ast}
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(2.8)

\big\langle\hat{a}(k,t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\big\rangle=\delta_{L}(k-k^{\prime})W_{L}^{\lambda}(k,t),

with
0\leq W_{L}^{\lambda}(k,t)\leq 1
.

(2)

In the case of the Gibbs state, denoted by
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle_{\lambda}
, the two-point correlation function is time independent.
Furthermore, at
\lambda=0
(the non-interacting gas) and at infinite volume
\Omega\nearrow\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, it is simply

(2.9)

\langle\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime})\rangle_{0}={}\delta(k-k^{\prime})W^{0}(k),\quad W^{0}(k)={}\frac{1}{e^{\frac{1}{T}\omega(k)}+1},

for
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, where
\delta
denotes the usual
\delta
-distribution at the origin.

Proof.

By translation invariance, we immediately have

\displaystyle\big\langle\hat{a}(k,t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\big\rangle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{x,y\in\Omega}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot(x-y)}e^{-2\pi iy\cdot(k-k^{\prime})}\big\langle a(0,t)^{\ast}a(x-y,t)\big\rangle

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\Omega}e^{-2\pi iy\cdot(k-k^{\prime})}\sum_{x\in\Omega}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}\big\langle a(0,t)^{\ast}a(x,t)\big\rangle

(2.10)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\delta_{L}(k-k^{\prime})\sum_{x\in\Omega}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}\big\langle a(0,t)^{\ast}a(x,t)\big\rangle,

establishing (
2.8
) with

W_{L}^{\lambda}(k,t)=\sum_{x\in\Omega}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}\big\langle a(0,t)^{\ast}a(x,t)\big\rangle.

The fact that
0\leq W_{L}^{\lambda}
follows directly from (
2.8
) since
\hat{a}(k,t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k,t)
is a non-negative operator, and
W_{L}^{\lambda}\leq 1
follows in the same way, using the anti-commutation relation (
2.4
).

For the Gibbs state, it is clear that
\big\langle\hat{a}(k,t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\big\rangle_{\lambda}=\langle\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime})\rangle_{\lambda}
, since
e^{-itH_{\lambda}}
and
e^{-\beta H_{\lambda}}
commute.
The computation of
W^{0}(k)
for the ideal gas is well-known.
∎

Throughout the paper, we will also use the notations

(2.11)

\widetilde{W}(k)\coloneq{}1-W(k),\quad\text{and}\quad W(k,\sigma)\coloneq{}\begin{cases}W(k)&\text{if }\sigma=-1,\\
1-W(k)&\text{if }\sigma=1.\end{cases}

Our goal is to prove an effective description for the two-point time correlation function
\langle\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\rangle_{\lambda}
in the weakly interacting regime
\lambda\to 0^{+}
up to the kinetic time
t\sim\lambda^{-2}
.

Prediction by the Boltzmann-Nordheim kinetic theory

In the following heuristic discussion, we will take the formal limit
L\to\infty
and set
\mathbb{T}^{d}=[0,1]^{d}
for simplicity.
If we think of
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle
as a quasi-free state, then we can solve the Cauchy problem (
2.7
) by Duhamel expansion and find that in the kinetic time
t\lambda^{-2}=O(\lambda^{-2})
, the two-point correlation function is approximated by

(2.12)

\langle\hat{a}(k,t\lambda^{-2})^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t\lambda^{-2})\rangle\approx{}\delta(k-k^{\prime})W(k,t),

where
W(k,t)
solves the Boltzmann-Nordheim equation

(2.13)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W(k,t)={}\mathcal{C}\big(W(t)\big)(k)

with the collision operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(W)(k_{1})={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3d}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})\delta(\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})-\omega(k_{4}))

(2.14)

\displaystyle\times\lvert\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})-\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{4})\rvert^{2}\big(\widetilde{W}_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}W_{3}W_{4}-W_{1}W_{2}\widetilde{W}_{3}\widetilde{W}_{4}\big).

Here we have introduced the shorthand notation
W_{j}=W(k_{j}),j=1,2,3,4
.
Note that
W^{0}
from (
2.9
) is the equilibrium to (
2.13
), as

\mathcal{C}(W^{0})={}0.

Therefore, we may expect that
\lim_{t\to\infty}W(t)=W^{0}
.
A straightforward computation shows that the remainder

\overline{W}\coloneq{}W-W^{0}

satisfies the equation

(2.15)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\overline{W}(k_{1})={}K[\overline{W}(t)]-\mu(k_{1})\overline{W}(t)+{\rm error\ terms},

where
K
is a compact operator of convolution type, and the collisional frequency is defined to be

\displaystyle\mu(k_{1})={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3d}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})\delta(\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})-\omega(k_{4}))

(2.16)

\displaystyle\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})\big(\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})-\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{4})\big)\big(W^{0}_{3}W^{0}_{4}-W^{0}_{2}W^{0}_{4}+W^{0}_{2}\widetilde{W}^{0}_{3}\big).

The well-posedness theory for the Boltzmann–Nordheim equation (
2.13
) can be established using the techniques from
[
9
]
; in particular, one has the uniform bound
0\leq W\leq 1
provided that the initial datum satisfies the same bound (which corresponds to Pauli’s exclusion principle for fermions).
We also refer to the recent work
[
10
]
for related results.

Establishing the leading-order behavior (
2.12
) is a long-standing problem in mathematical physics. Heuristically, it follows from the idea that the many-body quantum dynamics still admits a suitable quasi-free approximation; see, for example,
[
5
]
. However, propagating such a strong bound rigorously is notoriously difficult.

In the classical setting, the analogue of (
2.12
) for the kinetic-time behavior of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation is known as the
kinetic conjecture

[
13
]
, which was resolved recently in
[
2
]
for short kinetic times and in
[
3
]
for long kinetic times.

In the following, we will justify this heuristic argument for the many-body quantum problem.
For simplicity, we will consider the simplified two-point time correlation function
\langle\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\rangle_{\lambda}
instead of
\langle\hat{a}(k,t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\rangle_{\lambda}
.

2.1.
Assumptions

Before stating our main result, we list the assumptions that will be used.
To treat the fermionic quantum case, we essentially need the same assumptions as in
[
13
]
for the weakly non-linear Schrödinger equation.
However, since we will allow for a more general class of interaction potentials, we need to consider the following
modified dispersion relation
,

(2.17)

\omega^{\lambda}\coloneq{}\omega+\lambda R_{\lambda},

with

(2.18)

R_{\lambda}(k_{1})\coloneq{}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{2})[\widehat{V}(0)-\widehat{V}(k_{1}-k_{2})],

where
W_{L}^{\lambda}
is given by
Lemma

2.1
.
The reason for introducing this modification is to absorb first order effects in
\lambda
.
This will become clear later when we expand
\hat{a}(k,t)
in a pertubation series using the Duhamel formula.
The assumptions listed below are the same as in
[
13
]
, except that we require the estimates in
(DR2)
,
(DR3)
and
(DR4)
to hold for
\omega^{\lambda}
uniformly in
\lambda
in the weak coupling regime.

Assumption (V)
.

The potential
V:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and non-negative.
Its Fourier transform
\widehat{V}
belongs to
C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
is bounded.

Assumption (L1)

(
\ell_{1}
-clustering)
.

There exists
\lambda_{0}>0
and
c_{0}>0
independent of
n
such that for
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
and all
n\geq 4
, the following estimate holds true

(2.19)

\sum_{x\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{n}}\delta({x_{1}=0})\Bigg\lvert\bigg\langle\prod^{n}_{j=1}a(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}^{T}\Bigg\rvert\leq{}\lambda c_{0}^{n}n!,

where the truncation operator is defined in (
3.28
).
In addition,

(2.20)

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\big\lvert\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\rangle_{\lambda}-\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\rangle_{0}\big\rvert\leq{}\lambda 2c_{0}^{2}.

Assumption (DR1)
.

The periodic extension of
\omega
is real-analytic and
\omega(-k)=\omega(k)
.

Assumption (DR2)

(
\ell_{3}
-dispersivity)
.

Let us consider the free propagator

(2.21)

p_{t}^{\lambda}(x)\coloneq{}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\,e^{i2\pi x\cdot k}e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}(k)}.

There exist
\lambda_{0},\delta>0
such the following inequality holds true for all
0\leq\lambda<\lambda_{0}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(2.22)

\lVert p_{t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{3}^{3}={}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lvert p_{t}^{\lambda}(x)\rvert^{3}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1-\delta}.

Assumption (DR3)

(Constructive interference)
.

There exists a set
M^{\mathrm{sing}}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
consisting of a union of a finite number of closed, one-dimensional, smooth submanifolds, and constants
C,\lambda_{0}>0
, such that for all
0\leq\lambda<\lambda_{0}

t\in\mathbb{R}
,
k_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
,

(2.23)

\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\,e^{-it(\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k-k_{0}))}\bigg\rvert\leq{}\frac{C\langle t\rangle^{-1}}{d(k_{0},M^{\mathrm{sing}})},

where
d(k_{0},M^{\mathrm{sing}})
is the distance from
k_{0}
to
M^{\mathrm{sing}}
.

Assumption (DR4)

(Crossing bounds)
.

Define for
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

(2.24)

\mathcal{K}(x;t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},u_{1},u_{2})\coloneq{}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\,e^{i2\pi x\cdot k}e^{-i(t_{0}\omega^{\lambda}(k)+t_{1}\omega^{\lambda}(k+u_{1})+t_{2}\omega^{\lambda}(k+u_{2}))}.

We assume that there is a measurable function
F^{\mathrm{cr}}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,\infty]
and constants
0<\gamma\leq 1
,
c_{1},c_{2},\lambda_{0}>0
such that the following hold for all
0\leq\lambda<\lambda_{0}
.

(1)

For any
u_{j}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
\sigma_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
,
j=1,2,3
, and
0<\zeta\leq 1
, the following bounds are satisfied:

(2.25)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\lVert p_{t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{3}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{1}s,\sigma_{2}s,u_{1},u_{2})\rVert_{3}\leq{}\zeta^{\gamma-1}F^{\mathrm{cr}}(u_{2}-u_{1},\zeta),

and for any
n\in\{1,2,3\}
,

(2.26)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{j}s,0,u_{j},0)\rVert_{3}\leq{}\zeta^{\gamma-1}F^{\mathrm{cr}}(u_{n},\zeta).

(2)

For all
0<\zeta\leq 1
we have

(2.27)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}kF^{\mathrm{cr}}(k,\zeta)\leq{}c_{1}\langle\ln\zeta\rangle^{c_{2}},

and if also
u,k_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
, and
n\in\{1,2,3\}
, and we denote
k=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})
, then

(2.28)

\iint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}F^{\mathrm{cr}}(k_{n}+u;\zeta)\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\Theta(k,\sigma)+i\zeta\rvert}\leq{}c_{1}\langle\ln\zeta\rangle^{1+c_{2}},

where
\Theta:(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}\times\{\pm 1\}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(2.29)

\Theta(k,\sigma)={}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{3})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1})+\sigma(\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3})).

Note that
\Theta
of course depends on
\lambda
, but we will suppress this dependence from the notation throughout.
We note also that for fermions at thermal equilibrium, the
\ell^{1}
-clustering estimate of
(L1)
can be justified using the work of Salmhofer
[
17
]
.

2.2.
Main result

For any compactly supported
f,g\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
, we denote by
\hat{f}
,
\hat{g}
their Fourier transforms and consider the following testing two-point correlation operator

(2.30)

Q^{\lambda}[g,f](t)\coloneq{}\iint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k^{\prime})\big\langle\hat{a}(k^{\prime},0)^{\ast}e^{i\omega^{\lambda}(k)t/\varepsilon}\hat{a}(k,t/\varepsilon)\big\rangle_{\lambda},

where
\varepsilon=\lambda^{2}
,
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle_{\lambda}
is the Gibbs state
Z^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{T}H_{\lambda}}
, and
\omega^{\lambda}(k)
is the modified dispersion relation (
2.17
).

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that
d\geq 4
and that the assumptions of
Section

2.1
are satisfied.
There exists
t_{0}>0
such that for all
\lvert t\rvert<t_{0}
we have

(2.31)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\bigg\lvert Q^{\lambda}[g,f](t)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)W^{0}(k)e^{-\nu_{1}(k)\lvert t\rvert-it\nu_{2}(k)}\bigg\rvert={}0,

where
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
are real functions defined by a quantity which is proportional to the collisional frequency
\mu
of the Boltzmann-Nordheim operator at equilibrium.
To be precise,
\nu(k)=\nu_{1}(k)+i\nu_{2}(k)
, with

\displaystyle\nu(k_{1})={}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3d}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

\displaystyle\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})\big(\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})-\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{4})\big)e^{it(\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})-\omega(k_{4}))}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\times\big(W^{0}(k_{3})W^{0}(k_{4})-W^{0}(k_{2})W^{0}(k_{4})+W^{0}(k_{2})(1-W^{0}(k_{3}))\big).

As pointed out in
[
12
]
, the real part of the decay coefficient
\nu
satisfies
\mathrm{Re}\,\nu>0
, since a direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\,\nu(k_{1})={}\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3d}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}

\displaystyle\delta(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})\delta(\omega(k_{1})+\omega(k_{2})-\omega(k_{3})-\omega(k_{4}))

\displaystyle\times\big(\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{3})-\widehat{V}(k_{2}-k_{4})\big)^{2}(W^{0}_{1})^{-1}\widetilde{W}^{0}_{2}W^{0}_{3}W^{0}_{4}.

Theorem
2.2
focuses on the short kinetic time. The case of long kinetic time is more challenging and requires new ingredients going beyond the existing techniques in
[
13
]
.

Restriction to times
t>0

We recall the
cell step function

[k]:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\Omega^{\ast}
defined by
[k]_{i}\coloneq\lfloor L(k_{i}\mod 1)\rfloor/L
.
Since
[k]
is periodic, it is also identifiable with a map
\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\Omega^{\ast}
.
Then, for any
F:\Omega^{\ast}\to\mathbb{C}
, we have the following formula relating the discrete sum over
\Omega^{\ast}
to a Lebesgue integral,

(2.33)

\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}F(k)\,\mathrm{d}k=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}F([k])\,\mathrm{d}k,

where
F([k])
is now a piecewise constant step function on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

If the assumptions
(L1)
and
(DR1)
are satisfied, then for all
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
,

(2.34)

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\big\lvert W_{L}^{\lambda}([k])-W^{0}(k)\big\rvert\leq 2c_{0}^{2}\lambda.

Proof.

We have from
Lemma

2.1
that

\frac{1}{e^{\frac{1}{T}\omega(k)}+1}=W^{0}(k)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}\big\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\big\rangle_{0},

so since the left hand side is smooth by our assumptions
(DR1)
on
\omega
, the inverse Fourier transform
x\mapsto\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\rangle_{0}
is in
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
.
We can now estimate

\big\lvert W_{L}^{\lambda}([k])-W^{0}(k)\big\rvert\leq{}\big\lvert W^{0}([k])-W^{0}(k)\big\rvert+\big\lvert W_{L}^{\lambda}([k])-W^{0}([k])\big\rvert,

where the first term converges to zero uniformly on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
as
\Omega
grows, and the second term is bounded by

\big\lvert W_{L}^{\lambda}([k])-W^{0}([k])\big\rvert\leq{}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\Omega}\big\lvert\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\rangle_{0}\big\rvert+\sum\limits_{x\in\Omega}\big\lvert\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\rangle_{\lambda}-\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x)\rangle_{0}\big\rvert.

Here, the first term tends to zero as
L\to\infty
, and the second term is bounded by
\lambda 2c_{0}^{2}
by
(L1)
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Theorem

2.2
is true for
t=0
.
Furthermore, assuming that the theorem holds for
0<t<t_{0}
, then it also holds for
-t_{0}<t<0
.

Proof.

For
t=0
, we have by
Lemma

2.1
,

Q^{\lambda}[g,f](0)={}\iint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k^{\prime})\big\langle\hat{a}(k^{\prime})^{\ast}\hat{a}(k)\big\rangle_{\lambda}={}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)W_{L}^{\lambda}(k),

so using
Lemma

2.3
, we can replace
W_{L}^{\lambda}
by
W^{0}
and immediately obtain (
2.31
) at the initial time.

Now, suppose that the theorem is true for
0<t<t_{0}
, and denote

F(x,t)\coloneq{}\big\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x,t)\big\rangle_{\lambda}.

Since the time evolution commutes with spatial translation, we have by translation invariance that

\big\langle a(y)^{\ast}a(x,t)\big\rangle_{\lambda}={}F(x-y,t),

so repeating calculation in (
2.10
), we obtain

\big\langle\hat{a}(k)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k^{\prime},t)\big\rangle_{\lambda}={}\delta_{L}(k-k^{\prime})\widehat{F}(k,t).

This means that we can write

(2.35)

Q^{\lambda}[f,g](t)={}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\,\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)e^{i\omega^{\lambda}(k)t/\varepsilon}\widehat{F}(k,t/\varepsilon).

Noting that

F(-x,-t)^{\ast}={}\big\langle a(-x,-t)^{\ast}a(0)\big\rangle_{\lambda}={}\big\langle a(0,-t)^{\ast}a(x)\big\rangle_{\lambda}={}\big\langle a(0)^{\ast}a(x,t)\big\rangle_{\lambda}={}F(x,t),

which implies

\widehat{F}(k,-t)={}\sum\limits_{x\in\Omega}e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}F(-x,-t)={}\widehat{F}(k,t)^{\ast},

we conclude from (
2.35
) that

Q^{\lambda}[f,g](-t)={}\big(Q^{\lambda}[g,f](t)\big)^{\ast}.

It then follows easily for
0<t<t_{0}
that we can replace
t
by
-t
in (
2.31
).
∎

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In
Section

3
we develop the iterated Duhamel expansion and the corresponding diagrammatic representation.
In
Section

4
we review the momentum graph formalism and the classification of graphs needed in the proof.
In
Section

5
we introduce the key parameters and auxiliary estimates.
The graph bounds are established in
Section

6
.
Finally, in
Section

7
we combine these ingredients to prove the main theorem.

3.
Feynman diagrams

3.1.
Feynman diagrams for Duhamel expansions

3.1.1.
Duhamel expansions

We rewrite (
2.7
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{a}(k_{1},t)={}

\displaystyle-i\omega(k_{1})\hat{a}(k_{1},t)-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

(3.1)

\displaystyle\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t).

We will continue to expand (
3.1.1
) in several layers of Duhamel expansions.
This strategy leads to a technical difficulty of treating the wave number near the singular manifold
M^{\mathrm{sing}}
.
As a result, we introduce the following proposition about the existence of cut-off functions, whose construction is detailed in
[
13
, Section
7.1
]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
b>0
.
There are constants
C>0,\lambda_{0}^{\prime}>0
such that for any
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}
,
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
and for any pair of indices
i\neq j
,
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
, there exist smooth cut-off functions
\Phi_{0}:(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}\to[0,1]
and
\Phi_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=1-\Phi_{0}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),
such that

(1)

If
k_{i}+k_{j}=0
then
\Phi_{1}=0
and
\Phi_{0}=1
.

(2)

0\leq\Phi_{1}(k)\leq C\lambda^{-b}d(k_{i}+k_{j},M^{\mathrm{sing}})
.

(3)

\Phi_{1}(k_{3},k_{2},k_{1})=\Phi_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
,
\Phi_{0}(k_{3},k_{2},k_{1})=\Phi_{0}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
.

(4)

(3.2)

0\leq\Phi_{0}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}{\mathds{1}}\big(d(k_{i}+k_{j},M^{\mathrm{sing}})<\lambda^{b}\big).

(5)

There exist smooth functions
F_{1},F_{0}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
F_{0}=1-F_{1}
and
\Phi_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=F_{1}(k_{1}+k_{2})F_{1}(k_{2}+k_{3})F_{1}(k_{3}+k_{1})
.
Moreover, there exist constants
C_{F},C_{F}^{\prime}>0
such that for any
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
,

(v.1)

0\leq F_{1}(k),F_{0}(k)\leq 1
.

(v.2)

\lvert\nabla F_{1}(k)\rvert
,
\lvert\nabla F_{0}(k)\rvert\leq C_{F}\lambda^{-b}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

(v.3)

If
k\in M^{\mathrm{sing}}
, then
F_{1}(k)=0
and
F_{0}(k)=1
.

(v.4)

If
d(k,M^{\mathrm{sing}})\geq\lambda^{b}
, then
F_{1}(k)=1
and
F_{0}(k)=0
.

(v.5)

If
0\leq F_{1}(k)\leq C_{F}\lambda^{-b}d(k,M^{\mathrm{sing}})
.

(v.6)

(3.3)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}kF_{0}(k)\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k{\mathds{1}}\big(d(k,M^{\mathrm{sing}})<\lambda^{b}\big)\leq C^{\prime}_{F}\lambda^{b(d-1)}.

Of course, the functions
\Phi_{0}
,
\Phi_{1}
,
F_{0}
,
F_{1}
all depend on
\lambda
, but we will suppress this from the notation throughout.
Plugging the expression
1=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
into (
3.1.1
), we find

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{a}(k_{1},t)={}

\displaystyle-i\omega(k_{1})\hat{a}(k_{1},t)-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})\Phi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)

\displaystyle-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

(3.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})\Phi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t).

We now define a truncated product
[\hat{a}^{\ast}\hat{a}\hat{a}]^{T}
by writing

\displaystyle\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)={}

\displaystyle\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\langle\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\rangle_{\lambda}-\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\langle\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\rangle_{\lambda}

(3.5)

\displaystyle+[\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)]^{T}.

Inserting (
3.1.1
) into the term of (
3.1.1
) containing
\Psi_{0}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{a}(k_{1},t)={}

\displaystyle-i\omega(k_{1})\hat{a}(k_{1},t)-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \times\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})\Phi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})[&\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\delta_{L}(k_{2}+k_{3})W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{3})\\
&-\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\delta_{L}(k_{2}+k_{4})W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{4})]\end{aligned}

\displaystyle-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \times\big[&\Phi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\\
&+\Phi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})[\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)]^{T}\big],\end{aligned}

which, after integrating out the delta functions and taking into account the properties of the cut-off function
\Phi_{0}
, leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{a}(k_{1},t)={}

\displaystyle-i\omega(k_{1})\hat{a}(k_{1},t)-i\lambda\hat{a}(k_{1},t)\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{2})[\widehat{V}(0)-\widehat{V}(k_{1}-k_{2})]

\displaystyle-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})

(3.6)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \times\big[&\Phi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\\
&+\Phi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})[\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)\hat{a}(k_{4},t)]^{T}\big],\end{aligned}

At this point, we recall the definition of the modified dispersion relation (
2.17
), (
2.18
), whose role is to absorb the terms containing
\hat{a}(k_{1},t)
in (
3.1.1
).
In order to do that, we introduce the following new presentation of the field operators
\hat{a}
,

(3.7)

b(k,1,t)\coloneq e^{i\omega^{\lambda}(k)t}\hat{a}(k,t),\qquad b(k,-1,t)\coloneq e^{-i\omega^{\lambda}(k)t}\hat{a}(-k,t)^{\ast}.

A new equation for
b
can then be derived from (
3.1.1
),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}b(k_{1},1,t)={}

\displaystyle-i\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})

\displaystyle\times\exp{\big[-it(-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})+\omega^{\lambda}(k_{3})+\omega^{\lambda}(k_{4}))\big]}

\displaystyle\times\big[\Phi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})b(k_{2},-1,t)b(k_{3},1,t)b(k_{4},1,t)

(3.8)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Phi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})[b(k_{2},-1,t)b(k_{3},1,t)b(k_{4},1,t)]^{T}\big].

Note that this step relies on the stationarity of the Gibbs state
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle_{\lambda}
.
For general initial states,
\omega^{\lambda}
will depend also on
t
through
W_{L}^{\lambda}
, which would make absorbing the first two terms in (
3.1.1
) more complicated.

A similar computation can also be done for
b(k_{1},-1,t)
, starting from

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{a}(k_{1},t)^{\ast}={}i\omega(k_{1})\hat{a}(k_{1},t)^{\ast}+i\lambda

\displaystyle\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4})

\displaystyle\times\widehat{V}(k_{3}+k_{4})\hat{a}(-k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(-k_{3},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{4},t),

and using, similarly to (
3.1.1
),

\displaystyle\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{4},t)={}

\displaystyle\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\langle\hat{a}(k_{3},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\rangle_{\lambda}-\hat{a}(k_{3},t)^{\ast}\langle\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{4},t)\rangle_{\lambda}

(3.9)

\displaystyle+[\hat{a}(k_{2},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{3},t)^{\ast}\hat{a}(k_{4},t)]^{T}.

The following general equation for
b(k_{1},\sigma,t)
can then be derived,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}b(k_{1},\sigma,t)={}

\displaystyle-i\sigma\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{2}\big[(1+\sigma)\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})+(1-\sigma)\widehat{V}(k_{3}+k_{4})\big]

\displaystyle\times\exp{\big[-it(-\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{3})+\omega^{\lambda}(k_{4}))\big]}

\displaystyle\times\big[\Phi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})b(k_{2},-1,t)b(k_{3},\sigma,t)b(k_{4},1,t)

(3.10)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Phi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})[b(k_{2},-1,t)b(k_{3},\sigma,t)b(k_{4},1,t)]^{T}\big].

To simplify the expression, we denote

\displaystyle\Theta(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\omega^{\lambda}(k_{4})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})+\sigma(\omega^{\lambda}(k_{3})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4})),

(3.11)

\displaystyle U(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big[(1+\sigma)\widehat{V}(k_{2}+k_{3})+(1-\sigma)\widehat{V}(k_{3}+k_{4})\big],

\displaystyle\Psi_{j}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\Phi_{j}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})U(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma),\qquad j=1,2,

where
\Theta
is the same as in (
2.29
).
Plugging these expressions into (
3.10
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}b(k_{1},\sigma,t)={}-i\sigma\lambda\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\mathrm{d}k_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})

\displaystyle\times\exp{\big[-it\Theta(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)\big]}\big[\Psi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)b(k_{2},-1,t)b(k_{3},\sigma,t)b(k_{4},1,t)

(3.12)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Psi_{0}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)[b(k_{2},-1,t)b(k_{3},\sigma,t)b(k_{4},1,t)]^{T}\big].

We want to use this to obtain an expression for general products of
b
operators.
For this, we recall the product rule

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\prod_{j=1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)={}\sum_{m=1}^{n}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)\bigg)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}b(k_{m},\sigma_{m},t)\bigg(\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)\bigg),

where we use the general convention that the operators are ordered from left to right according to the order of the index in the
\prod
-symbol, i.e., the product on the left hand side above is by definition
b(k_{1},\sigma_{1},t)\cdots b(k_{n},\sigma_{n},t)
.
Introducing a new parameter
\kappa>0
which will be specified later, plugging (
3.12
) into the expression above leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big[e^{\kappa t}\prod_{j=1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)\Big]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\kappa e^{\kappa t}\prod_{j=1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)-i\lambda\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sigma_{m}\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}_{2}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}_{3}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{m}-k_{2}^{\prime}-k_{3}^{\prime}-k_{4}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\exp{\big[\kappa t-it\Theta(k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime},k_{4}^{\prime},\sigma_{m})\big]}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)\big[\Psi_{1}(k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},\sigma)b(k_{2}^{\prime},-1,t)b(k_{3}^{\prime},\sigma_{m},t)b(k_{4}^{\prime},1,t)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\Psi_{0}(k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime},k_{4}^{\prime},\sigma_{m})[b(k_{2}^{\prime},-1,t)b(k_{3}^{\prime},\sigma_{m},t)b(k_{4}^{\prime},1,t)]^{T}\big]\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t).

Integrating both sides of the above equation yields

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},t)={}e^{-\kappa t}\prod_{j=1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},0)+\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}se^{-(t-s)\kappa}\prod_{j=1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},s)

\displaystyle-i\lambda\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sigma_{m}\iiint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}_{2}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}_{3}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}_{4}\delta_{L}(k_{m}-k_{2}^{\prime}-k_{3}^{\prime}-k_{4}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp{\big[-(t-s)\kappa-is\Theta(k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime},k_{4}^{\prime},\sigma_{m})\big]}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},s)\big[\Psi_{1}(k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime},k_{4}^{\prime},\sigma_{m})b(k_{2}^{\prime},-1,s)b(k_{3}^{\prime},\sigma_{m},s)b(k_{4}^{\prime},1,s)

(3.14)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\Psi_{0}(k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime},k_{4}^{\prime},\sigma_{m})[b(k_{2}^{\prime},-1,s)b(k_{3}^{\prime},\sigma_{m},s)b(k_{4}^{\prime},1,s)]^{T}\big]\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},s),

which provides the basis for the full pertubation expansion of
b(k,\sigma,t)
that we will use to treat the correlation operator (
2.30
).

Observe that the term in (
3.14
) associated to
\Psi_{1}
contains a product of
n+2
field operators
b
.
The idea is then to iteratively plug (
3.14
) back into itself in the term containing
\Psi_{1}
, using different values of
\kappa
at each step.
Starting with
n=1
, we label the terms in (
3.14
) as

\displaystyle b(k,\sigma,t)={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{0}(t,k,\sigma,\kappa_{0})[b(0)]+\kappa_{0}\int_{0}^{t}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathcal{E}_{0}^{1}(s,t,k,\sigma,\kappa_{0})[b(s)]

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathcal{E}_{1}^{2}(s,t,k,\sigma,\kappa_{0})[b(s)]+\int_{0}^{t}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathcal{E}_{1}^{3}(s,t,k,\sigma,\kappa_{0})[b(s)],

where
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{0}
is the first term in (
3.14
),
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{1}
comes from the second term,
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{2}
is the term containing
\Psi_{0}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{3}
is the term with
\Psi_{1}
.
The subscripts appearing on the
\mathcal{E}
’s will indicate that any term in
\mathcal{E}_{n}
contains
2n+1
factors of
b
.
Because of the integral over
s
, it is convenient to imagine that we split the time interval
[0,t]
into two slices of length
s_{0}=s
and
s_{1}=t-s
.
Denoting
\sigma_{1}=-1
,
\sigma_{2}=\sigma
, and
\sigma_{3}=1
, we can write for instance

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}^{3}(s,t,k,\sigma,\kappa_{0})[b(s_{0})]={}-i\lambda\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\delta_{L}(k-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})

\displaystyle\times\sigma\Psi_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\sigma)\prod\limits_{j=1}^{3}b(k_{j},\sigma_{j},s_{0})\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\delta(t-s_{0}-s_{1})e^{-s_{1}\kappa_{0}-is_{0}\Theta(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\sigma)},

and similarly for
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{2}
.
Then, plugging (
3.14
) into
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{3}
replaces
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{3}
with a sum of four new terms
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{0}
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{2}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{3}
.

Repeating this process
N
times, we obtain an
N
-time Duhamel generalization of (
3.14
).
In this expansion, the time interval
[0,t]
is divided into
N+1
time slices
[0,s_{0}]
,
[s_{0},s_{0}+s_{1}]
,
\dots
,
[s_{0}+\cdots+s_{N-1},t]
and
t=s_{0}+\cdots+s_{N}
.
In order to better represent these Duhamel expansions, we will construct their presentations in terms of Feynman diagrams in the next subsection.

3.1.2.
Feynman diagrams

As discussed in the previous subsection, the time interval
[0,t]
is divided into
N+1
time slices, each of length
s_{i}
.
We define the sets of indices

(3.15)

I_{N}=\{1,\dotsc,N\}\mbox{ and }I_{N^{\prime},N}=\{N^{\prime},N^{\prime}+1,\dotsc,N\}.

We now how to construct the Feynman diagrams corresponding to the expansion.
The time slices are represented from the bottom to the top of the diagram, with the lengths,
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
,
\dots
,
s_{N}
, as shown in
Fig.

1
.

Figure 1.
An example of a Feynman diagram. At time slice
s_{0}
, the edges are
k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,3},k_{0,4},k_{0,5}
, with the parities
-,-,+,-,+
. At time slice
s_{1}
, the edges are
k_{1,1},k_{1,2},k_{1,3}
, with the parities
-,-,+
. At time slice
s_{2}
, the edge is
k_{2,1}
, with parity
-
.

Indexing the segments

At time slice
s_{j}
, since we expand the term containing
\Psi_{1}
, the delta function associated to
\Psi_{1}
in (
3.14
) means that the three momenta
k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}
are combined into the momentum
k_{1}
.
This is represented on the diagram by the fact that at time slice
s_{j}
, there is exactly one triplet of the segments of time slice
s_{j-1}
fuses into one segment of time slice
s_{j}
.
Those segments of time slice
s_{j-1}
are denoted by
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}},k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1},k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}
and the one at time slice
s_{j}
is denoted by
k_{j,\ell_{j}}
.
By the delta function associated to
\Psi_{1}
in (
3.12
), we have
k_{j,\ell_{j}}=k_{j-1,\ell_{j}}+k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1}+k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}
.
In these notations, the
j
is the index of time slice
s_{j}
and
\ell_{j}
is the index of the segment where the fusion happens i.e. where we plug (
3.14
) into itself at time slice
s_{j}
.
As we notice from (
3.14
) there is a sign parameter
\sigma
associated to each momentum
k
.
We call those parameters
parities
.
We then denote these parameters for
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}},k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1},k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}
, and
k_{j,\ell_{j}}
by
\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1},\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}
, and
\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}
.
Now, at the very first time slice
N
, whose length is
s_{N}
, there is only one segment
k_{N,1}
being decomposed into
k_{N-1,1},k_{N-1,2},k_{N-1,3}
.
In this case,
\ell_{N}=1
.
We denote the number of segments at this time slice by
m_{0}=1
.
However, at the second time slice
s_{N-1}
,
\ell_{N-1}
can be either
1,2
, or
3
.
Each choice of
\ell_{N-1}
leads to a different Feynman diagram.
The number of segments at this time slice is
m_{1}=m_{0}+2=3
.
By induction, the number of segments at time slice
s_{j}
is
m_{N-j}=1+2(N-j)
.
In this time slice
s_{j}
, the fusion only happens at the segment
k_{j,\ell_{j}}
, where the three segments
\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1},\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}
are combined.
This leads us to the following way of indexing the segments in two consecutive time slices
s_{j-1}
and
s_{j}
:
k_{j,l}=k_{j-1,l},\sigma_{j,l}=\sigma_{j-1,l},
for
l\in\{1,\cdots,\ell_{j}-1\}
and
k_{j,l}=k_{j-1,l+2},\sigma_{j,l}=\sigma_{j-1,l+2},
for
l\in\{\ell_{j}+1,\cdots,m_{N-i}\}
, with the notice that
m_{N-i}=m_{N-i+1}-2
.
Looking closer into equation (
3.14
), we can see that the parities associated to
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}
are
\sigma,-1,\sigma,1
in the term containing
\Psi_{1}
.
This essentially means that
\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}=\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1}
,
\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}}=-1
and
\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}=1
.
We now denote
\ell=(\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{N})
, which is a vector in the space

(3.16)

G_{N}\coloneq{}I_{m_{N-1}}\times I_{m_{N-2}}\times\cdots\times I_{m_{0}}.

We also need the notation

(3.17)

\mathcal{I}_{n;m_{0}}\coloneq{}\{(j,l)\mid 0\leq j\leq n-1,1\leq l\leq m_{0}+2(n-j)\},\mbox{ and }\mathcal{I}_{n}\coloneq{}\mathcal{I}_{n;1},

which collects all index pairs associated to the above segments, excluding the final time slice.

Graphically, we place an
interaction vertex
in the diagram at the places where three segments fuse into one.
We also place an
initial time vertex
at the bottom of each of the
2N+1
edges at the bottom of the diagram (at time
t=0
).
The parities
\sigma_{j,l}
are represented on the diagram by adding an arrow on the corresponding edge.
An upward pointing arrow represents the parity
\sigma=1
, while a downward pointing arrow represents
\sigma=-1
.
See
Fig.

1
for a simple example of a Feynman diagram.

With all of these definitions in hand, we are able to write down the full Duhamel expansion, up to
N
time slices.

The full Duhamel expansion

Plugging (
3.14
) back into itself at the terms containing
\Psi_{1}
, we find by induction that

\displaystyle b(k,\sigma,t)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathcal{E}_{n}^{0}(t,k,\sigma,\kappa)[b(0)]+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\kappa_{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{0}\mathcal{E}_{n}^{1}(s_{0},t,k,\sigma,\kappa)[b(s_{0})]

(3.18)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{0}\mathcal{E}_{n}^{2}(s_{0},t,k,\sigma,\kappa)[b(s_{0})]+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{0}\mathcal{E}_{N}^{3}(s_{0},t,k,\sigma,\kappa)[b(s_{0})].

The terms in the formulation (
3.18
) are explicitly written as follows.

(i)
\kappa
denotes the vector
\kappa=(\kappa_{0},\cdots,\kappa_{N-1})
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N}
.

(ii) The first quantity
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{0}(t,k,\sigma,\kappa)
in (
3.18
) takes the form (for
n\geq 1
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}^{0}(t,k_{n,1},\sigma_{n,1},\kappa)[b]={}

\displaystyle(-i\lambda)^{n}\sum_{\ell\in G_{n},\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\prod_{j=1}^{1+2n}b(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]

(3.19)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{n}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{n-j}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-it_{j}(s)\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})},

in which
t_{j}(s)=\sum_{l=0}^{j-1}s_{l}
is the total time before time slice
s_{j}
;
k_{j:l}
denotes the triplet
(k_{j,l},k_{j,l+1},k_{j,l+2})
;
\bar{k}
and
\bar{\sigma}
represent all of the quantities
k_{j,l},\sigma_{j,l}
appearing in the integration; and
\Theta_{j:l}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})
is the function
\Theta(k_{j:l},\sigma_{j+1,l})
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{0}(t,k,\sigma,\kappa)[b]=e^{-\kappa_{0}t}b(k,\sigma)
.

(iii) The function
\Delta_{n,\ell}
contains all of the
\delta
-functions appearing up to the
n
th time slice.
To be precise,

\displaystyle\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})={}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg\{

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{\ell_{j}-1}\Big[\delta_{L}(k_{j,l}-k_{j-1,l}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{j,l}=\sigma_{j-1,l})\Big]

\displaystyle\times\delta_{L}\big(k_{j,\ell_{j}}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}\big)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=\ell_{j}+1}^{m_{n-j}}\Big[\delta_{L}(k_{j,l}-k_{j-1,l+2}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{j,l}=\sigma_{j-1,l+2})\Big]

(3.20)

\displaystyle\times{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}}=-1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1}=\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j+2}}=1)\bigg\}.

This function makes sure that
k_{j,l}=k_{j-1,l},\sigma_{j,l}=\sigma_{j-1,l}
, for
l\in\{1,\cdots,\ell_{j}-1\}
;
k_{j,l}=k_{j-1,l+2},\sigma_{j,l}=\sigma_{j-1,l+2}
, for
l\in\{\ell_{j}+1,\cdots,m_{N-j}\}
;
\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}=\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1}
;
\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}}=-1
;
\sigma_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}=1
; and
k_{j,\ell_{j}}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+1}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+2}=0
, as discussed in the previous subsection.

(iv) The last quantity
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{3}
can now be defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{3}_{n}(s_{0},t,k_{n,1},\sigma_{n,1},\kappa)[b(s_{0})]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i\lambda)^{n}\sum_{\ell\in G_{n},\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]

(3.21)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{1+2n}b(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{n-j}-it_{j}(s_{0},s)\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}.

The only difference between the two formulas (
3.19
) and (
3.21
) is the integration with respect to
\mathrm{d}s
.
In (
3.19
) the integration is taken over
(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n}}
and in (
3.21
) it is over
(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{n}}
, and the factor
e^{-s_{0}\kappa_{n}}
does not appear in (
3.21
).

(v) The second quantity
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{1}
is defined as follows.
We set
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{0}(s_{0},t,k,\sigma,\kappa)[b(s_{0})]=e^{-(t-s_{0})\kappa_{0}}b(k,\sigma,s_{0})
, and for
n>0
, put

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{1}_{n}(s_{0},t,k_{n,1},\sigma_{n,1},\kappa)[b(s_{0})]={}\int_{0}^{t-s_{0}}\mathrm{d}re^{-r\kappa_{n}}\mathcal{E}^{3}_{n}(s_{0}+r,t,k_{n,1},\sigma_{n,1},\kappa)[b(s_{0})]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i\lambda)^{n}\int_{0}^{t-s_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}r\sum_{\ell\in G_{n},\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})

\displaystyle\times e^{-r\kappa_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{1+2n}b(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-r-\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{n-j}-it_{j}(s_{0}+r,s)\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}.

(vi) And, finally,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{2}_{n}(s_{0},t,k_{n,1},\sigma_{n,1},\kappa)[b(s_{0})]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i\lambda)^{n}\sum_{\ell\in G_{n},\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}}\Psi_{0}(k_{0:\ell_{1}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=2}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{1}-1}b(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\prod_{l=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{1}+2}b(k_{0,l},\sigma_{0,l},s_{0})\Bigg]^{T}\prod_{j=\ell_{1}+3}^{1+2n}b(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})

(3.23)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{n-j}-it_{j}(s_{0},s)\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})},

where
\bar{k}
and
\bar{\sigma}
represent all of the quantities
k_{j,l},\sigma_{j,l}
appearing in the integration.

3.1.3.
Expansions of
Q^{\lambda}[g,f]
.

Let us now consider the operator from (
2.30
),

(3.24)

Q^{\lambda}[g,f][t]=\iint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(-k^{\prime})\big\langle b(k^{\prime},-1,0)b(k,1,t/\varepsilon)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\mbox{ with }\varepsilon=\lambda^{2}.

Plugging (
3.18
) into (
3.24
), we find

(3.25)

Q^{\lambda}[g,f](t)=Q^{main}+Q^{err}_{1}+Q^{err}_{2}+Q^{err}_{3},

where

(3.26)

Q^{main}=\iint_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(-k^{\prime})\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\big\langle b(k^{\prime},-1,0)\mathcal{E}_{n}^{0}(t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(0)]\big\rangle_{\lambda},

and

(3.27)

\displaystyle Q^{err}_{1}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\kappa_{n}\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\Big\langle\langle\hat{f},b(0)\rangle^{\ast}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{1}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rangle_{\lambda},

\displaystyle Q^{err}_{2}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\Big\langle\langle\hat{f},b(0)\rangle^{\ast}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{2}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rangle_{\lambda},

\displaystyle Q^{err}_{3}={}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\Big\langle\langle\hat{f},b(0)\rangle^{\ast}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{3}_{N}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rangle_{\lambda}.

To study in details the operators
Q^{main},Q^{err}_{1},Q^{err}_{2},Q^{err}_{3}
, we will need the following definition of truncated moments, which is a generalization of (
3.1.1
) and (
3.9
).

Definition
.

For any finite, non-empty, ordered set
I
, we denote by
\pi(I)
the set of its ordered partitions:
S\in\pi(I)
if and only if
S\subset\mathcal{P}(I)
such that each
A\in S
is ordered (carrying the order from
I
) and non-empty,
\cup_{A\in S}A=I
and if
A,A^{\prime}\in S
,
A\neq A
then
A^{\prime}\cap A=\emptyset
. We also assume that if
A\in S
, then
A
has an even number of elements. Moreover,
\pi(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.

For any state
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle
, consider the moment

\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}a(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle.

This quantity is
0
if
n
is odd.
We define its truncation in the recursive manner

(3.28)

\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}a(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle=\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{n})}\epsilon(S)\prod_{A=\{J_{1},\dots,J_{\lvert A\rvert}\}\in S}\bigg\langle\prod_{l=1}^{\lvert A\rvert}a(x_{J_{l}},\sigma_{J_{l}})\bigg\rangle^{T},

where
\epsilon(S)
is the sign of the permutation corresponding to
S
, which is defined as follows:
Labelling the elements of
S=\{A_{1},\dotsc,A_{k}\}
,
\epsilon(S)
is the sign of the permutation that maps the ordered set
I_{n}
to the ordered set
\{A_{1},\dotsc,A_{k}\}
.
Since each
A_{j}
is assumed to have an even number of elements,
\epsilon(S)
is independent of the labeling of the elements of
S
(see
Lemma

3.10
below).

We also define the truncated correlation function

(3.29)

{C}_{n}(k,\sigma,\lambda,\Omega)\coloneq{}\sum_{x\in\Omega^{n}}{\mathds{1}}_{\{x_{1}=0\}}e^{-i2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\cdot k_{j}}\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}a(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle^{T}.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that the state
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle
is translation invariant.
Then, for any ordered index set
I
, and any
k\in(\Omega^{\ast})^{I}
,
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{I}
, we have

(3.30)

\bigg\langle\prod_{j\in I}\hat{a}(k_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle=\sum_{S\in\pi(I)}\epsilon(S)\prod_{A=\{J_{1},\dots,J_{\lvert A\rvert}\}\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{{\lvert A\rvert}}k_{J_{l}}\bigg){C}_{\lvert A\rvert}(k_{A},\sigma_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg].

Proof.

We need to study

\bigg\langle\prod_{j\in I}\hat{a}(k_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle=\sum_{x\in\Omega^{I}}e^{-2\pi i\sum_{j}x_{j}\cdot k_{j}}\bigg\langle\prod_{j\in I}a(x_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle.

By inserting (
3.28
) into the above equation and rearranging the terms, we find

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\prod_{j\in I}\hat{a}(k_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rangle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Omega^{I}}e^{-2\pi i\sum_{j}x_{j}\cdot k_{j}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I)}\epsilon(S)\prod_{A\in S}\bigg\langle\prod_{l=1}^{{\lvert A\rvert}}a(x_{J_{l}},\sigma_{J_{l}})\bigg\rangle^{T}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\pi(I)}\epsilon(S)\prod_{A\in S}\Bigg[\sum_{x\in\Omega^{\lvert A\rvert}}e^{-2\pi i\sum_{l}x_{J_{l}}\cdot k_{J_{l}}}\bigg\langle\prod_{l=1}^{\lvert A\rvert}a(x_{J_{l}},\sigma_{J_{l}})\bigg\rangle^{T}\Bigg].

(
3.30
) then follows by the translation invariance and (
3.29
).
∎

Remark 3.3

(Truncated pair correlations)
.

Suppose that
n=2
and consider the Gibbs state
\langle{}\cdot{}\rangle_{\lambda}
.
Since truncating a moment with just two factors is a trivial operation, we have by definition (
3.29
) and
Lemma

2.1
that

\displaystyle C_{2}((k_{1},k_{2}),(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}),\lambda,\Omega)={}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{x_{2}\in\Omega}e^{-2\pi ix_{2}k_{2}}\big\langle a(0,\sigma_{1})a(x_{2},\sigma_{2})\big\rangle_{\lambda}

(3.31)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{2},\sigma_{1}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{1}=-\sigma_{2}),

where we used the notation from (
2.11
).

Remark 3.4

(Permutations for pairing clusters)
.

Consider a cluster decomposition
S=\{A_{1},\dotsc,A_{k}\}
where all the clusters form pairs,
\lvert A_{j}\rvert=2
.
Denoting
A_{j}=\{a_{j,1},a_{j,2}\}
and ordering the
A_{j}
’s such that
a_{j,1}<a_{k,1}
when
j<k
, it is apparent that the permutation corresponding to
S
belongs to the set of pairings

\displaystyle P_{2k}\coloneq{}\big\{\tau\in S_{2k}\mid\,

\displaystyle\tau(2j-1)<\tau(2j)\text{ for }j=1,\dotsc,k,

\displaystyle\tau(2j-1)<\tau(2j+1)\text{ for }j=1,\dotsc,k-1\big\},

which is reminiscent of Wick’s theorem, see e.g.
[
18
, Theorem 10.2]
.

A straightforward application of
Lemma

3.2
above leads to.

Proposition 3.5

(Main term)
.

For any
N\geq 1
, we have

(3.32)

Q^{main}[g,f](t)={}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{\ell\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{0,2n+1})}\mathcal{F}^{main}_{n}(S,\ell,t/\varepsilon,\kappa)

where, defining
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\cup\{(n,1)\}\cup\{(0,0)\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{main}_{n}(S,\ell,t/\varepsilon,\kappa)=(-i\lambda)^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum_{\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{A\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j\in A}k_{0,j}\bigg)C_{\lvert A\rvert}(k_{0,A},\sigma_{0,A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg]

\displaystyle\times{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{0,0}=-1)\hat{g}(k_{n,1})^{\ast}\hat{f}(k_{n,1})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]

(3.33)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}-\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{n}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{n-j}}\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}e^{-is_{j}\sum_{l=j+1}^{n}\Theta_{l-1:\ell_{l}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}.

Let us consider the error terms
Q^{err}_{1}
,
Q^{err}_{2}
and
Q^{err}_{3}
, that would lead to sums over terms of the type:

(3.34)

\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\Big\langle\langle\hat{f},b(0)\rangle^{\ast}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{j}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rangle_{\lambda},

which can be bounded, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, as

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\Big\langle\langle\hat{f},b(0)\rangle^{\ast}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{j}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rangle_{\lambda}\bigg\rvert^{2}

(3.35)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{f}(k)b(0)\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{j}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda},

where the absolute value should be understood in the sense of operators,
\lvert A\rvert^{2}=A^{\ast}A
.
Notice that the first quantity can be expressed as

\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{f}(k)b(0)\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}=\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\lvert\hat{f}(k)\rvert^{2}W_{L}^{\lambda}(k),

which is uniformly bounded.
As a result, instead of
Q^{err}_{1}
,
Q^{err}_{2}
and
Q^{err}_{3}
, we need to estimate

(3.36)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}^{err}_{1}\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\kappa_{n}\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{1}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{2}^{err}\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{2}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{3}^{err}\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t/\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}s\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{3}_{N}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}.

To proceed further, we will need the following concept of interlacing two time sequences.

Definition
.

Let
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Let
J
be a map from
I_{n+n^{\prime}}
to
\{\pm 1\}
.
Consider the inverse images
J^{-1}(1)
and
J^{-1}(-1)
.
If
J^{-1}(1)
and
J^{-1}(-1)
has
n
and
n^{\prime}
elements, respectively, then
J
is said to
interlace

(n,n^{\prime})
.
We then define further two maps

(3.37)

J_{+}(j,J)\coloneq\sum_{l=1}^{j}{\mathds{1}}(J(l)=1),\qquad J_{-}(j,J)\coloneq\sum_{l=1}^{j}{\mathds{1}}(J(l)=-1).

We also define
\gamma(j;J)
as follows

\displaystyle\gamma(j;J)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{2(n-m)+1}\sigma_{m,l}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{m,l})+\sum_{l=1}^{2(n-m^{\prime})+1}\sigma_{m^{\prime},l}^{\prime}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{m^{\prime},l}^{\prime})

(3.38)

\displaystyle-i(\kappa_{n-m}+\kappa_{n-m^{\prime}}),

with
m=m(j)=J_{+}(j-2,J)+1
and
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}(j)=J_{-}(j-2,J)+1
.

We then have the following Lemma, that allows us to interlace the Duhamel expansions.
The proof of this lemma can be found in
[
13
, Lemma 4.6]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
t>0
,
n,n^{\prime}\geq 0
, and suppose that
\gamma^{+}_{l},\gamma_{l^{\prime}}^{-}\in\mathbb{C}
are given for
l\in I_{0,n}
,
l^{\prime}\in I_{0,n^{\prime}}
.
It holds true that

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{l=0}^{n}s_{l}\bigg)\prod_{l=0}^{n}e^{-is_{l}\gamma^{+}_{l}}\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n^{\prime}}}}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{l=0}^{n^{\prime}}s_{l}^{\prime}\bigg)\prod_{l=0}^{n^{\prime}}e^{-is_{l}^{\prime}\gamma^{-}_{l}}

(3.39)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{J\mbox{ interlaces }(n,n^{\prime})}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n+n^{\prime}}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j=0}^{n+n^{\prime}}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{n+n^{\prime}}e^{-ir_{j}(\gamma^{+}_{J_{+}(j;J)}+\gamma^{-}_{J_{-}(j;J)})}.

A straightforward application of Lemma
3.6
leads to the following representations.

Proposition 3.7
.

The components of
\bar{Q}^{err}_{3}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{3}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}

(3.40)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{J\mbox{ interlaces }(n-1,n-1)}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\mathcal{F}^{3}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},t/\varepsilon-s,\kappa),

where, denoting
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\cup\{(n,1)\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{3}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},\tau,\kappa)={}

\displaystyle(-\lambda^{2})^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\lvert\hat{g}(k_{n,1})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n,\ell}(k,\sigma)\Delta_{n,\ell^{\prime}}(k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{A\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j\in A}K_{j}\bigg)C_{\lvert A\rvert}(K_{A},o_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\sigma_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}}^{\prime}\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell^{\prime}_{j}},-\sigma^{\prime}_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}})\big]

(3.41)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{2,2n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(\tau-\sum_{j=2}^{2n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=2}^{2n}e^{-is_{j}\gamma(j;J)},

where
K=(k_{0,{}\cdot{}}^{\prime},k_{0,{}\cdot{}})
and
o=(\sigma_{0,{}\cdot{}}^{\prime},\sigma_{0,{}\cdot{}})
denote the combined momentum and parity vectors at the initial time slice.

Proof.

Using first by
[
13
, Lemma 4.4]
that

\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2n+1}\sigma_{0,j}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,j})={}\sigma_{n,1}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1})+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma}),

we can rewrite

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{2n+1}b(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})={}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{2n+1}e^{i\sigma_{0,j}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,j})s_{0}}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})

(3.42)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle e^{i\sigma_{n,1}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1})s_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{is_{0}\Theta_{j-1;\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}\prod_{j=1}^{2n+1}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0}),

and thus (when ignoring the first factor on the right hand side above) the oscillating exponential in (
3.21
) becomes

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{is_{0}\Theta_{j-1;\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})-it_{j}(s_{0},s)\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=2}^{n}\prod_{m=1}^{j-1}e^{-is_{m}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}={}\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}e^{-is_{m}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}.

It then follows that we can write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{3}_{n}(s_{0},t,k,1,\kappa)[b(s_{0})]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i\lambda)^{n}\sum_{\ell\in G_{n},\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1)\hat{g}(k_{n,1})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\times e^{i\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1})s_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{1+2n}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j},s_{0})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]

(3.43)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j=0}^{n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-is_{j}\gamma_{j}^{+}},

where the
\gamma_{j}^{+}
are defined by

\displaystyle\gamma^{+}_{n}={}

\displaystyle-i\kappa_{0},

(3.44)

\displaystyle\gamma^{+}_{l}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=l+1}^{n}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(k,\sigma)-i\kappa_{n-l},\mbox{ for }1\leq l\leq n-1.

Next, we use this to expand the left hand side of (
3.40
), however, in the expansion of the conjugated factor we make a change of variables
\sigma_{i,j}^{\prime}=-\sigma_{i,2(n-i+1)-j}
,
k_{i,j}^{\prime}=-k_{i,2(n-i+1)-j}
, and
\ell_{i}^{\prime}=2(n-i+1)-\ell_{i}
.
This corresponds to inverting the order and swapping the signs of all variables in each time slice, or, graphically speaking, inverting the corresponding Feynmann diagram and changing all the parities.
Now, using that
\Theta(-(k_{3},k_{2},k_{1}),-\sigma)=-\Theta((k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),\sigma)
, the oscillating factor in the time integral from the conjugate tree can, as before, be written
\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-is_{j}\gamma_{j}^{-}}
, with
\gamma_{j}^{-}
given by

\displaystyle\gamma^{-}_{n^{\prime}}={}

\displaystyle-i\kappa_{0},

(3.45)

\displaystyle\gamma^{-}_{l^{\prime}}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=l^{\prime}+1}^{n}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})-i\kappa_{n^{\prime}-l},\mbox{ for }1\leq l^{\prime}\leq n^{\prime}-1.

Further, by stationarity of the initial state, the resulting expansion contains products of the form

\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{1+2n}\hat{a}(k_{0,j}^{\prime},\sigma_{0,j}^{\prime})\prod_{j=1}^{1+2n}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}.

After inserting the truncated correlation functions using
Lemma

3.2
, and applying
Lemma

3.6
to interlace the time sequences, we thus arrive at

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{3}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{J\mbox{ interlaces }(n-1,n-1)}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\mathcal{F}^{3}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},t/\varepsilon-s,\kappa),

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{3}_{n}={}

\displaystyle(-\lambda^{2})^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum\limits_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n,\ell}(k,\sigma)\Delta_{n,\ell^{\prime}}(k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\hat{g}(k_{n,1})^{\ast}\hat{g}(-k_{n,1}^{\prime})\prod_{A\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j\in A}K_{j}\bigg)C_{\lvert A\rvert}(K_{A},o_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg]

\displaystyle\times e^{is(\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1})-\omega^{\lambda}(-k_{n,1}^{\prime}))}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\sigma_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}}^{\prime}\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell^{\prime}_{j}},-\sigma^{\prime}_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}})\big]

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,2n-2}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}-s-\sum_{j=0}^{2n-2}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{2n-2}e^{-ir_{j}(\gamma_{J_{+}(j;J)+1}^{+}+\gamma_{J_{-}(j;J)+1}^{-})}.

The presence of the cluster
\delta
-functions implies that
\sum_{j=1}^{4n+2}K_{j}=0
, which in turn implies, by iteratively following the
\delta
-functions from the interactions in the direction of time, that we must have
k_{n,1}+k_{n,1}^{\prime}=0
in
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
This means that
\omega^{\lambda}(-k_{n,1}^{\prime})=\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1})
, so the
s
-dependent factor in the third line above vanishes.
Finally, shifting the indices in the time integral upwards by two and setting
m=m(j)=J_{+}(j-2,J)+1
and
m^{\prime}=m(j)^{\prime}=J_{-}(j-2,J)+1
, we arrive at (
3.41
) after noting that

\displaystyle\gamma_{J_{+}(j-2;J)+1}^{+}+\gamma_{J_{-}(j-2;J)+1}^{-}={}\gamma_{m}^{+}+\gamma_{m^{\prime}}^{-}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\Theta_{i-1:\ell_{i}}(k,\sigma)+\sum_{i=m^{\prime}+1}^{n}\Theta_{i-1:\ell_{i}^{\prime}}(k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})-i(\kappa_{n-m}+\kappa_{n-m^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2(n-m)+1}\sigma_{m,i}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{m,i})+\sum_{i=1}^{2(n-m^{\prime})+1}\sigma_{m^{\prime},i}^{\prime}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{m,i}^{\prime})-i(\kappa_{n-m}+\kappa_{n-m^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\gamma(j;J),

by
[
13
, Lemma 4.4]
and the definition (
3.38
) of
\gamma(j;J)
.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

The components of
\bar{Q}^{err}_{1}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{1}_{n}(\tilde{s},t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(\tilde{s})]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}

(3.46)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{J\mbox{ interlaces }(n,n)}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},t/\varepsilon-\tilde{s},\kappa),

where, denoting
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\cup\{(n,1)\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},\tau,\kappa)={}

\displaystyle(-\lambda^{2})^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\lvert\hat{g}(k_{n,1})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n,\ell}(k,\sigma)\Delta_{n,\ell^{\prime}}(k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{A\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j\in A}K_{j}\bigg)C_{\lvert A\rvert}(K_{A},o_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime}\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell^{\prime}_{j}},-\sigma^{\prime}_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}})\big]

(3.47)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,2n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(\tau-\sum_{j=0}^{2n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{2n}e^{-is_{j}\gamma(j;J)}.

Proof.

The proof follows the same line of argumentation as
Proposition

3.7
, the main difference being the extra time integration in (
3.22
).
For notational convenience, we rename the integration variable
r
in (
3.22
) to
s_{0}
.
Using (
3.42
) (with
s_{0}
replaced by
\tilde{s}
) to extract the phase factor from the product of
b
operators, we obtain as before the oscillating factor

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{i\tilde{s}\Theta_{j-1;\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})-it_{j}(\tilde{s}+s_{0},s)\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}={}\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}e^{-is_{m}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})},

which in turn leads to the total exponential

\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{n-j}}\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}e^{-is_{m}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})}={}\prod_{m=0}^{n}e^{-is_{m}\gamma_{m}^{+}}.

This way, we obtain an expression analogous to (
3.43
), except the time integral is over
(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n}}
and contains an extra factor
e^{-is_{0}\gamma_{0}^{+}}
, and the delta function in the time integral has
t
replaced by
t-\tilde{s}
.
The rest of the proof is exactly the same as in
Proposition

3.7
, and we arrive at (
3.46
) and (
3.47
).
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

The components of
\bar{Q}^{err}_{2}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\Big\lvert\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{2}_{n}(s,t/\varepsilon,k,1,\kappa)[b(s)]\Big\rvert^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\lambda}

(3.48)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{J\mbox{ interlaces }(n-1,n-1)}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},t/\varepsilon-s,\kappa),

where
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}=0
if the cluster decomposition
S
contains a pairing between two initial time vertices that are connected to the same of the two bottom interaction vertices (amputated vertices) in the corresponding diagram (see
Section

3.2.2
below).
Otherwise, denoting
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\cup\{(n,1)\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},\tau,\kappa)={}

\displaystyle(-\lambda^{2})^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\lvert\hat{g}(k_{n,1})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n,\ell}(k,\sigma)\Delta_{n,\ell^{\prime}}(k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{A\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j\in A}K_{j}\bigg)C_{\lvert A\rvert}(K_{A},o_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\big[\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}}\Psi_{0}(k_{0:\ell_{1}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}})\sigma_{1,\ell^{\prime}_{1}}^{\prime}\Psi_{0}(-k^{\prime}_{0:\ell^{\prime}_{1}},-\sigma^{\prime}_{1,\ell^{\prime}_{1}})\big]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=2}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\sigma_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}}^{\prime}\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell^{\prime}_{j}},-\sigma^{\prime}_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}})\big]

(3.49)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{2,2n}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(\tau-\sum_{j=2}^{2n}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=2}^{2n}e^{-is_{j}\gamma(j;J)}.

Proof.

Noting that (
3.42
) also holds for a product containing the truncated factor in the definition (
3.23
) of
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{2}
, the proof follows exactly the lines of
Proposition

3.7
up until the point of inserting the cumulant expansion (
3.30
) into the expectation

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{1}^{\prime}-1}\hat{a}(k_{0,j}^{\prime},\sigma_{0,j}^{\prime})\Bigg[\prod_{j=\ell_{1}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{1}^{\prime}+2}\hat{a}(k_{0,j}^{\prime},\sigma_{0,j}^{\prime})\Bigg]^{T}\prod_{j=\ell_{1}^{\prime}+3}^{1+2n}\hat{a}(k_{0,j}^{\prime},\sigma_{0,j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{1}-1}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})\Bigg[\prod_{j=\ell_{1}}^{\ell_{1}+2}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})\Bigg]^{T}\prod_{j=\ell_{1}+3}^{1+2n}\hat{a}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})\Bigg\rangle_{\lambda}.

For the sake of readability, we will do this step just in the case
n=1
, where only the truncated factors remain.
In this simple case, where the vector of parities is forced to be
(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma)=(-1,-1,1,-1,1,1)
, we use the definitions (
3.1.1
) and (
3.9
) of the truncated factors to expand the expectation into a sum of nine terms,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big[\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big]^{T}\big[\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big]^{T}\Big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad-\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle-\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}
