\displaystyle\quad-\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle+\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad-\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}\big\langle\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big\rangle_{\lambda}.

Now, inserting the cumulant expansion (
3.30
) into each of these, it follows that any given contribution from the first term above is cancelled exactly when the corresponding partition
S
contains one of the pairs from the truncated products (
3.1.1
), (
3.9
), that is,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big[\hat{a}(k_{1}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2}^{\prime},-1)\hat{a}(k_{3}^{\prime},1)\big]^{T}\big[\hat{a}(k_{1},-1)\hat{a}(k_{2},1)\hat{a}(k_{3},1)\big]^{T}\Big\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\tilde{\pi}(I_{6})}\epsilon(S)\prod_{A=\{J_{1},\dots,J_{\lvert A\rvert}\}\in S}\bigg[\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert A\rvert}}K_{j}\bigg){C}_{\lvert A\rvert}(K_{A},o_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\bigg],

where
\tilde{\pi}(I_{6})
is the set of all ordered partitions of
\{k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\}

not
containing any of the pairings
\{k_{1}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime}\}
,
\{k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime}\}
,
\{k_{1},k_{2}\}
,
\{k_{1},k_{3}\}
.
Note also that that any partition containing one of the pairs
\{k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}\}
,
\{k_{2},k_{3}\}
will not contribute in the expression above, because the corresponding
C_{2}
-factors vanish by (
3.31
).
Thus, for the simple case
n=1
, the amplitude
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{2}
vanishes for any cluster decomposition
S
containing a pairing between the momenta
\{k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime},k_{3}^{\prime}\}
, or between the momenta
\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\}
, which are exactly the sets of momenta connected to the bottom two vertices in the corresponding diagram.
The same conclusion holds generally for any
n\geq 1
, that is,
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}
vanishes if
S
contains a pairing in
\{k_{0,\ell_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime},k_{0,\ell_{1}^{\prime}+1}^{\prime},k_{0,\ell_{1}^{\prime}+2}^{\prime}\}
, or in
\{k_{0,\ell_{1}},k_{0,\ell_{1}+1},k_{0,\ell_{1}+2}\}
.
The expression (
3.49
) for other cluster decompositions is then obtained by following the same steps as in the proof of
Proposition

3.7
.
∎

3.2.
Diagrams for main and amputated terms

This subsection is devoted to the diagrams of the two key quantities that we need to estimate: the main and the error terms.

3.2.1.
Main diagrams

The key difference between (
3.33
) and (
3.19
) is the appearance of the delta function
\delta_{L}\big(\sum_{j\in A}k_{0,j}\big)
acting on the set
A
and the two new pairities
{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1)
,
{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{0,0}=-1)
.
The construction of the diagram for (
3.33
) can be done by modifying the graphs introduced in Subsection
3.1.2
as follows (see Figure
2
):

Figure 2.
An example of a Feynman diagram with clusters, also featuring the additional initial vertex with momentum
k_{0,0}
.
Two new cluster vertices are added at the bottom of the diagram, to connect
k_{0,0},k_{0,3}
and
k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,4},k_{0,5}
.

(1)

For each
A
, we add a
cluster vertex
at the bottom of the graph and connect this new vertex to all of the vertices
k_{0,j}
,
j\in A
, appearing in the delta function
\delta_{L}\big(\sum_{j\in A}k_{0,j}\big)
.

(2)

To represent the parity
{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1)
, we just assign the value
1
to
\sigma_{n,1}
, which corresponds to the parity of the top of the graph.

(3)

To represent the parity
{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{0,0}=-1)
, we will also add a vertex
(0,0)
and a segment
k_{0,0}
with
\sigma_{0,0}=-1
.
We assign the value of
k_{0,0}
to be
-k_{n,1}
.
This segment goes from the vertex
(0,0)
to the top line.

(4)

Thanks to the parities
\sigma_{n,1}=1
and
\sigma_{0,0}=-1
, the remaining parities can be computed from top to bottom using the same rule discussed in
Section

3.1.2
.
At each iteration, the parity remains unchanged in the middle line and we set
-1
for the segment on the left and
1
for the segment on the right.
The cluster vertices do not affect the parities.

(5)

In this diagram, when all cluster vertices and their edges are removed, the diagram splits into two components.
The left one contains only one edge
k_{0,0}
and is called the
minus tree
.
The right one is called the
plus tree
.

(6)

The time-dependent factor in (
3.33
) can also be expressed under the form

(3.50)

\prod_{j=0}^{n}e^{-is_{j}\gamma_{j}},\mbox{ where }\gamma_{j}=\sum_{l=j+1}^{n}\Theta_{l-1:\ell_{l}}(k,\sigma)-i\kappa_{n-j}.

Since
-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1})=\sigma_{0,0}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,0})
, we have that

\mathrm{Re}\gamma_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{2(n-j)+1}\sigma_{j,l}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j,l})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n,1}).

(7)

Each fusion vertex carries a factor
-i\lambda\Psi_{1}
and each cluster vertex carries a factor
C_{\lvert A\rvert}
.

3.2.2.
Diagrams for error terms

In this subsection, we will construct Feynman diagrams for the terms
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n},\mathcal{F}^{3}_{n}
.
The diagram can be constructed in a similar manner with that of (
3.33
) (see Figure
3
).

Figure 3.
In this diagram, the plus and the minus trees are denoted by the plus
+
and the minus
-
signs. Each vertex at the bottom of the plus tree is paired to a vertex at the bottom of the minus tree.

(1)

The appearance of
k,k^{\prime}
and
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
indicates that we need two graphs.
Moreover, the quantity
{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)
shows that the two tops of the two graphs have the parities
-1
and
1
.
We set the left one to be
-1
.

(2)

Thanks to (i), when all cluster vertices and their edges are removed, the diagram also splits into two components.
The left one is called the
minus tree
and the right one is called the
plus tree
.

(3)

The delta function
\delta\big(t-\sum_{j=2}^{2n}s_{j}\big)
in the expressions for
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{3}
indicates that the first two time slices have zero length.
We say that these time slices are
amputated
, and we also call the corresponding diagrams
amputated diagrams
.
This can be denoted on the diagram for instance by shading the first two time slices.

(4)

To make the identification of the amputated terms with their corresponding Feynman diagrams unique, we can without loss of generality assume that the first fusion from the bottom always takes place in the minus tree.

(5)

For each
A
, we add a cluster vertex at the bottom of the graph and connect the new vertex to all of the vertices
k_{0,j}
, and
k_{0,j}^{\prime}
,
j\in A
.
The plus and minus trees are indeed connected only by the cluster vertices.

(6)

For
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{3}
, each interaction vertex in the plus tree carries a factor
i\lambda\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j-1}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})
, and each interaction vertex in the minus tree carries a factor
-i\lambda\sigma_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}}^{\prime}\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell^{\prime}_{j-1}},\sigma^{\prime}_{j,\ell^{\prime}_{j}})
.
The same is true for
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}
, except
\Psi_{1}
is replaced by
\Psi_{0}
at the amputated vertices at the bottom of the diagram.
Each cluster vertex carries a factor
C_{\lvert A\rvert}
with a corresponding
\delta_{L}
-function.

3.3.
Signs of cluster decompositions, and leading motives

3.3.1.
Properties of the sign of a cluster decomposition
S

The presence of the sign
\epsilon(S)
in the definition (
3.28
) of the truncated moments is a distinctly
fermionic
feature, and it can be seen as an expression of the Pauli exclusion principle.
By commutativity, it does not appear in the classical setting
[
13
]
, or in the case of bosons.
Ultimately,
\epsilon(S)
will be responsible for some of the signs appearing in (
2.2
) in the main theorem.

Here we investigate more closely the sign
\epsilon(S)
for a given cluster decomposition
S
of the set
I_{n}
, in particular the behaviour of the sign when attaching two diagrams together, as explained below.
First, we have:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
S=\{A_{1},\dotsc,A_{k}\}
be a clustering of
I_{n}
, and assume that
\lvert A_{j}\rvert
is even for all
j
.
Then the sign
\epsilon(S)
is independent of the ordering of the elements
A_{j}
of
S
.

Proof.

Denoting the cluster elements by
A_{j}=\{a_{j,1},\dotsc,a_{j,n_{j}}\}
with
n=\sum_{j}n_{j}
, the permutation
\tau_{S}
corresponding to
S
is by definition

(3.51)

\tau_{S}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&\cdots&n_{1}&n_{1}+1&\cdots&n-1&n\\
a_{1,1}&a_{1,2}&\cdots&a_{1,n_{1}}&a_{2,1}&\cdots&a_{k,n_{k}-1}&a_{k,n_{k}}\end{pmatrix},

and so
\epsilon(S)\coloneq\sgn(\tau_{S})=(-1)^{N(\tau_{S})}
, where
N(\tau_{S})
is the number of inversions of
\tau_{S}
, that is, the number of pairs
i<j
with
\tau_{S}(i)>\tau_{S}(j)
.
Note that by construction, there is no contribution to the number of inversions coming from pairs within any given cluster
A_{j}
, since we assume
a_{j,1}<a_{j,2}<\cdots<a_{j,n_{j}}
, so all inversions of
\tau_{S}
must come from pairs between the clusters.

Now, fix
j\in\{1,\dotsc,n-1\}
and suppose that we switch the order of
A_{j}
and
A_{j+1}
in
S
.
It is clear that this action does not change the number of inversions between
A_{j}
and any of the other clusters, apart from
A_{j+1}
.
It follows that we can assume that
S
has only two clusters
S=\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
.
Now, an inversion
i<j
of
\tau_{S}
must satisfy
i\in\{1,\dotsc,n_{1}\}
,
j\in\{n_{1}+1,\dotsc,n_{1}+n_{2}\}
, and
\tau_{S}(i)=a_{1,i}>a_{2,j-n_{1}}=\tau_{S}(j)
.
After switching the order of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, the permutation
\tau_{\widetilde{S}}
of
\widetilde{S}=\{A_{2},A_{1}\}
attains these values at
n_{2}+i
and
j-n_{1}
, respectively.
That is, the corresponding pair in
\widetilde{S}
will be
j-n_{1}<n_{2}+i
with
\tau_{\widetilde{S}}(j-n_{1})=a_{2,j-n_{1}}<a_{1,i}=\tau_{\widetilde{S}}(n_{2}+i)
.
It is now clear that and inversion of
S
corresponds to a non-inversion of
\widetilde{S}
(and vice versa), so

N(\tau_{S})=n_{1}+n_{2}-N(\tau_{\widetilde{S}}),

from which it follows that
\epsilon(S)=\epsilon(\widetilde{S})
, since
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
are assumed to be even.
∎

Remark 3.11

(Reading the sign
\epsilon(S)
from a diagram)
.

Consider any clustering
S=\{A_{1},\dotsc,A_{k}\}
of
I_{n}
.
We make the following observations that allows one to graphically read the sign
\epsilon(S)
from the drawing of a diagram.

(1)

Assume the sets
A_{j}=\{a_{j,1},\dotsc,a_{j,n_{j}}\}
are ordered such that
a_{i,1}<a_{j,1}
whenever
i<j
.
Assume further that on the diagram, all cluster vertices are placed on the same horizontal line below the initial time vertices in such a way that the vertex of
A_{i}
is to the left of
A_{j}
whenever
i<j
.
Then it is apparent that if a cluster edge of
A_{i}
intersects a cluster edge of
A_{j}
, it exactly corresponds to an inversion of the permutation of
S
(just keep the representation (
3.51
) in mind).
Consequently, the number of inversions of the permutation determined by
S
is exactly equal to the number of intersections
g
between the cluster edges of the graph, see e.g.
Fig.

4
.
In particular,
\epsilon(S)=(-1)^{g}
.

(2)

If
\lvert A_{j}\rvert
is even for all
j
, then the placement of the cluster vertices is irrelevant when determining
\epsilon(S)
(as long as the cluster vertices are placed below the initial time vertices).
The sign
\epsilon(S)
can still be calculated as
\epsilon(S)=(-1)^{g}
.

To see this, take two clusters
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
and consider what happens if we move one of the cluster vertices.
Since
\lvert A_{1}\rvert
and
\lvert A_{2}\rvert
are both even, the number of intersections between their cluster edges can only change by an even number, as long as the cluster vertex remains below the horizontal
t=0
line, see e.g.
Fig.

4
for an illustration.
Hence the parity of the total number of intersections remains the same.

Figure 4.
Two illustrations of the cluster decomposition
S=\{\{1,4\},\{2,5,6,7\},\{3,8\}\}
.
At the top, the clusters are drawn according to the instructions of
Remark

3.11
(a), so the sign of the corresponding permutation can be read as
\epsilon(S)=(-1)^{5}=-1
.
At the bottom, the rightmost cluster vertex have been moved, changing the number of intersections by an even number, thus leaving the parity invariant.

Under certain conditions, it is possible to attach one Feynman diagram to the bottom of another one, and later it will be useful to know how the signs of the corresponding clusters are related.
Take two Feynman diagrams
\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2}
with clusters
S_{1},S_{2}
.
Consider a cluster
A\in S_{1}
, and assume that
\mathscr{G}_{2}
has a number of (plus or minus) trees that is equal to the number of elements in
A
.
(So far, we have only considered Feynman diagrams consisting of either one or two trees, but nothing prevents us from generalising to diagrams with an arbitrary number of trees).
If, furthermore, the parities of the cluster edges of
A
coincide with the parities of the edges at the top of
\mathscr{G}_{2}
, then
\mathscr{G}_{2}
can be attached to the bottom of
\mathscr{G}_{1}
at the cluster
A
.
The resulting graph
\mathscr{G}
is also a Feynman diagram with interaction vertices equal to the union of the interaction vertices of
\mathscr{G}_{1}
and
\mathscr{G}_{2}
, and with clusters
S=(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\setminus\{A\}
, up to re-ordering.
At the bottom of
\mathscr{G}
, each initial time vertex of
\mathscr{G}_{1}
connected to
A
is replaced by the set of initial time vertices of
\mathscr{G}_{2}
at the bottom of the corresponding tree of
\mathscr{G}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.12

(Multiplicativity of the sign
\epsilon(S)
)
.

Let
\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{G}_{2}
be two Feynman diagrams with corresponding cluster decompositions
S_{1},S_{2}
.
Assume that
S_{1},S_{2}
contain only clusters with an even number of elements, and that
S_{1}
contains a cluster where
\mathscr{G}_{2}
can be attached, forming a composite diagram
\mathscr{G}
.
Then the sign of the cluster decomposition
S
of
\mathscr{G}
satisfies

\epsilon(S)=\epsilon(S_{1})\epsilon(S_{2}).

Sketch of proof.

For simplicity, we consider only the simple case where
\mathscr{G}_{2}
consists of two trees, and where
\mathscr{G}_{2}
attaches to
\mathscr{G}_{1}
at a pairing.
Note that since
\mathscr{G}_{2}
is a Feynman diagram, there is an odd number of initial vertices at the bottom of each of its trees.
Furthermore, since each cluster of
S_{2}
has an even number of elements, the number of cluster edges from the plus tree connecting to the minus tree must be odd.

In the diagram
\mathscr{G}
, we need to determine the parity of the total number of intersections between cluster edges.
Note that for any given cluster edge coming from
S_{2}
, the number of intersections between this edge and all other cluster edges originating from
S_{2}
is the same as in the original graph
\mathscr{G}_{2}
.
The same is true when considering intersections between cluster edges originating from
S_{1}\setminus\{A\}
, where
A
is the cluster to which
\mathscr{G}_{2}
is attached.
Hence, we only need to argue that the number of intersections between
S_{2}
and
S_{1}\setminus\{A\}
has the same parity as the number of intersections between
A
and
S_{1}\setminus\{A\}
in the original graph
\mathscr{G}_{1}
.

Consider thus a cluster edge
e
from a cluster in
S_{1}\setminus\{A\}
that intersects with a cluster edge
e^{\prime}
from
A
in the original graph
\mathscr{G}_{1}
.
The initial vertex
v^{\prime}
connected to
e^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{G}_{1}
gets replaced by an odd number of initial vertices after attaching
\mathscr{G}_{2}
, corresponding to the initial vertices from one of the trees of
\mathscr{G}_{2}
.
Furthermore, an odd number of cluster edges connect these new vertices in
\mathscr{G}
to the other tree coming from
\mathscr{G}_{2}
.
The remaining (even) number of new initial time vertices in the tree that replaces
v^{\prime}
will not contribute to the number of intersections with
e
, simply by drawing them so that they do not intersect with the cluster edges from
S_{1}
.
This means that the intersection between
e
and
e^{\prime}
gets replaced by an odd number of intersections between
e
the cluster edges connecting the new vertices replacing
v^{\prime}
to the other tree in
\mathscr{G}_{2}
.
In other words, the number of intersections
N(S)
between the cluster edges in
\mathscr{G}
must satisfy

N(S)=N(S_{1})+N(S_{2})+2m,

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and the assertion of the lemma follows.
∎

3.3.2.
Leading motives and main terms

It turns out (and will be proved later) that with suitable choices of
N
and
\kappa
, the only terms giving a non-zero contribution in the weakly interacting limit come from the main term (
3.32
), and only a certain type of diagrams will contribute to the limit.
These leading graphs are precisely the graphs where the clusters
S
consist only of pairings, and which are formed by iteration of
leading motives
, also called
immediate recollisions
, which can be attached to pairings at the bottom of any existing graph.

+
-
G1
-

-
+
G2
-

+
-
G3
-

-
+
G4
-

+
+
L1

+
+
L2

+
+
L3

+
+
L4

+
+
L5

+
+
L6

Figure 5.
Half of the leading motives.
The top four are
gain motives
, while the bottom six are called
loss motives
.
The remaining leading motives (denoted by G1
,\dotsc,
G4,L1
{}^{-},\dotsc,
L6
-
) can be obtained from the ones depicted by first inverting the parities of all edges, and then inverting the order of the edges below each interaction vertex (for the loss motives, this corresponds to ”inverting” the entire motive).

There are twenty such leading motives, half of them are shown in
Fig.

5
.
The gain motives (e.g. the four top motives in
Fig.

5
) can be used to replace any pairing that has the correct parity.
For instance, the depicted gain motives can only replace a pairing whose left leg has the parity
-1
, while the remaining ”inverted” motives can replace a pairing whose left leg has the parity
+1
.
The loss motives may be attached to any edge in the graph that has the correct parity, so any of the twelve loss motives may be attached to any pairing by simply attaching it to the leg that has the suitable parity.

Remark 3.13

(Leading motives)
.

The leading motives have two defining features.
The first feature is that they
preserve the incoming momentum
, which for the loss motives simply means that the momenta corresponding to the top and bottom edges (where the motives are attached to a diagram) coincide.
This is easily checked by noting that the momentum at the top edge of a loss motive is equal to the sum of the five momenta at the bottom, four of which cancel pairwise, due to the pairings.
For gain motives, the momenta corresponding to the two top edges are equal, but with opposite signs.

The second feature of the leading motives is that they
preserve the phase
, which simply means that the phase factors
\Theta
corresponding to the two vertices are the same, but with
opposite
signs.
Suppose that we attach any leading motive to an existing Feynman diagram, and that the vertices of the motive in the resulting diagram are
v_{i-1}
and
v_{i}
.
Then, since
\Theta_{i-1:\ell_{i}}(k,\sigma)=-\Theta_{i:\ell_{i+1}}(k,\sigma)
, the presence of the vertices
v_{i-1}
and
v_{i}
will only give a contribution to the phase factor
\gamma_{i}
.
All other phase factors
\gamma_{j}
will be independent of
v_{i-1}
and
v_{i}
by (
3.50
), because the contributions from these two vertices cancel out.

Example 3.14

(Amplitude for the loss motive L1)
.

Figure 6.
The leading motive L1 attached to a single pairing with parities
-,+
.

For later reference, let us calculate the amplitude
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{main}
for the diagram in
Fig.

6
, obtained by attaching the motive L1 to a single pairing with parities
-,+
.
In this case,
\ell=(1,1)
and
S=\{\{0,3\},\{1,4\},\{2,5\}\}
.
Since there are three intersections between cluster edges in the diagram, we have
\epsilon(S)=-1
.
It as also apparent from the diagram that the momenta are related by

k_{0,0}={}-k_{0,3},\qquad k_{0,1}={}-k_{0,4},\qquad k_{0,2}={}-k_{0,5},

k_{1,1}={}k_{0,1}+k_{0,2}+k_{0,3},

and

k_{2,1}={}k_{1,1}+k_{1,2}+k_{1,3}={}\sum_{j=1}^{5}k_{0,j}={}k_{0,3}.

From the two interaction vertices we get the factors

\displaystyle-\Psi_{1}(k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,3},-1)={}

\displaystyle-\Phi_{1}(k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,3})U(k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,3},-1)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\Phi_{1}(k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,3})\widehat{V}(k_{0,2}+k_{0,3}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(k_{1,1},k_{1,2},k_{1,3},1)={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(k_{1,1},k_{0,4},k_{0,5})\widehat{V}(k_{1,1}+k_{0,4}).

Since all the clusters in the diagram are pairs, by
Remark

3.3
, we get the three factors
C_{2}((k_{0,0},k_{0,3}),(-1,1),\lambda,\Omega)=W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{0,3},-1)=W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{0,3})
,
W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{0,4})
, and
W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{0,5})
from the cluster vertices.
What remains is to calculate the phase factors in (
3.33
),

\Theta_{1:1}(k,\sigma)={}\Theta(k_{1,1},k_{0,4},k_{0,5},1),

and note that

\displaystyle\Theta_{0:1}(k,\sigma)={}

\displaystyle\Theta(k_{0,1},k_{0,2},k_{0,3},-1)={}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,3})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,1})-(\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,2})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1,1}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-(\omega^{\lambda}(-k_{0,5})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1,1})+\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,4})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,3}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\Theta(k_{1,1},k_{0,4},k_{0,5},1),

so the phase in the initial time slice vanishes.
Finally, we can express everything in terms of the variables
k_{0}\coloneq k_{2,1}
,
k_{1}\coloneq k_{1,1}
,
k_{2}\coloneq k_{0,4}
, and
k_{3}\coloneq k_{0,5}
, and (
3.33
) reduces to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{main}_{2}(S,\ell,t/\varepsilon,\kappa)={}

\displaystyle-\lambda^{2}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{4}}\mathrm{d}k\delta_{L}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\hat{g}(k_{0})^{\ast}\hat{f}(k_{0})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\times\Phi_{1}(-k_{2},-k_{3},k_{0})\Phi_{1}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{0})W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{2})W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{3})

(3.52)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}-\sum_{j=0}^{2}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{2}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{2-j}}e^{-is_{1}\Theta(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},1)}.

4.
A review of momentum graphs

In Subsections
3.2.1
and
3.2.2
, we introduced Feynman diagrams for the main and error terms of the Duhamel expansion of
Q^{\lambda}[g,f](t)
.
We will combine these two diagrams into a single class of diagrams called momentum graphs, and review the general properties of these graphs.
Since the general structure of the graphs appearing in our problem coincides completely with those appearing in the setting of the weakly nonlinear Schrödinger equation
[
13
]
, we can reuse the toolbox that has already been developed for the momentum graphs in
[
13
]
.
For the sake of completeness and for later reference, we recall in this section the relevant notions and results on momentum graphs, but mostly refer to
[
13
]
for the proofs.

We add one more delta-function to (
3.33
) by multiplying with a factor

(4.1)

\displaystyle 1={}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k_{e_{0}}\delta_{L}(k_{e_{0}}-k-k^{\prime}),

where
(k,k^{\prime})=(k_{n,1},k_{0,0})
for a main diagram and
(k,k^{\prime})=(k_{n,1},k_{n,1}^{\prime})
for the diagram of an error term.
In the new graph, the additional delta function is represented by introducing two new vertices and one new edge to the graph.
One of the new vertices is a
fusion vertex
connecting two edges
k
and
k^{\prime}
to the new edge
k_{e_{0}}
, and the other vertex is a
root vertex
placed at the top of the graph, at the other end of
k_{e_{0}}
.
The new graph is called the
momentum graph
.
We denote a momentum graph by
\mathscr{G}=(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
, where
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{E}
are the sets of vertices and edges.

This graph depends on the cluster partitioning
S
, the number of interaction vertices
n^{\prime}
and
n
in the minus and plus trees, the interaction histories
\ell^{\prime}
and
\ell
in the minus and plus trees, and finally the interlacing function
J
.
In the case of a main diagram,
n^{\prime}=0
, and then
\ell^{\prime},J
are not relevant.
As a consequence, given
S,n^{\prime},n,\ell^{\prime},\ell,J
, we can reconstruct the momentum graph.

Construction of momentum graphs

Given the parameters
S,n^{\prime},n,\ell^{\prime},\ell,J
, the momentum graph can be reconstructed the following way (see Figure
7
).

Figure 7.
An example of a momentum graph with
n=n^{\prime}=2
and cluster decomposition
S=\{\{1,5\},\{2,9\},\{3,4,7,8\},\{6,10\}\}
.
The sets
\mathscr{V}_{R}
,
\mathscr{V}_{F}
,
\mathscr{V}_{0}
,
\mathscr{V}_{C}
and the vertices
v_{R}
,
v_{N}
are marked on the graph.

•

Let
N=n+n^{\prime}
denote the total number of interactions.
We start with
\mathscr{G}^{0}=(\mathscr{V}^{0},\mathscr{E}^{0})
, where
\mathscr{V}^{0}
contains two vertices
v_{R}
and
v_{N+1}
, and
\mathscr{E}^{0}
contains only one edge
e_{0}
that connects those two vertices.

•

At the first iteration, we attach one new edge
e_{1}
to
v_{N+1}
.
This edge belongs to the minus tree.
Using
J
, we can determine if the final interaction should be in the minus tree.
If this is the case, we label the edge
e_{1}=\{v_{N+1},v_{N}\}
; otherwise, we do not label it.

•

In the second iteration, we attach a new edge
e_{2}
to
v_{N+1}
.
This edge then belongs to the plus tree.
If the final interaction does not occur in the minus tree, then it is in the plus tree, and we label
e_{2}=\{v_{N+1},v_{N}\}
.

•

The next three steps are to attach another three edges to
v_{N}
, from left to right (with parities
-1,\sigma,1
, as explained in
Sections

3.2.1
and
3.2.2
).
For the moment, we leave the vertices that these edges connect to unlabelled.

•

Using
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
and
J
, we can determine which edge should connect to the next interaction vertex.
This edge has an unlabelled vertex which we now denote by
v_{N-1}
.

•

This procedure of attaching three new edges to the most newly created interaction vertex and then labelling the next interaction vertex is iterated
N
times in total.
This produces a tree starting from
v_{R}
with a vertex set composed of
N+2
labelled and
2N+2
unlabelled vertices.

•

The labelled vertices belong to
\mathscr{V}_{R}=\{v_{R}\}
or
\mathscr{V}_{F}=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{N+1}\}
.
The set
\mathscr{V}_{F}
is called the
fusion vertex set
.
When excluding the topmost fusion vertex
v_{N+1}
, the remaining fusion vertices
\mathscr{V}_{I}=\mathscr{V}_{F}\setminus\{v_{N+1}\}
are called
interaction vertices
.
They correspond to the interaction vertices in the original diagrams from
Section

3
.
The set of the remaining vertices is denoted by
\mathscr{V}_{0}
and is called the
initial time vertex
set.

•

For each cluster
A
in
S
, we add a vertex labelled
u_{A}
.
The set
\mathscr{V}_{C}=\{u_{A}\}_{A\in S}
is called the
cluster vertex set
.
Then the vertex set of the final graph
\mathscr{G}
is
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}_{R}\cup\mathscr{V}_{F}\cup\mathscr{V}_{0}\cup\mathscr{V}_{C}
.
The remaining edges connecting the initial time vertices to the cluster vertices can be added in a natural way (going through
\mathscr{V}_{0}
from left to right).

The above process gives us an unoriented graph
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{G}(S,J,n,\ell,n^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})=(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
.
For two edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
, we say that
e<e^{\prime}
if
e
is created before
e^{\prime}
.
This defines then a complete order
e\leq e^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{E}
.

4.1.
Properties of momentum graphs

We collect here various results on the properties of momentum graphs.
We simply recall the relevant definitions and constructions, but otherwise state the results without proof, since all the proofs are contained in
[
13
]
.
To study the properties of the momentum graph
\mathscr{G}
constructed above, we need the following concepts.

•

Define the function
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{V}\to[0,N+2]
, in which
\mathcal{T}(v)=N+2
if
v\in\mathscr{V}_{R}
,
\mathcal{T}(v)=j
if
v=v_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\cdots,N+1\}
, and
\mathcal{T}(v)=0
in the other cases (meaning
v\in\mathscr{V}_{0}\cup\mathscr{V_{C}}
).
This function gives a natural time-order on the vertices of
\mathscr{G}
.

•

We also define

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}(e)={}\max\{\mathcal{T}(v)\mid v\in e\}\mbox{ for }e\in\mathscr{E}.

It is clear that
e\leq e^{\prime}
leads to
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}(e)\geq\widehat{\mathcal{T}}(e^{\prime})
.

•

For any
v\in\mathscr{V}
, we denote the set of edges attached to
v
by
\mathscr{E}(v)=\{e\in\mathscr{E}\mid v\in e\}
.

•

For
v\in\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{V}_{R}
, there is a delta function associated to it

(4.2)

\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{e\in\mathscr{E}_{+}(v)}k_{e}-\sum_{e\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)}k_{e}\bigg),

where
\mathscr{E}(v)=\mathscr{E}_{+}(v)\cup\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
.
If
v\in\mathscr{V}_{C}
, then
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)=\emptyset
and
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v)=\mathscr{E}(v)
.
Otherwise,
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v)=\{e\}
, where
e
is the first edge attached to
v
in the graph construction described above (meaning that
e
maximizes
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}(e^{\prime})
for
e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}(v)
), and
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)=\mathscr{E}(v)\setminus\{e\}
.

•

The main aim of this section is to develop a standard procedure for integrating out all the delta functions.
This can be done (see below) by associating to each vertex a particular edge which will be used in the integration.
This edge is called
integrated
and the remaining edges are called
free
.
We denote by
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
the set of integrated edges and by
\mathscr{F}
the set of free edges.
We call this process
integrating the momentum constraints
.

•

For any fusion vertex
v\in\mathscr{V}_{F}
, we then call the number of free edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
the
degree
of the fusion vertex and denote it by
\deg(v)
.

Resolution of the momentum constraints

Below, we introduce the algorithm that determines the sets of free and integrated edges in the graph
\mathscr{G}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a momentum graph.
There exists a spanning tree
\mathscr{T}
of
\mathscr{G}
that determines an integration of the momentum constraints.
It satisfies that for any free edge
f
, all the edges
e<f
satisfy that
k_{e}
is independent of
k_{f}
.
Moreover, all free edges end at a fusion vertex: if
f
is free, there is a fusion vertex
v\in\mathscr{V}_{F}
and
v^{\prime}\in\mathscr{V}
such that
\mathcal{T}(v)>\mathcal{T}(v^{\prime})
and
f
is the edge connecting
v
and
v^{\prime}
.

Part of proof.

The construction consists of two steps.
First, we will construct an unoriented tree
\mathscr{T}=(\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}})
from the graph
\mathscr{G}
.
This can be done by an iteration process.
Let us first start with
\mathscr{T}^{(0)}=(\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(0)},\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(0)})
in which both
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(0)},\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(0)}
are empty sets.
The key idea is to go through all edges in
\mathscr{E}
in the opposite direction that they were created above.
To be more precise, suppose that at step
l
, the graph
\mathscr{T}^{(l-1)}
is created.
Now, at step
l
, the corresponding edge is denoted by
e
.
If adding
e
to
\mathscr{T}^{(l-1)}
creates a loop i.e. a subset of the graph in which we can start with any vertex, follow the edges and return to the same vertex (see Figure
8
), then we do not add
e
to
\mathscr{T}^{(l-1)}
and set
\mathscr{T}^{(l)}=\mathscr{T}^{(l-1)}
.

Figure 8.
A momentum graph with
n^{\prime}=3
and
n=2
, where the free edges are denoted by dashed lines.
The remaining (integrated) edges constitute the spanning tree constructed in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

If adding
e
to
\mathscr{T}^{(l-1)}
does not create a loop, then we define
\mathscr{T}^{(l)}
to be the graph created from this addition
That is,
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l)}
is created by adding the vertices of
e
to
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l-1)}
and
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l)}
is created by adding
e
to
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l-1)}
.
In the first case, since adding
e
to
\mathscr{T}^{(l-1)}
would create a loop, the vertices of
e
would already belong to
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l-1)}=\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l)}
.
In the second case, it is obvious that the vertices of
e
also belong to
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}^{(l)}
.
In both cases, no vertex in
e
is lost in the iteration step.
At the final step, we obtain the graph
\mathscr{T}=(\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}})
.
It is then clear that
\mathscr{T}
is a tree, and as every vertex of
\mathscr{V}
belongs to some edge, we have
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathscr{V}
.
Moreover, no loop is contained in
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}\subset\mathscr{E}
; and for every
e\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}
, adding
e
to
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}
would create a loop composed of edges
e^{\prime}
satisfying
e^{\prime}\geq e
and
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}(e^{\prime})\leq\widehat{\mathcal{T}}(e)
.

Second, we create a new oriented tree
\mathscr{T}_{0}
by adding an orientation to
\mathscr{T}
.
We denote the root of the tree to be
v_{R}
and iterate the following algorithm.
Now, let us notice that for any vertex
v
, there is a unique path connecting
v_{R}
and
v
.
The path can be oriented such that it begins with
v
and ends with
v_{R}
.
This process is iterated for all the edges of the tree
\mathscr{T}
.
The iterations may have two different vertices that share edges along the path, but these edges have the same orientation at all of the steps in the algorithm.
Moreover, if two such paths share a vertex, the paths must coincide after this vertex; otherwise, a loop exists in the graph, which is a contradiction with the construction described above.
The resulting graph has the following important feature: for every
v\in\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{V}_{R}
, there is unique edge
E(v)\in\mathscr{E}(v)
pointing out of the vertex.
Moreover, the map
E:\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{V}_{R}\to\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}
is one-to-one.

As a result, we can integrate all the delta functions, using the variable
k_{E(v)}
associated to the edge
E(v)
.
After integrating all the edges, the remaining variables consist of
k_{e}
, with
e\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}
.
These are free integration variables, and we set
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}
to be the set of free edges and
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}=\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{T}}
to be the set of integrated edges.
∎

The rest of this subsection mainly describes how the integrated edges of a graph depend on the free ones.
First, we define a few more concepts related to the oriented graph.

•

For any
v\in\mathscr{V}
, let
\mathscr{F}(v)
denote the set of free edges attached to
v
, i.e.
\mathscr{F}(v)\coloneq\mathscr{E}(v)\cap\mathscr{F}
.

•

Any
v\in\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{V}_{R}
is associated with an
edge parity
mapping
\sigma_{v}:\mathscr{E}(v)\to\{\pm 1\}
, defined by

(4.3)

\sigma_{v}(e)={}\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{ if }e\in\mathscr{E}_{+}(v),\\
-1,&\mbox{ if }e\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v).\end{cases}

The following lemmas detail how the integrated edges belonging to an interaction vertex may depend on the free edges of the graph.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
v
be any fusion vertex.
Then
\mathrm{deg}(v)\in\{0,1,2\}
.

(1)

If
v\in\mathscr{V}_{I}
and
\mathrm{deg}(v)=1
, then
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)=\{f,e,e^{\prime}\}
, where
f
is a free edge and
k_{e}=-k_{f}+q
,
k_{e^{\prime}}=q^{\prime}
, where both
q
and
q^{\prime}
are independent of
k_{f}
.

(2)

If
v\in\mathscr{V}_{I}
and
\mathrm{deg}(v)=2
, then
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)=\{f,f^{\prime},e\}
, where
f,f^{\prime}
are free edges and
k_{e}=-k_{f}-k_{f^{\prime}}+q
, where
q
is independent of both
k_{f}
and
k_{f^{\prime}}
.

It is worth noting that the vertex degrees are
not
a graph invariant; they depend on the construction of the spanning tree in
Theorem

4.1
.
It is easy to construct a different spanning tree such that the vertices can have
\deg(v)=3
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any integrated edge
e=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
, the following identities hold true

\displaystyle k_{e}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\mathscr{P}(v_{1})}\sum_{f=(v,v_{f})\in\mathscr{F}(v)}{\mathds{1}}(v_{f}\notin\mathscr{P}(v_{1}))\big(-\sigma_{v_{1}}(e)\sigma_{v}(f)\big)k_{f}

(4.4)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\sigma_{v_{1}}(e)\sum_{f\in\mathscr{F}}{\mathds{1}}(\exists v\in f\cap\mathscr{P}(v_{1})\mbox{ and }f\cap\mathscr{P}(v_{1})^{c}\neq\emptyset)\sigma_{v}(f)k_{f}.

Moreover, if
f=(v,v^{\prime})
, such that
f\neq e
,
v\in\mathscr{P}(v_{1})
and
v^{\prime}\notin\mathscr{P}(v_{1})
, then
f
is free.

In the above formula, the edge
e
is said to
depend
on the edges
f
, and
k_{e}
is said to
depend
on
k_{f}
.
To put it in words, the integrated edge
e
depends on any given free edge
f
if and only if there is there is a path in the oriented tree
\mathscr{T}_{0}
going to
e
from exactly
one
of the vertices connected to
f
(and not from the other).
As a consequence of the lemma, given a free edge
f
, any integrated edge can depend on it only through
\pm k_{f}
.
It is not possible to have any other prefactors in the linear relations of (
4.4
).

Corollary 4.4
.

For any edge
e\in\mathscr{E}
, there exists a unique set of free edges
\mathscr{F}_{e}
and parities
\sigma_{e,f}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that

(4.5)

k_{e}={}\sum_{f\in\mathscr{F}_{e}}\sigma_{e,f}k_{f}.

Moreover,
k_{e}
is independent of all free momenta if and only if
k_{e}=0
.
This is equivalent to
\mathscr{F}_{e}=\emptyset
, which occurs only when the number of connected components of
\mathscr{G}
increases by
1
when
e
is removed from the graph.

This corollary implies for instance that the edge
e_{0}
attached to the root vertex
v_{R}
always satisfies
k_{e_{0}}=0
, since removing
e_{0}
isolates
v_{R}
in the graph.
Hence, by (
4.1
), the top momenta of the plus and minus trees always sum to zero.
We already used this fact in the expansions of the main term
Q^{main}
and the error terms
\bar{Q}^{err}_{j}
in
Section

3
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
v_{0}\in\mathscr{V}_{I}
and suppose that
\mathrm{deg}(v_{0})=2
.
Let
f,f^{\prime}
be the two free edges ending at
v_{0}
.

(1)

Consider an integrated edge
e=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
.
Then
k_{e}=F_{e}(k_{f},k_{f^{\prime}})+q_{e}
, where
q_{e}
is independent of
k_{f}
and
k_{f^{\prime}}
, and
F_{e}(k_{f},k_{f^{\prime}})
is one of the seven functions:
0
,
\pm k_{f}
,
\pm k_{f^{\prime}}
,
\pm(k_{f}+k_{f^{\prime}})
.

(2)

If
v\in\mathscr{V}_{I}
, and suppose
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
,
e\neq e^{\prime}
, then
k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}}=F(k_{f},k_{f^{\prime}})+q
, where
q
is independent of
k_{f}
and
k_{f^{\prime}}
, and
F(k_{f},k_{f^{\prime}})
is also one of the seven functions:
0
,
\pm k_{f}
,
\pm k_{f^{\prime}}
,
\pm(k_{f}+k_{f^{\prime}})
.

(3)

If
v=v_{0}
, and suppose
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
,
e\neq e^{\prime}
, then
k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}}=F(k_{f},k_{f^{\prime}})+q
, where
q
is independent of
k_{f}
and
k_{f^{\prime}}
, and
F(k_{f},k_{f^{\prime}})
is one of the three functions
-k_{f}
,
-k_{f^{\prime}}
, or
k_{f}+k_{f^{\prime}}
.

In particular, the lemma rules out that an integrated momentum
k_{e}
can depend on
k_{f}
and
k_{f^{\prime}}
through
\pm(k_{f}-k_{f^{\prime}})
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}
,
e\neq e^{\prime}
, and
\mathscr{F}_{e}=\mathscr{F}_{e^{\prime}}
.
Then either
k_{e}=0=k_{e^{\prime}}
or if one removes both
e
and
e^{\prime}
, the graph
\mathscr{G}
splits into two disconnected graphs.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}
,
e\neq e^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathscr{F}_{e}=\mathscr{F}_{e^{\prime}}
if and only if there is a parity
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
k_{e}=\sigma k_{e^{\prime}}
independently of the free momenta.

Definition
.

Two sets
V_{1},V_{2}\subset\mathscr{V}_{0}
are said to have a connection between them if there is a cluster that connects them in the sense that there is
A\in S
such that
A\cap V_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
A\cap V_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
If there is no connection between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, they are said to be isolated from each other.

The two following results are helpful to identify graphs where the corresponding amplitude is identically zero.
Conversely, they also assert useful properties for graphs with non-zero amplitudes.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
v\in\mathscr{V}_{I}
and suppose
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
,
e\neq e^{\prime}
.
Then
k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}}
is independent of all free momenta if and only if the initial time vertices at the bottom of the interaction trees beginning with
e
and
e^{\prime}
are isolated from the rest of the initial time vertices.
It also follows that
k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}}=0
.

Lemma 4.9
.

If the momentum graph has an edge
e
, which is different from the topmost edge, such that
k_{e}=0
, then
S
contains a cluster with an odd number of vertices.

Lemma 4.10
.

A momentum graph has exactly
2(n+n^{\prime})+2-\lvert S\rvert
free momenta.

As a side note, the proof of this in
[
13
]
uses a construction of a second spanning tree along with the fact that the size of spanning trees is a graph invariant.
However, since by the construction of the spanning tree in
Theorem

4.1
there is exactly one integrated edge for every vertex in the graph (excluding
v_{R}
), the result can be obtained by simply counting vertices and edges.

4.2.
Classification of momentum graphs

We now introduce the following new concepts for a momentum graph.

(1)

Let
j
be a time slice in
I_{0,n+n^{\prime}}
of a momentum graph.
If the length of this time slice is
0
, this time slice is then called
amputated
.
If it ends in an interaction vertex, whose degree is either
1
or
2
, the time slice is called
short
.
Otherwise, it is a
long
time slice.

(2)

The graph is called
irrelevant
, if the corresponding amplitude is equal to zero.
Otherwise, it is
relevant
.

(3)

The graph is called
pairing
if for every
A\in S
, we have
\lvert A\rvert=2
.
Otherwise, it is
non-pairing
.

(4)

The graph is called
higher order
if it is relevant and non-pairing.

(5)

The graph is called
fully paired
if it is pairing and it has no interaction vertices of degree one.

(6)

A pairing graph is called
partially paired
if it is not fully paired.

Remark 4.11
.

We have the following examples of irrelevant graphs:

•

If
S
contains a cluster
A
with an odd number of vertices, then the graph is irrelevant because the corresponding
C_{\lvert A\rvert}
is identically zero.

•

If
S
does not respect the parities of the edges in the initial time slice, then the graph is irrelevant, also because some of the truncated correlation functions
C_{\lvert A\rvert}
vanish.

•

If there is a vertex
v\in\mathscr{V}_{I}
with two edges
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
satisfying
k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}}=0
, then the graph is irrelevant because of the presence of the cut-off function
\Phi_{1}
at the vertex.
(The same conclusion also holds for the amputated vertices of the terms
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}
because of
Proposition

3.9
).

4.2.1.
The iterative cluster scheme

An important tool for estimating the amplitudes associated with a momentum graph is knowing bounds on the number of interaction vertices of various degrees.
Denote by
n_{j}
the number of interaction vertices of degree
j
.
Then, since we know from
Lemma

4.2
that the degree of a vertex can only to be either
0,1
, or
2
, it is clear that the total number of interaction vertices can be expressed as
n+n^{\prime}=n_{0}+n_{1}+n_{2}
.

Denote also by
n_{l}(j)
with
l=0,1,2
the number of interaction vertices of degree
l
below and including
v_{j}
.

To estimate these numbers, one can apply the
iterative cluster scheme
(see Figure
9
),
which follows the evolution of the cluster structure when integrating out the delta functions from the bottom to the top of the graph, as described in
Section

4.1
.
We first define
S^{(0)}
to be
S
.

Figure 9.
An illustration of the
iterative cluster scheme
applied to the momentum graph in
Fig.

7
, with the cluster decomposition
S=\{\{1,5\},\{2,9\},\{3,4,7,8\},\{6,10\}\}
.
At each level, we have denoted which three of the edges will be fused in the next iteration.

The cluster
S^{(j)}
is defined to be a clustering of the edges intersecting with the time slice
j
.
The construction
S^{(j)}
is done using an iterative procedure in which the interaction vertices are added to the graph, from the bottom to the top.
The additional vertex
v_{j}
fuses the three edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{j})
into the new one in
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{j})
.
The three edges of
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{j})
belong to some clusters in the previous iteration
S^{(j-1)}
.
The cluster
S^{(j)}
is constructed by joining all clusters of
S^{(j-1)}
containing an edge from
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{j})
, and replacing the three edges by the one in
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{j})
.
The rest remain the same.

If two of the three old edges belong to the same cluster in
S^{(j-1)}
, the addition of the second one would create a loop in the construction of the tree.

If all of the three old edges belong to the same cluster in
S^{(j-1)}
, the addition of the second and third ones would create two loops in the construction of the tree.

Therefore, this process determines the degree of the added interaction vertex.
If the new vertex fuses three separate clusters, it has degree 0.
If it fuses two separate clusters, it has degree 1.
Otherwise, it has degree 2.

The iterative cluster scheme shows that
\lvert S^{(j)}\rvert=\lvert S^{(j-1)}\rvert-2+\deg(v_{j})
.
In addition, the structure of the clustering is conserved, meaning each
S^{(j)}
contains only even clusters.

We then have the following two important lemmas concerning
n_{0},n_{1}
and
n_{2}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Consider a relevant graph, and define
r=n+n^{\prime}+1-\lvert S\rvert
.
Then
n_{np}\leq r\leq n+n^{\prime}
, where
n_{np}
is the number of clusters that are not pairs, that is,
n_{np}\coloneq\lvert\{A\in S\mid\lvert A\rvert>2\}\rvert
.
Moreover
r=0
if and only if
S
is a pairing.
In addition,
n_{2}-n_{0}=r
,
n_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(n+n^{\prime}-r-n_{1})
,
0\leq n_{1}\leq n+n^{\prime}
, and
0\leq n_{0}\leq\lfloor\frac{n+n^{\prime}-r}{2}\rfloor
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Consider a relevant graph, and an interaction vertex
v_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n+n^{\prime}
, then

n_{2}(j)\leq r+n_{0}(j)\mbox{ and }n_{0}(j)\geq\frac{j-(n_{1}+r)}{2},

where
r
is defined in
Lemma

4.12
.

4.3.
Fully paired graphs

The goal of this section is to give a detailed study on fully paired graphs.
We have the following observations.

(1)

Since we have both
n_{1}=0
and
r=0
for fully paired graphs, it follows immediately from the statements of
Lemma

4.12
that
n_{2}=n_{0}=\frac{n+n^{\prime}}{2}
.

(2)

From
Lemma

4.13
, we have
n_{0}(j)\geq\frac{j}{2}
and
n_{2}(j)\leq n_{0}(j)
, implying that necessarily
\deg(v_{1})=0
and
\deg(v_{n+n^{\prime}})=2
.
Therefore, the degrees of the interaction vertices form a sequence
(0,2,0,2,\dotsc,0,2)
, or this behavior ends in two or more consecutive zeros in the middle.

(3)

Let us now consider the first phase, for any relevant graph

(4.6)

\mathrm{Re}\gamma(0;J)={}\sum_{l=1}^{2n+1}\sigma_{0,l}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,l})+\sum_{l=1}^{2n^{\prime}+1}\sigma_{0,l}^{\prime}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0,l}^{\prime})={}0,

due to the fact that the terms cancel each other pairwise because of the pairings of momenta and parities on the initial time slice.

(4)

For pairing graphs, we will drop the dependence on
J
, for the sake of simplicity.
In other words, we denote
\gamma(m)
instead of
\gamma(m;J)
.

(5)

Recall that the time slice
m<n+n^{\prime}
is called
long
if
\mathrm{deg}(v_{m+1})=0
.
The long time slice
m
is
trivial
if
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)=0
.
Note that the initial time slice in any fully paired graph is always long and trivial.

(6)

We define the
index of the last trivial long time slice
to be the last trivial long time slice in the initial sequence of trivial long time slices

(4.7)

m^{\prime}_{0}\coloneq\max\{0\leq m\leq n+n^{\prime}\mid\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j)=0\mbox{ if }0\leq j\leq m,\mbox{ and the slice $j$ is long}\}.

(7)

Let us consider a degree two interaction vertex
v
.
The two free edges associated to
v
can be denoted by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, with
e_{1}<e_{2}
.
We now denote the third (integrated) edge in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v)
by
e_{3}
and the unique edge in
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v)
by
e_{0}
.
The momenta associated to
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
and
e_{0}
are denoted buy
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
and
k_{0}
.
It is then clear that
k_{3}=k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}
.
The two edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
generate two loops.
The directions of these loops start from
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
and end at
e_{3}
.
Following the two directions, there is a unique vertex
v^{\prime}
where the two loops meet.
We call it the
X-vertex
, or the
crossing-vertex
associated to the double loop of
v
.
By construction,
v^{\prime}
must be attached to at least three distinct integrated edges, so it must be a degree zero vertex (since there are no degree one vertices in fully paired graphs).
Any other vertices along the two loops are called
T
-vertices (or
through-vertices
) of the double loop of
v
, and they are classified into three types:

•

A
T_{1}
-vertex belongs to the path from
v\to v^{\prime}
containing the edge
e_{1}
.

•

A
T_{2}
-vertex belongs to the path from
v\to v^{\prime}
containing the edge
e_{2}
.

•

A
T_{3}
-vertex belongs to the path from
v^{\prime}\to v
.

The reason for this naming convention of the
T
-vertices is the following:
If
w
is a
T_{j}
-vertex,
j=1,2,3
, then there are exactly two momenta
k_{e}
,
e\in\mathscr{E}(w)
such that
k_{e}
depends on
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, and the dependence is of the type
\pm k_{j}
.
This is a consequence of
Lemma

4.3
combined with
Lemma

4.5
.

(8)

For an interaction vertex
v_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n+n^{\prime}
, we define

(4.8)

\Theta_{j}\coloneq{}\Theta(k_{\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{j})},\sigma_{+}(v_{j})).

Now, let us recall that
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(0)=0
, which means, for every time slice
j
of a fully paired graph
0\leq j\leq n+n^{\prime}
, the following holds true

(4.9)

\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j)={}\sum_{l=j+1}^{n+n^{\prime}}\Theta_{l}={}-\sum_{l=1}^{j}\Theta_{l}.

(9)

Let
m
be an arbitrary long time slice,
0\leq m\leq n+n^{\prime}-1
.
This long time slice ends in the interaction vertex
v_{m+1}
with
\deg(v_{m+1})=0
.
Consider also an arbitrary vertex
v_{j_{2}}
with
\mathrm{deg}(v_{j_{2}})=2
, and denote the two free edges of
v_{j_{2}}
by
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

(ix.1)

If
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)
does not depend on
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, the time slice
m
is said to be
independent of the double loop of
v_{j_{2}}
.

(ix.2)

If
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)
depends on
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
but
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)-\Theta_{j_{2}}
does not, the time slice
m
is said to be
nested inside the double loop of
v_{j_{2}}
.

(ix.3)

If both
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)
and
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)-\Theta_{j_{2}}
depend on
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, the time slice
m
is said to
propagate a crossing with the double loop of
v_{j_{2}}
.

We can now give the following definition which allows for complete classification of the relevant fully paired graphs.
Recall from
Section

3.3.2
that a graph is called
leading
if it can be obtained by starting from the trivial loop and then iteratively attaching leading motives to the bottom of the graph.

Definition
.

We consider a relevant fully paired graph and iterate through its degree two vertices starting from the bottom of the graph, and consider the double loops associated with the vertices.
There will be the following cases.

(1)

If all long time slices of the graph are independent of the double loop, we move to the next vertex in the list.

(2)

If there are long time slices depending on the double loop and if all of them are nested inside the loop, then the double loop is called a
nest
and the graph is called
nested
(see Figure
10
).

Figure 10.
An example of a nested graph.
This graph is constructed by inserting the leading motive L1 into the edge that connects the two vertices in the motive L5.
It is easily checked that
\Theta_{1}=-\Theta_{4}
and
\Theta_{2}=-\Theta_{3}
, and thus the time slice
1
is nested in the double loop of
v_{4}
.

(3)

If there is a long time slice depending on the double loop but not nested inside it, the topmost of these time slices is called the
last propagated crossing slice
and the graph is called
crossing
(see Figure
11
).

Figure 11.
An example of a crossing graph.
Denoting the free momenta of
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
by
k_{1},k_{2}
and
k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}
, respectively, it is easily checked that
\gamma(1)=-\Theta_{1}=\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2}^{\prime})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})+\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{1}^{\prime})-\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1}-k_{1}^{\prime}-k_{2}^{\prime})\neq\Theta_{3}
, meaning that the time slice
1
propagates a crossing with the double loop of
v_{3}
.

It turns out (see
Lemma

4.15
below), that the initial sequence of double loops satisfying
\mathrm{(i)}
above are formed by iteration of leading motives (or immediate recollisions).
Thus, if a graph is not nested or crossing, it will be a leading graph (see
Proposition

4.16
below).
A posteriori, another way of phrasing the definition above is thus that if a graph is not leading, we can consider the first degree two vertex from the bottom that does not correspond to an immediate recollision.
Then either there is a long time slice propagating a crossing with the double loop of this vertex (and the graph is crossing), or the double loop is a nest (and the graph is nested).
Note also that these definitions only concern the first degree two vertex in the graph that is not part of an immediate recollision; a nested graph may have crossings with degree two vertices above the initial nest, and a crossing graph may have nests above the initial crossing vertex.

Before providing the proof of this classification of the fully paired graphs, we need to know that the dispersion relation is sufficiently non-degenerate.
The lemma below is taken from
[
13
]
.
The proof holds without change for the modified dispersion relation, since the only needed input is that a function satisfying
(DR2)
cannot be constant.
However, we recall the proof here since it will be instructive for our treatment of the crossing and nested graphs later.

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose that the modified dispersion relation
\omega^{\lambda}
satisfies
(DR2)
, and let
v_{j_{0}}
be an interaction vertex in a relevant graph.
Suppose that
f\in\mathscr{F}_{e}
for some
e\in\mathscr{E}(v_{j_{0}})
, i.e.,
k_{e}
depends on
k_{f}
.
The following hold true.

(1)

\nabla_{k_{f}}\Theta_{j_{0}}\neq 0
.

(2)

\Theta_{j_{0}}
cannot be independent of all free momenta.

Proof.

Suppose on the contrary that
\Theta_{j_{0}}
is a constant in
k_{f}
.
We denote the momenta associated to
v_{j_{0}}
by
k_{0},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
and the edges by
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
, respectively.
For the sake of simplicity, we also denote
\mathscr{F}_{i}=\mathscr{F}_{e_{i}}
.
By
Lemma

4.3
and
Corollary

4.4
, each
k_{i}
depends on some free momenta and
\mathscr{F}_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

Recall that any given edge can depend on the free edge
f
only if it is contained in the unique loop defined by
f
in the spanning tree of the graph.
This implies that
f
can appear in zero or exactly two of the four sets
\mathscr{F}_{0},\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{2},\mathscr{F}_{3}
.
Thus, there are unique
j,l
such that
f\in\mathscr{F}_{j}\cap\mathscr{F}_{l}
, and we denote the other two indices by
j^{\prime},l^{\prime}
.
It follows that
k_{j}=\pm(k_{f}+u)
and
k_{l}=\pm(k_{f}+u^{\prime})
, where
u
,
u^{\prime}
are linear combinations of the remaining free momenta, by
Lemma

4.3
.

Since
\nabla_{k_{f}}\Theta_{j_{0}}=0
, it follows that
\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{f})\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{f}+u^{\prime}-u)=0
for all
k_{f}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and any value of
u^{\prime}-u
.
If
u^{\prime}-u
is not identically zero, then it depends on some other free momentum which is not
k_{f}
, so by differentiating with respect to this free momentum, it follows that the Hessian of
\omega^{\lambda}
is uniformly zero.
Then
\omega^{\lambda}
has to be a linear map which is also periodic, implying that
\omega^{\lambda}
is a constant.
This is however not possible since
\omega^{\lambda}
satisfies
(DR2)
, so it follows that
u^{\prime}=u
.
Therefore,
k_{l}=\pm k_{j}
.
Recalling that
k_{0}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
, we have two cases:

Case 1:
k_{l}=k_{j}
.

If either
l
or
j
is
0
, we then have
k_{l^{\prime}}+k_{j^{\prime}}=0
, where
l^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{1,2,3\}
are the remaining indices, so in this case, the graph would be irrelevant by
Remark

4.11
.
If both
l
and
j
are different from
0
, we have for instance
k_{0}=k_{j^{\prime}}+k_{j}+k_{l}=k_{j^{\prime}}\pm 2(k_{f}+u)
, where
k_{j^{\prime}}
and
u
are both independent of
k_{f}
.
This contradicts the form of the presentation (
4.5
).

Case 2:
k_{l}+k_{j}=0
.

If one of
l,j
is
0
, say
j=0
then
k_{l^{\prime}}+k_{j^{\prime}}=2k_{j}=\pm 2(k_{f}+u)
.
This is not possible since
k_{l^{\prime}}
and
k_{j^{\prime}}
are both independent of
k_{f}
.
Otherwise, if
l
and
j
are both non-zero, then
k_{l}+k_{j}=0
implies by
Remark

4.11
that the graph is irrelevant.

Thus our initial assumption cannot hold, and we conclude that
\nabla_{k_{f}}\Theta_{j_{0}}\neq 0
.
The second statement is an obvious corollary of this.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Consider a relevant fully paired graph and suppose that all of its long time slices are independent of double loops of the first
M
degree two vertices.
Then all the double loops of these
M
vertices are immediate recollisions.
That is, there is a graph from which the full graph can be obtained by iteratively adding
M
leading motives.

Proof.

We only need to show that the double loop of the first degree two vertex,
v_{i_{0}}
, is an immediate recollision, and then the statement follows by induction in
M
.
Denote by
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
, and
k_{0}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
the momenta of the edges connected to
v_{i_{0}}
, where
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are free.
We know that
v_{i_{0}}
cannot be the first vertex of the graph since then we would have
\deg(v_{i_{0}})=0
, so we may consider the time slice
i_{0}-2
, which is the topmost long time slice below
v_{i_{0}}
.
We will show that
v_{i_{0}}
and
v_{i_{0}-1}
along with their edges constitute a leading motive by showing the following:

(1)

The double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
passes through only two interaction vertices,
v_{i_{0}-1}
and
v_{i_{0}}
.

(2)

The ”incoming” momentum is preserved, i.e., the momenta of the edges in
\mathscr{E}(v_{i_{0}-1})
correspond exactly to the momenta of the edges in
\mathscr{E}(v_{i_{0}})
, up to a sign.

(3)

The double loop preserves the phase,
\Theta_{i_{0}-1}=-\Theta_{i_{0}}
.

Then, since
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-2)
is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
, we immediately get from (
4.9
) that

(4.10)

\Theta_{i_{0}-1}+\Theta_{i_{0}}=\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-2)-\sum_{j=i_{0}+1}^{N}\Theta_{j}

is also independent of the double loop, since each term
\Theta_{j}
is independent for
j>i_{0}
, by construction of the spanning tree of the graph.
It follows that
v_{i_{0}-1}
must be the
X
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
.
Indeed, assuming for contradiction that this is not the case, then
v_{i_{0}-1}
is a
T_{n}
-vertex, meaning that

\Theta_{i_{0}-1}={}\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n}+u)\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{n}+u^{\prime})+\alpha^{\prime},

for some
u,u^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}
that do not depend on the free momenta of
v_{i_{0}}
.
If for instance
n=1
,
\Theta_{i_{0}-1}
is independent of
k_{2}
, so we differentiate with respect to
k_{2}
to obtain

0={}\nabla_{k_{2}}(\Theta_{i_{0}-1}+\Theta_{i_{0}})={}\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{3})\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})={}\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2}),

which holds for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, implying that
\omega^{\lambda}
has to be constant, which is incompatible with
(DR2)
.
It follows similarly that
v_{i_{0}-1}
cannot be a
T_{2}
-vertex.
If
v_{i_{0}-1}
is a
T_{3}
-vertex, we get by differentiating
\Theta_{i_{0}-1}+\Theta_{i_{0}}
with respect to
k_{1}
,

0={}\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}+u)\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}+u^{\prime})\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1}),

in which case
\omega^{\lambda}
would also have to be constant.
The only remaining possibility is that
v_{i_{0}-1}
is the
X
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
.

Next we note that since
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j)
is independent of
k_{1},k_{2}
for all
j<i_{0}-1
by assumption, we have for all remaining time slices
j<i_{0}-2
that

\Theta_{j}=\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j-1)-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j)

is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
.
Supposing for the moment that
v_{j}
is a
T
-vertex of the double loop, then exactly two edges of
v_{j}
depend on at least one of the free momenta of
v_{i_{0}}
, say
k_{1}
.
It then follows from
Lemma

4.14
that
\nabla_{k_{1}}\Theta_{j}\neq 0
, contradicting the fact that
\Theta_{j}
is independent of the double loop.
Thus we conclude that
v_{j}
is not a
T
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
implying the first statement (i) above, since it also cannot be the
X
-vertex.

Let us consider now the loops created by the free edges of
v_{i_{0}}
in the spanning tree.
Each of the loops start at
v_{i_{0}}
, goes down along a free edge, and passes through
v_{i_{0}-1}
before returning to
v_{i_{0}}
along the integrated edge in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
, possibly going through some cluster vertices along the way.
There are two cases:

Case 1
:
If neither of the edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
is connected directly to
v_{i_{0}-1}
, then they must each be paired to the edges of
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}-1})
through a cluster vertex at the bottom of the graph.
In this case, the momentum
k_{0}^{\prime}
of the unique edge in
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{i_{0}-1})
satisfies
k_{0}^{\prime}=-k_{0}
, so we conclude that each of the momenta of the edges in
\mathscr{E}(v_{i_{0}-1})
is exactly equal to (minus) a momentum of a unique edge in
\mathscr{E}(v_{i_{0}})
.
Since the pairings have to respect the parities of the edges, we find that
\Theta_{i_{0}-1}=-\Theta_{i_{0}}
is the only possible option, and the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
thus corresponds to a gain motive (recall the leading motives in
Fig.

5
).
We may cut the motive out of the graph by cutting each of the edges corresponding to
k_{0}^{\prime}
and
k_{0}
, and afterwards extending these edges to the bottom of the graph and connecting them via a pairing.

Case 2
: If one of the edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
is equal to the unique edge in
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{i_{0}-1})
, then the two remaining edges of
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
must be paired to edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}-1})
through clusters.
Thus we conclude that we can label the edges
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}-1})=\{e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime}\}
and
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{j_{0}})=\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
such that the corresponding momenta satisfy
k_{e_{1}}=-k_{e_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}
,
k_{e_{2}}=-k_{e_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}
,
and
k_{e_{3}}=k_{e_{0}^{\prime}}^{\prime}
, where
e_{0}^{\prime}
is the unique edge in
\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{i_{0}-1})
.
It follows that the final momentum satisfies
k_{e_{3}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=k_{e_{0}^{\prime}}^{\prime}-k_{e_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}-k_{e_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=k_{e_{1}}+k_{e_{2}}+k_{e_{3}}=k_{e_{0}}=k_{0}
, and that the parities of the edges
e_{3}^{\prime}
and
e_{0}
have to be equal.
Consequently, we must have
\Theta_{i_{0}-1}=-\Theta_{i_{0}}
, and the double loop of
v_{i_{0}}
corresponds to a loss motive.
We can cut the motive out of the graph by cutting the edges
e_{0}
and
e_{3}^{\prime}
, and then repairing the graph by gluing the two edges together.

We have thus proved the lemma for
M=1
.
Assume now that the lemma is true for
M-1
for all relevant fully paired graphs, and consider a graph where all long time slices are independent of the double loops of the first
M
degree two vertices.
By what we have just shown, the double loop of the first degree two vertex is an immediate recollision, so we can cut it out of the graph as described above.
Since immediate recollisions preserve the phase, the resulting graph then satisfies that all long time slices are independent of the double loops of the first
M-1
degree two vertices, which concludes the proof of the lemma by induction.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

Consider a fully paired graph with non-zero associated amplitude and suppose that it is not nested or crossing.
Then all of its long time slices are trivial, and the graph is leading.
In general, all relevant fully paired graphs, in which all long time slices are trivial, are leading.

Proof.

From the assumption, the graph is relevant and all of its long time slices are independent of all of the double loops.

Lemma

4.15
implies that the graph is leading, and all long time slices are trivial since immediate recollisions preserve the phase, which is initially
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(0)=0
.

Conversely, if all long time slices are trivial, they satisfy
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)=0
independently of all free momenta, and thus by
Lemma

4.15
the graph is leading.
∎

Nested and crossing graphs

Before concluding this section, we state and prove the following structural lemmas which will be useful later for analysing the phase factors of nested and crossing graphs.
The assertions of the lemmas are already contained in the proofs of the amplitude bounds for nested and crossing graphs in the setting of the weakly non-linear Schrödinger equation
[
13
]
, but for the sake of exposition and later reference, we state the results separately here and flesh out some details of the proofs.
We provide a slightly different exposition than in
[
13
]
that does not rely as heavily on the iterative cluster scheme.

Lemma 4.17

(Nested graphs)
.

Consider a relevant nested graph and let
i_{2}
be the index of the first interaction vertex (from the bottom) which is not an immediate recollision.
Further, let

N_{2}\coloneq\{m\mid\text{The time slice $m$ is long and nested inside the double loop of $v_{i_{2}}$}\},

and denote
j_{0}\coloneq\min N_{2}
.
Then the following hold true:

(1)

j_{0}
is not the initial time slice, and the vertex
v_{j_{0}}
siting at the bottom of the time slice
j_{0}
is the
X
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
In particular,
j_{0}-1
and
j_{0}
are both long.

(2)

The phase factor
\Theta_{j_{0}}+\Theta_{i_{2}}
is independent of the double loops of all degree two vertices up to
v_{i_{2}}
.

(3)

For any other degree zero vertex
v_{j}
below
v_{i_{2}}
which is
not
the degree zero vertex of an immediate recollision,
\Theta_{j}
is independent of the double loops of all degree two vertices up to
v_{i_{2}}
.
In particular,
v_{j}
cannot be a
T
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.

(4)

The double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
corresponds to that of a leading motive, but with a
delayed
recollision.
In particular,
\Theta_{j_{0}}=-\Theta_{i_{2}}
.

Proof.

Recall first that by definition of nested graphs, the vertex
v_{i_{2}}
exists, and there is at least one long time slice that is nested inside its double loop, and any long time slice that depends on the double loop is nested inside it.
If we denote by
k_{1},k_{2}
the free momenta of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
, then
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0})
depends on
k_{1},k_{2}
, but
\mathrm{Re}(j_{0})-\Theta_{i_{2}}
does not.
Further, we have
j_{0}<i_{2}-1
because
i_{2}-1
by definition is not a long time slice, and no time slice above
i_{2}-1
can depend on the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.

Note now that the initial time slice does not depend on
k_{1},k_{2}
since
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(0)=0
, so
j_{0}>0
and hence
j_{0}-1
is the index of a time slice.
Assuming for contradiction that
j_{0}-1
is a short time slice, then
\deg v_{j_{0}}=2
, so
v_{j_{0}}
cannot be the bottom interaction vertex of the graph.
Hence
j_{0}-1>0
, and it must belong to an immediate recollision by definition of
v_{i_{2}}
, which implies that
j_{0}-2
must be a long time slice, and
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0})=\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0}-2)
.
It follows that
j_{0}-2
is also nested inside the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
which is in contradiction with the choice of
j_{0}
.
Thus we conclude that
j_{0}-1
is a long time slice that is not nested inside the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
, and therefore it must be independent of the double loop (simply because the double loop does not have any crossings).

Note now that since
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0})
is nested inside the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
,
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0})-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0}-1)=-\Theta_{j_{0}}
must depend on the double loop, and

\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0})-\Theta_{i_{2}}-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0}-1)=-(\Theta_{j_{0}}+\Theta_{i_{2}})

must be independent of the double loop.
By the exact same argument as the one starting from (
4.10
) in the proof of
Lemma

4.15
, we conclude that
v_{j_{0}}
must be the
X
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
, which finishes the proof of
(i)
.

Consider the set of degree zero vertices below
v_{i_{2}}
that do not belong to an immediate recollision.
Since any degree two vertex below
v_{i_{2}}
by definition belongs to an immediate recollision, the set of indices of these vertices is

(4.11)

B_{0}^{\prime}\coloneq\{1\leq j<i_{2}\mid\deg v_{j}=0,\text{ and }\deg v_{j+1}=0\text{ or }j+1=i_{2}\}.

Since both
j_{0}-1
and
j_{0}
are long time slices, we have
j_{0}\in B_{0}^{\prime}
.
Fix now a degree two vertex
v_{j_{2}}
with
j_{2}<i_{2}
, and a
j\in B_{0}^{\prime}
.
Then either
j=i_{2}-1
, in which case
\Theta_{j}
is independent of the double loop of
v_{j_{2}}
(because
v_{j}
is above
v_{j_{2}}
in the graph), or
\deg v_{j+1}=0
.
For the latter case, we note that every long time slice is independent of the double loop of
v_{j_{2}}
, by definition of
i_{2}
.
In particular,
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j)-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j-1)=-\Theta_{j}
is independent of this double loop.
Taking
j=j_{0}
concludes the proof of
(ii)
.

To finish the proof of
(iii)
, we still need to argue that
\Theta_{j}
is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
for
j\in B_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{j_{0}\}
.
By definition of
j_{0}
, this is automatically true for any
j\in B_{0}^{\prime}
with
j<j_{0}
.
Writing
B_{0}^{\prime}\cap\{i\geq j_{0}\}=\{j_{0},j_{1},\dotsc,j_{m}\}
, we assume for induction for some
1\leq k\leq m
that
\Theta_{j_{i}}
is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
.
Since for each such
i
, the vertices between
v_{j_{i-1}}
and
v_{j_{i}}
all belong to immediate recollisions, we can remove the phase factors of these recollisions and obtain

(4.12)

\Theta_{j_{k}}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{k})-\Theta_{i_{2}}={}\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{0})-\Theta_{i_{2}}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\Theta_{j_{i}},

where the right hand side is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
It follows that the two terms
\Theta_{j_{k}}
and
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{k})-\Theta_{i_{2}}
are either both dependent or both independent of the double loop.
Assuming for contradiction that they depend on the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
, we infer that
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{k})
must be independent, since otherwise the time slice
j_{k}
would propagate a crossing, which cannot happen because the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
is a nest.
From (
4.12
) we may then conclude that
\Theta_{j_{k}}-\Theta_{i_{2}}
is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
However, since
v_{j_{k}}
must then be a
T
-vertex of the double loop, this is not possible by the same argument we used to show that
v_{j_{0}}
is the
X
-vertex.
Hence we conclude that
\Theta_{j_{k}}
is independent of the double loop, and further by
Lemma

4.14
that
v_{j_{k}}
cannot be a
T
-vertex, finishing the proof of
(iii)
.

Finally, to see that
\emph{(iv)}
holds, we note now that by
(iii)
, the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
only goes through
v_{j_{0}}
and immediate recollisions.
Since these preserve the phase and the incoming momentum, the same argumentation as in the proof of
Lemma

4.15
shows that the momenta of
v_{j_{0}}
and
v_{i_{2}}
are pairwise equal, and that
\Theta_{j_{0}}=-\Theta_{i_{2}}
.
Hence, double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
corresponds to that of a leading motive, up to the presence of the immediate recollisions.
(Alternatively, one may cut all the immediate recollisions below
v_{i_{2}}
out of the graph to obtain a second graph, which by
(iii)
satisfies that all its long time slices are independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
).
∎

Lemma 4.18

(Crossing graphs)
.

Consider a relevant crossing graph and let
i_{2}
be the index of the first interaction vertex (from the bottom) which is not an immediate recollision.
Further, let
i_{0}-1
be the index of the last propagated crossing slice.
The following properties hold:

(1)

There exist a
p\in\{0,1\}
and an
\alpha_{1}
which is independent of the double loop of any
v_{j}
,
j\leq i_{2}
, such that

(4.13)

\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)=p\Theta_{i_{2}}+\Theta_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{1}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2}).

Further,
v_{i_{0}}
is a vertex in the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
(and thus either a
T
- or the
X
-vertex), and
\Theta_{i_{0}}
is independent of any free momenta ending before
v_{i_{2}}
.

(2)

If
v_{i_{0}}
is a
T_{j}
-vertex, then

(4.14)

\Theta_{i_{0}}=\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u)\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u^{\prime})+\alpha^{\prime}

for some choice of signs, where
\alpha^{\prime},u^{\prime},u
are independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
Further,
u^{\prime}-u
is not identically zero; it depends on some free momenta.

(3)

If
v_{i_{0}}
is the
X
-vertex, then

(4.15)

\Theta_{i_{0}}=\alpha^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u_{j})

for some choice of signs, where
\alpha^{\prime},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
are independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
In this case,
p=1
in (
4.13
), and the
u_{j}
cannot all be identically zero; at least one of them depends on some free momenta ending above
v_{i_{2}}
.

Proof.

First, we rewrite

(4.16)

\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)=\Theta_{i_{2}}+\Theta_{i_{0}}+a_{1}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2}),

where

(4.17)

a_{1}=\sum_{j=i_{0}+1}^{i_{2}-1}\Theta_{j}.

By construction,
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})
does not depend on the edges that are associated to a lower ranked vertex, and hence it does not depend on
k_{1},k_{2}
, nor on any double loop of
v_{j}
with
j\leq i_{2}
.
Our goal now is to prove that we can choose
p\in\{0,1\}
such that

\alpha_{1}\coloneq a_{1}+(1-p)\Theta_{i_{2}},

is also independent of any such double loop.
It is then obvious that (
4.13
) holds.

Now, by
Lemma

4.15
and definition of
i_{2}
, for any
j<i_{2}
with
\deg(v_{j})=2
, the double loop of
v_{j}
is determined by a leading motive, and
\Theta_{j-1}=-\Theta_{j}
.
Now, if
j\geq i_{0}+2
, the terms cancel each other in the sum (
4.17
).
Assuming for the moment that
\deg(v_{i_{0}+1})=2
, then
\Theta_{i_{0}}=-\Theta_{i_{0}+1}
and
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)=\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}+1)
.
This implies that
i_{0}+1
must be a short time slice, since otherwise also
i_{0}+1
propagates a crossing with
v_{i_{2}}
, which is not allowed by definition of
i_{0}-1
.
This means that
\deg(v_{i_{0}+1})=\deg(v_{i_{0}+2})=2
, so
v_{i_{0}+2}
cannot be an immediate recollision, and we conclude that necessarily
i_{0}+2=i_{2}
.
However, in this case
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)=\Theta_{i_{2}}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})
, and thus
i_{0}-1
does not propagate a crossing.
Hence we conclude that we always have
\deg(v_{i_{0}+1})=0
, which implies that the sum (
4.17
) cannot contain the phase of just one of the vertices of any given immediate recollision.

It follows that if we consider the set of degree zero vertices between
v_{i_{0}}
and
v_{i_{2}}
that do not belong to an immediate recollision,

I^{\prime}\coloneq\{i_{0}+1\leq j\leq i_{2}-1\mid\deg(v_{j})=0,\text{ and }\deg(v_{j+1})=0\mbox{ or }j+1=i_{2}\},

then
a_{1}=\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}\Theta_{j}
.
As a result,
a_{1}
is independent of all of the double loops of
v_{j}
with
j<i_{2}
.
If
I^{\prime}=\emptyset
, then
a_{1}=0
and the claim holds for
p=1
.
If
I\neq\emptyset
, we denote
j^{\prime}=\min I^{\prime}
.
Clearly,
\mathrm{deg}(v_{j^{\prime}})=0
and the time slice
j^{\prime}-1>i_{0}-1
is long, with
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j^{\prime}-1)=\Theta_{i_{2}}+a_{1}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})
.
In view of the fact that this time slice cannot propagate a crossing, either
a_{1}
or
a_{1}+\Theta_{i_{2}}
is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
For the first case, we take
p=1
and
\alpha_{1}=a_{1}
.
For the second case, we take
p=0
and
\alpha_{1}=a_{1}+\Theta_{i_{2}}
.
This finishes the proof of (
4.13
).

To conclude the proof of
(i)
, note now that if
v_{i_{0}}
is not a vertex in the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
, then
\Theta_{i_{0}}
is independent of the double loop.
By (
4.13
), this would imply that either
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)
or
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)-\Theta_{i_{2}}
is independent of the double loop, contradicting the fact that
i_{0}-1
propagates a crossing.
Finally, recalling that all long time slices are independent of the double loops of any
v_{j}
with
j<i_{2}
(since these are immediate recollisions), it follows that also
\Theta_{i_{0}}=\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0}-1)-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{0})
is independent of these loops, since we have shown that
i_{0}
is a long time slice.

Now, for points
(ii)
and
(iii)
, we note that the equations (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) always hold for any
T_{j}
- and
X
-vertices, respectively.
We only need to show the statements about the additional momenta appearing in the formulas.

If
v_{i_{0}}
is a
T_{j}
-vertex, the
j
-part of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
goes through
v_{i_{0}}
via two edges
e,e^{\prime}
.
If both
e,e^{\prime}
are in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
, then
k_{e}=\sigma(k_{j}+u)
and
k_{e^{\prime}}=-\sigma(k_{j}+u^{\prime})
for some choice of
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
, implying that
u^{\prime}-u=-\sigma(k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}})
.
Otherwise, the loop uses
\tilde{e}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
and
\tilde{e}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{i_{0}})
,
k_{\tilde{e}}=\sigma(k_{j}+u)
and
k_{\tilde{e}}=\sigma(k_{j}+u^{\prime})
, leading to
u^{\prime}-u=\sigma(k_{\tilde{e}^{\prime}}-k_{\tilde{e}})=\sigma(k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}})
with
e,e^{\prime}
being the remaining two edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{i_{0}})
.
Now, if
u^{\prime}-u
is independent of all free momenta, then
Lemma

4.8
implies that
k_{e}+k_{e^{\prime}}=0
, meaning that the graph is irrelevant.

Assume finally that
v_{i_{0}}
is the
X
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
We show first by induction that all of the
\Theta_{j}
with
j\in I^{\prime}
are independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
Write
I^{\prime}=\{j_{1},\dotsc,j_{m}\}
and assume for some
1\leq k\leq m
that
\Theta_{k+1},\dotsc,\Theta_{j_{m}}
are all independent of the double loop, and note that

\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{k}-1)=\Theta_{i_{2}}+\Theta_{j_{k}}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})+\sum_{i=k+1}^{m}\Theta_{j_{i}}.

Assuming for contradiction that
\Theta_{j_{k}}
depends on the double loop, then
v_{j_{k}}
must be a
T
-vertex, and
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(j_{k}-1)
must be independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
since the long time slice
j_{k}-1
cannot propagate a crossing.
If
v_{j_{k}}
is a
T_{1}
- or a
T_{2}
- vertex, then differentiating the above equation with respect to the free momentum of
v_{i_{2}}
that
\Theta_{j_{k}}
does not depend on, it follows that the gradient of
\omega^{\lambda}
is constant, which is incompatible with
(DR2)
.
On the other hand, if
v_{j_{k}}
is a
T_{3}
-vertex, then
\Theta_{j_{k}}
is of the form (
4.14
), in which case we have
