\nabla_{k_{1}}\Theta_{i_{2}}=-\nabla_{k_{1}}\Theta_{j_{k}}=-\nabla_{k_{2}}\Theta_{j_{k}}=\nabla_{k_{2}}\Theta_{i_{2}}.

This in turn implies that
\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1})=\pm\nabla\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})
for all
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, so
\nabla\omega^{\lambda}
is again a constant.
We conclude that
\Theta_{j}
cannot depend on
k_{1},k_{2}
for any
j\in I^{\prime}
, from which it follows that
p=1
in (
4.13
).

Suppose now for contradiction that
u_{1}=u_{2}=u_{3}=0
.
We will show that the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
is then actually a nest, and the graph is nested.
In this case, the last term in (
4.15
) must be equal to
\alpha^{\prime}=\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0})
, and one of the edges
e_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}(v_{i_{0}})
has corresponding momentum
k_{e_{0}^{\prime}}=\pm k_{0}
.
Denote by
e_{0}\in\mathscr{E}_{+}(v_{i_{2}})
the edge with momentum
k_{e_{0}}=k_{0}
.
Since the graph is relevant, it is then apparent from
Lemma

4.7
and
Lemma

4.6
that
k_{e_{0}}
and
k_{e_{0}^{\prime}}
depend on the same set of free edges, and that removal of
e_{0}
and
e_{0}^{\prime}
from the graph splits the graph into two connected components.
Note that one of these components contains the entire double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
, and that this component has the structure of a Feynman diagram.
In particular, the edges
e_{0}
and
e_{0}^{\prime}
must have the same parity, since the sum of parities in each time slice remains constant throughout any diagram.
Thus, we may cut the connected component containing the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
out of the graph, and then attach it to the trivial loop to obtain another momentum graph.
Since this operation preserves the graph structure (including vertex degrees and the way each edge depends on free momenta), the resulting graph is fully paired, implying
n_{2}=n_{0}
, and all degree two vertices, except for
v_{i_{2}}
, are immediate recollisions.
This implies that the connected component of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
contains only a single degree zero vertex that does not correspond to an immediate recollision, which must then be
v_{i_{0}}
.
Denoting the indices of this set of vertices by
B_{0}^{\prime}
, as in (
4.11
), we have shown that
\Theta_{j}
is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
for each
j\in B_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{i_{0}\}
.
Finally, considering
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})
and removing all immediate recollisions from this phase, we conclude that

\Theta_{i_{0}}+\Theta_{i_{2}}=-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})-\sum_{j\in B_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{i_{0}\}}\Theta_{j}

is independent of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
However, since
p=1
in (
4.13
), it follows that the time slice
i_{0}-1
is actually nested in the double loop, and that the graph is nested.
∎

5.
Key parameters and main lemmata

In this section, we specify our choices of parameters in the Duhamel expansion, and provide an example of a dispersion relation
\omega
and interaction
V
satisfying the assumptions of
Section

2.1
.
Further, we also collect some main technical results that are needed to treat the terms in the expansion.

5.1.
Key parameters

We take all the same parameters as in
[
13
]
, but we recall them here for convenience, and motivate the definitions.

Definition

(Key parameters)
.

Let
\delta
be the constant defined in
(DR2)
and
\gamma
be the constant defined in
(DR4)
.
We define the parameters

(5.1)

b=\frac{3}{4},\quad\gamma^{\prime}=\min\Big(\frac{1}{4},2\gamma,2\delta\Big),\quad a_{0}=\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{24},\quad\mbox{ and }b_{0}=16\Big(3+\frac{1}{a_{0}}\Big).

Moreover, we also need

(5.2)

\varepsilon=\lambda^{2}\mbox{ and }N_{0}(\lambda)=\max\bigg(1,\bigg\lfloor\frac{a_{0}\lvert\ln\lambda\rvert}{\ln\langle\ln\lambda\rangle}\bigg\rfloor\bigg),

where
\lfloor x\rfloor
is the integer part of
x\geq 0
.
For
N_{0}=N_{0}(\lambda)
, we also define

(5.3)

\kappa^{\prime}(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}N_{0}^{b_{0}},

and take the sequence
\kappa=\kappa(\lambda)
to be

(5.4)

\kappa_{n}(\lambda)={}\begin{cases}0&0\leq n<N_{0}/2,\\
\kappa^{\prime}(\lambda)&N_{0}/2\leq n\leq N_{0}.\end{cases}

It is immediately apparent that this choice of parameters satisfies
N_{0}\to\infty
,
\max_{n}\kappa_{n}\to 0
, and

(5.5)

\frac{N_{0}\ln\langle\ln\lambda\rangle}{\lvert\ln\lambda\rvert}\to a_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{N_{0}\ln N_{0}}{\lvert\ln\lambda\rvert}\to a_{0},

as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
.

The reason for making the specific choice (
5.2
) of
N_{0}(\lambda)
is that we will need to control expressions of the type

(5.6)

c^{N_{0}}\lambda^{p_{1}}N_{0}^{p_{2}N_{0}+c^{\prime}}((n_{1}N_{0})!)^{n_{2}}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{n_{3}N_{0}+c^{\prime}}\to 0\qquad\text{when}\qquad\lambda\to 0^{+},

for specific values of
c,c^{\prime}\geq 0
,
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, compare e.g. with the results in
Section

6
below.
Indeed, (
5.6
) holds true whenever
p_{1}-a_{0}(p_{2}+n_{1}n_{2}+n_{3})>0
, with the decay going like a power of
\lambda
.
To see this, simply note for
\lambda<1
that we may bound
\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{n_{3}N_{0}}\lesssim\lambda^{-a_{0}n_{3}}
and
N_{0}^{(p_{2}+n_{1}n_{2})N_{0}}\lesssim\lambda^{-a_{0}(p_{2}+n_{1}n_{2})}
, by (
5.5
).
Hence, using
n!\leq n^{n}
, we conclude that (
5.6
) is bounded by

(cn_{1}^{n_{1}n_{2}})^{N_{0}}(N_{0}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle)^{c^{\prime}}\lambda^{p_{1}-a_{0}(p_{2}+n_{1}n_{2}+n_{3})}\leq{}C_{\alpha}\lambda^{-\alpha}(\langle\ln\lambda\rangle)^{2c^{\prime}}\lambda^{p_{1}-a_{0}(p_{2}+n_{1}n_{2}+n_{3})},

for any
\alpha>0
, and the assertion follows.
For instance, we obtain decay up to
\lambda^{2}
in

c^{N_{0}}\lambda^{-2}N_{0}^{-\frac{b_{0}}{4}N_{0}+4N_{0}+c^{\prime}}(4N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{4N_{0}+c^{\prime}}\to 0,

and decay up to
\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}/2}
in

c^{N_{0}}\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}N_{0}^{4N_{0}+c^{\prime}}(4N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{4N_{0}+c^{\prime}}\to 0.

Finally, the advantage of choosing the sequence
\kappa
as in (
5.4
) is twofold:
The presence of the non-zero entries in the second half of the sequence allows us to obtain a little extra decay in
\lambda
for amplitudes of graphs that are large (compared to
N_{0}
).
This is mainly useful for controlling the error terms.
On the other hand, since the rest of the entries of
\kappa
vanish, the sequence does not even appear in the amplitudes of graphs that are small compared to
N_{0}
.
This will be useful when we compute the limit of the leading diagrams in
Section

7
.

5.2.
From phases to resolvents, cluster combinatorics, and integrals over free momenta

We now recall the following technical lemmas, whose proofs can be found in
[
13
, Section 7]
.

Lemma 5.1

(From phases to resolvents)
.

Let
I
be a non-empty finite index set and
t>0
.
Suppose that
A
is a non-empty subset of
I
,
\gamma_{j}\in D
,
j\in I
, with
D
being a compact subset of
\mathbb{C}
.
We choose an additional time index label
J
, i.e. suppose
J\notin I
with
\gamma_{J}\in D
, and denote
A^{c}=I\setminus A
,
A^{\prime}=A^{c}\cup\{J\}
.
Then for any path
\Gamma_{D}
going once anticlockwise around
D
, the following holds true

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j\in I}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j\in I}e^{-i\gamma_{j}s_{j}}

(5.7)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\oint_{\Gamma_{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}s\delta\bigg(t-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}s_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j\in A^{\prime}}e^{-i\gamma_{j}s_{j}}\Big|_{\gamma_{J}=z}\prod_{j\in A}\frac{i}{z-\gamma_{j}}.

Lemma 5.2

(Cluster combinatorics)
.

Suppose that
(L1)
holds.
There is a constant
c
such that for all
N>0
and
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}

\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{N})}\prod_{A\in S}\sup_{k,\sigma,\Omega}\big\lvert C_{\lvert A\rvert}(k,\sigma,\lambda,\Omega)\big\rvert\leq{}c^{N}N!

If the sum is over non-pairing
S
, the bound can be improved by a factor of
\lambda
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.2
for the classical case in
[
13
]
carries over to the
fermionic
quantum case without change.
For the non-pairing clusters,
(L1)
is the only needed input, but for the pairing clusters, the bound relies on the fact that
W_{L}^{\lambda}
is bounded, cf.
Remark

3.3
, which is not the case for bosons.

In
[
13
]
, the following three results are proved for the unmodified dispersion relation
\omega
.
However, since we require the modified dispersion relation
\omega^{\lambda}
to satisfy
(DR2)
and
(DR3)
uniformly in
\lambda
, the proofs carry over to our case without change.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose
(DR2)
holds with constants
C,\delta>0
, and assume
f\in\ell_{1}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{3})
.
Then for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
k_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
,

(5.8)

\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\,e^{is(\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma^{\prime}\omega^{\lambda}(k^{\prime})+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k-k^{\prime}))}\hat{f}(k,k^{\prime},k_{0}-k-k^{\prime})\bigg\rvert\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{1}\langle s\rangle^{-1-\delta}.

As discussed in
[
13
, Section 7.4]
,
Proposition

5.3
can be used to give rigorous meaning to the energy conservation delta functions in the collision operator (
2.14
) and (
2.16
).

Lemma 5.4

(Integrals over free momenta - degree one vertex)
.

If
(DR3)
is satisfied, then for any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lvert\beta\rvert>0
, and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
,

(5.9)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\frac{F_{1}(\sigma^{\prime}k)}{\lvert\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k)-\alpha+i\beta\rvert}\lesssim{}\lambda^{-b}\langle\ln\lvert\beta\rvert\rangle^{2}.

Here, the function
F_{1}
is the one appearing in the construction of the momentum cut-off function
\Phi_{1}
in
Proposition

3.1
.

Lemma 5.5

(Integrals over free momenta - degree two vertex)
.

Suppose that
(DR2)
and
(DR3)
are satisfied.
Then for any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lvert\beta\rvert>0
, and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
,

(5.10)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2d}}\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\frac{1}{\lvert\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma^{\prime}\omega^{\lambda}(k^{\prime})+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k-k^{\prime})-\alpha+i\beta\rvert}\lesssim{}\langle\ln\lvert\beta\rvert\rangle.

The proofs of
Lemmas

5.4
and
5.5
are based on the following formula (see e.g.
[
11
, Lemma 4.21]
), expressing a resolvent as an oscillating integral,

(5.11)

\frac{1}{\lvert r+i\beta\rvert}={}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s\,e^{isr}F(s;\beta),

for
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<\beta\leq 1
, where the function
F(s;\beta)
satisfies
0\leq F(s;\beta)\leq\langle\ln\beta\rangle e^{-\beta\lvert s\rvert}+{\mathds{1}}(\lvert s\rvert\leq 1)\ln\frac{1}{\lvert s\rvert}
.

Lemmas

5.1
,
5.4
and
5.5
will be especially useful to bound the amplitudes for graphs that are not fully paired in
Section

6
below.

5.3.
Extended Bessel functions and nearest neighbor interactions

We consider here the modified dispersion relation (
2.17
) and take a closer look at
(DR1)
,
(DR2)
,
(DR3)
and
(DR4)
from
Section

2.1
.
The goal of this section is to provide an example of
\omega
and
V
such that
\omega^{\lambda}
satisfies these assumptions.
Note first that regardless of
\omega
and
V
, the quantity
\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{2})\widehat{V}(0)
is a constant and thus will only appear as a constant phase factor in the integrals in
(DR2)
,
(DR3)
and
(DR4)
.
As a result, we only need to consider the convolution term

(5.12)

\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}(k_{1})\coloneq{}-\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}W_{L}^{\lambda}(k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}-k_{2}),

and modify the dispersion relation as

(5.13)

\widetilde{\omega}^{\lambda}\coloneq{}\omega+\lambda\widetilde{R}_{\lambda}

when checking that the assumptions hold true.
Similar as in
[
13
]
, we consider the nearest neighbour dispersion relation

(5.14)

\omega(k)\coloneq c-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\cos(2\pi k_{j}),

where the constant
c
is arbitrary, and
k_{j}
denotes the
j
’th coordinate of
k\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
We will also assume that the interaction potential is of the same form as
\omega
, i.e.,

(5.15)

\widehat{V}(k)={}\tilde{c}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\cos(2\pi k_{j}-\alpha_{j}),

for some constants
\tilde{c},\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
which will not play a role in the following.
The constants
c,\tilde{c}
have no influence on the absolute values of the integrals in
(DR2)
,
(DR3)
and
(DR4)
, so we can assume them to be zero.
Furthermore, we will also take
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{d}
to be zero in the following, for simplicity.

Lemma 5.6
.

There are constants
C,\lambda_{0}>0
such that for any
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
u_{1},u_{2}\in[0,2\pi]^{d}
, and
0\leq\lambda<\lambda_{0}
, the function
\mathcal{K}
from (
2.24
) satisfies

(5.16)

\Big\lVert\mathcal{K}\Big({}\cdot{};t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\frac{u_{1}}{2\pi},\frac{u_{2}}{2\pi}\Big)\Big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\leq C\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\langle r_{j}\rangle^{\frac{1}{7}}},

where
r_{j}=\lvert t_{0}+t_{1}e^{iu_{1,j}}+t_{2}e^{iu_{2,j}}\rvert
for
j=1,\dotsc,d
.
Furthermore, it follows that

(5.17)

\lVert p_{t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}^{3}\leq{}C\langle t\rangle^{-\frac{3d}{7}},

so that
(DR2)
is satisfied for
d\geq 3
.

Proof.

We will prove (
5.16
) by providing bounds on
\mathcal{K}
in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
and
\ell^{4}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
, and then interpolating.
Note first that we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\Big(x;t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\frac{u_{1}}{2\pi},\frac{u_{2}}{2\pi}\Big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{[0,2\pi]^{d}}\mathrm{d}p\,e^{ip\cdot x}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\Big[e^{i(t_{0}\cos(p_{j})+t_{1}\cos(p_{j}+u_{1,j})+t_{2}\cos(p_{j}+u_{2,j}))}

\displaystyle\times e^{-i\lambda\big(\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}kW_{L}^{\lambda}(k)(t_{0}\cos(p_{j}-2\pi k_{j})+t_{1}\cos(p_{j}+u_{1,j}-2\pi k_{j})+t_{2}\cos(p_{j}+u_{2,j}-2\pi k_{j}))\big)}\Big]

(5.18)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{[0,2\pi]^{d}}\mathrm{d}p\,e^{ip\cdot x}\prod_{j=1}^{d}e^{ir_{j}\big(\cos(p_{j}+\phi_{j})-\lambda\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}kW_{L}^{\lambda}(k)\cos(p_{j}+\phi_{j}-2\pi k_{j})\big)},

where
r_{j}=\lvert t_{0}+t_{1}e^{iu_{1,j}}+t_{2}e^{iu_{2,j}}\rvert
, and the vector
\phi
is independent of
p
.
The numbers
\phi_{j}
are chosen so that independently of
p_{j}\in[0,2\pi]
,

\displaystyle t_{0}\cos(p_{j})+t_{1}\cos(p_{j}+u_{1,j})+t_{2}\cos(p_{j}+u_{2,j})={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}[e^{ip_{j}}(t_{0}+t_{1}e^{iu_{1,j}}+t_{2}e^{iu_{2,j}})]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle r_{j}\cos(p_{j}+\phi_{j}).

Applying Parseval’s identity to (
5.18
) immediately implies that
\lVert\mathcal{K}\rVert_{\ell^{2}}=(2\pi)^{-d}
.
On the other hand, continuing from (
5.18
), we also have

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{K}\Big(x;t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\frac{u_{1}}{2\pi},\frac{u_{2}}{2\pi}\Big)\Big\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\int_{[0,2\pi]^{d}}\mathrm{d}p\,e^{ip\cdot x}\int_{[0,2\pi]^{d}}\mathrm{d}q\prod_{j=1}^{d}e^{ir_{j}(\cos(p_{j}+q_{j}+\phi_{j})-\cos(q_{j}+\phi_{j}))}

\displaystyle\times e^{-i\lambda r_{j}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}kW_{L}^{\lambda}(k)(\cos(p_{j}+q_{j}+\phi_{j}-2\pi k_{j})-\cos(q_{j}+\phi_{j}-2\pi k_{j}))},

so denoting

\psi_{j}(p_{j},q_{j})\coloneq{}\sin\big(\frac{p_{j}}{2}+q_{j}+\phi_{j}\big)-\lambda\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}kW_{L}^{\lambda}(k)\sin\big(\frac{p_{j}}{2}+q_{j}+\phi_{j}-2\pi k_{j}\big),

it follows, again by Parseval’s identity, that

(5.19)

\displaystyle\Big\lVert\mathcal{K}\Big({}\cdot{};t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\frac{u_{1}}{2\pi},\frac{u_{2}}{2\pi}\Big)\Big\rVert_{\ell^{4}}^{4}={}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4d}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}p_{j}\bigg\lvert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}q_{j}e^{-2ir_{j}\sin(\frac{p_{j}}{2})\psi_{j}(p_{j},q_{j})}\bigg\rvert^{2}.

We now ignore the subscript
j
and fix the
p
-variable, and note that at any critical point where
\partial_{q}\psi(p,q)=0
, we must have
\lvert\cos(\frac{p}{2}+q+\phi)\rvert\leq\lambda
, since
0\leq W_{L}^{\lambda}\leq 1
.
It follows for
\lambda
small enough, independently of
L
, that we can estimate the double derivative at critical points by
\lvert\partial_{q}^{2}\psi(p,q)\rvert\geq\lvert\sin(\frac{p}{2}+q+\phi)\rvert-\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}
, so by the stationary phase approximation (e.g.
[
7
, Proposition 2.6.7, Corollary 2.6.8]
), we conclude for
r\sin(p/2)
sufficiently large that

\bigg\lvert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}q\,e^{-2ir\sin(\frac{p}{2})\psi(p,q)}\bigg\rvert\leq{}\frac{C}{(\sin(\frac{p}{2})r)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Since the
q
-integral is also bounded by
2\pi
uniformly in
p
, we obtain for any
r\geq 0
,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}p\bigg\lvert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}q\,e^{-2ir\sin(\frac{p}{2})\psi(p,q)}\bigg\rvert^{2}\leq{}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}p\frac{C}{1+\sin(\frac{p}{2})r}\leq{}\frac{C\log(1+r)}{r}.

Using that the
p
-integral is also uniformly bounded for any
r
, we finally have

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}p\bigg\lvert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}q\,e^{-2ir\sin(\frac{p}{2})\psi(p,q)}\bigg\rvert^{2}\leq{}\frac{C}{\langle r\rangle^{\frac{6}{7}}}.

Returning to (
5.19
), we have proven that

\displaystyle\Big\lVert\mathcal{K}\Big({}\cdot{};t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},\frac{u_{1}}{2\pi},\frac{u_{2}}{2\pi}\Big)\Big\rVert_{\ell^{4}}^{4}\leq{}

\displaystyle C\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\langle r_{j}\rangle^{\frac{6}{7}}}.

Noting that
\frac{1}{3}=\frac{\theta}{4}+\frac{1-\theta}{2}
for
\theta=\frac{2}{3}
, we get by standard interpolation in
\ell^{p}
,

\lVert\mathcal{K}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\leq{}\lVert\mathcal{K}\rVert_{\ell^{4}}^{\theta}\lVert\mathcal{K}\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{1-\theta}={}\lVert\mathcal{K}\rVert_{\ell^{4}}^{\frac{2}{3}}\lVert\mathcal{K}\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq{}C\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{(\langle r_{j}\rangle^{\frac{6}{7}})^{\frac{2}{12}}}={}C\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\langle r_{j}\rangle^{\frac{1}{7}}},

finishing the proof of (
5.16
).
The bound (
5.17
) follows immediately, noting that
p_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=\mathcal{K}(x;t,0,0,0,0)
.
∎

Lemma 5.7

(Constructive interference)
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2
.
Let
M^{\mathrm{sing}}\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{d}
be the subset consisting of the points
k\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
with all but one component equal to either
0
or
\frac{1}{2}
.
There exist
C,\lambda_{0}>0
, such that for any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have for all
0\leq\lambda<\lambda_{0}
,

(5.20)

\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\,e^{-it(\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k-k_{0}))}\bigg\rvert\leq{}\frac{C\langle t\rangle^{-1}}{d(k_{0},M^{\mathrm{sing}})}.

Proof.

From (
5.18
) we have

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\,e^{-it(\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k-k_{0}))}\bigg\rvert={}\lvert\mathcal{K}(0;t,\sigma t,0,-k_{0},0)\rvert

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\bigg\lvert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}p_{j}\,e^{ir_{j}\big(\cos(p_{j}+\phi_{j})-\lambda\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}kW_{L}^{\lambda}(k)\cos(p_{j}+\phi_{j}-2\pi k_{j})\big)}\bigg\rvert,

with
r_{j}=\lvert t\rvert\lvert 1+\sigma e^{-2\pi ik_{0,j}}\rvert
.
By the argumentation following (
5.19
), we can choose
\lambda
small enough, independently of
L
, so that by the stationary phase approximation, we have for any value of
r_{j}\geq 0
,

(5.21)

\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\,e^{-it(\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k-k_{0}))}\bigg\rvert\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\langle r_{j}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Now, using
\lvert 1+\sigma e^{-2\pi ik_{0,j}}\rvert\geq\lvert\sin(2\pi k_{0,j})\rvert
and picking the index
j
corresponding to the
second
largest of the numbers
\lvert\sin(2\pi k_{0,j})\rvert
, we find

\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}k\,e^{-it(\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k-k_{0}))}\bigg\rvert\leq{}\frac{C}{\langle t\rangle\lvert\sin(2\pi k_{0,j})\rvert},

so choosing
M^{\mathrm{sing}}
as described in the statement finishes the proof.
∎

With (
5.16
) in hand, the crossing bounds in
(DR4)
can be proven in the exact same way as in
[
13
]
.
However, the proof in
[
13
]
can easily be extended to dimensions
d\geq 3
instead of only
d\geq 4
, so we write out the details here for completeness.

Lemma 5.8

(Crossing bounds)
.

Suppose that
d\geq 3
.
Let
u_{m}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
\sigma_{m}\in\{\pm 1\}
for
m=1,2,3
, and
0<\zeta\leq 1
.
Then we have

(5.22)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\prod\limits_{m=1}^{3}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{m}s,0,u_{m},0)\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\leq{}C\zeta^{\frac{1}{7}-1}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{n,j})\rvert^{\frac{1}{7d}}}

for any
n=1,2,3
, and

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\lVert p_{t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{1}s,\sigma_{2}s,u_{1},u_{2})\rVert_{\ell^{3}}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\zeta^{\frac{1}{7}-1}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\lvert\sin(2\pi(u_{2,j}-u_{1,j}))\rvert^{\frac{1}{7d}}}.

It follows that
(DR4)
is satisfied with

(5.24)

F^{cr}(u,\zeta)\coloneq C\prod_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{j})\rvert^{\frac{1}{7}}}.

Proof.

We start out by proving (
5.22
).
By
Lemma

5.6
, we can bound

\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{m}s,0,u_{m},0)\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{d}\langle r_{j}(u_{m})\rangle^{-1/7},

where
r_{j}(u_{m})=\lvert t\pm se^{i2\pi u_{m,j}}\rvert\geq\max\{\lvert\lvert t\rvert-\lvert s\rvert\rvert,\lvert s\rvert\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{m,j})\rvert\}
.
We use the latter bound on
r_{j}
in a single coordinate
u_{n,i}
of
u_{n}
satisfying that
\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{n,i})\rvert
is as large as possible, and we apply the former bound in all other coordinates of the
u_{m}
, to get sufficient decay in
t
.
This means that we can bound

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\prod\limits_{m=1}^{3}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{m}s,0,u_{m},0)\rVert_{\ell^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\prod\limits_{m=1}^{3}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\langle r_{j}(u_{m})\rangle^{\frac{1}{7}}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\langle\lvert t\rvert-\lvert s\rvert\rangle^{-\frac{3d-1}{7}}\frac{1}{(\lvert s\rvert\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{n,i})\rvert)^{\frac{1}{7}}},

where, for
d\geq 3
, the
t
-integral is bounded independently of
s
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}t\langle\lvert t\rvert-\lvert s\rvert\rangle^{-\frac{3d-1}{7}}\leq{}2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-\frac{3d-1}{7}}<\infty.

Finally, noting that
\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{n,i})\rvert\geq\prod_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\sin(2\pi u_{n,j})\rvert^{1/d}
by choice of the coordinate
u_{n,i}
, it follows that (
5.22
) holds.

To prove (
5.23
), we have similarly, with the use of (
5.17
),

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\lVert p_{t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\sigma_{1}s,\sigma_{2}s,u_{1},u_{2})\rVert_{\ell^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\langle t\rangle^{-\frac{2d}{7}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\langle r_{j}\rangle^{-\frac{1}{7}},

where in this case we can bound

\displaystyle r_{j}={}

\displaystyle\lvert t\pm s(e^{i2\pi u_{1,j}}\pm e^{i2\pi u_{2,j}})\rvert

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\max\big\{\big\lvert\lvert t\rvert-\lvert s\rvert\lvert 1\pm e^{i2\pi(u_{2,j}-u_{1,j})}\rvert\big\rvert,\lvert s\rvert\lvert\sin(2\pi(u_{2,j}-u_{1,j}))\rvert\big\}.

As before, using the latter bound in the coordinate where
\lvert\sin(2\pi(u_{2,i}-u_{1,i}))\rvert
is the largest possible, and the former bound in all other coordinates, leads to

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}\langle t\rangle^{-\frac{2d}{7}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\langle r_{j}\rangle^{-\frac{1}{7}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\frac{e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}}{\langle t\rangle^{\frac{2d}{7}}}\frac{1}{(\lvert s\rvert\lvert\sin(2\pi(u_{2,i}-u_{1,i}))\rvert)^{\frac{1}{7}}}\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\langle\lvert t\rvert-\lvert s\rvert\lvert 1\pm e^{i2\pi(u_{2,j}-u_{1,j})}\rvert\rangle^{\frac{1}{7}}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s\frac{e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}}{\lvert s\rvert^{\frac{1}{7}}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\lvert\sin(2\pi(u_{2,j}-u_{1,j}))\rvert^{\frac{1}{7d}}},

and (
5.23
) follows.

We still need to check that (
2.27
) and (
2.28
) hold with our choice (
5.24
) of
F^{cr}
.
Since
F^{cr}
is independent of
\zeta
, and belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, (
2.27
) holds trivially with
c_{2}=0
.
For (
2.28
), we consider first
n=1
and rewrite the resolvent using (
5.11
).
We thus have to estimate

\displaystyle\iint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}F^{cr}(k_{1}+u)\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\Theta(k,\sigma)+i\zeta\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}sF(s;\zeta)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}F^{cr}(k_{1}+u)\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\,e^{-is(\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2}))}\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}sF(s;\zeta)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}F^{cr}(k_{1}+u)\prod_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\langle r_{j}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}},

where we used the estimate (
5.21
) with
r_{j}=\lvert s\rvert\lvert 1+\sigma e^{-2\pi i(k_{1,j}-k_{0,j})}\rvert
.
Plugging in the expression for
F^{cr}
and then applying Hölder’s inequality in the
k
-integral (for instance with the conjugate pair
(3,\frac{3}{2})
), we obtain that this is bounded from above by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s\frac{F(s;\zeta)}{\langle s\rangle^{\frac{d}{2}}}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}k_{1,j}\frac{1}{\lvert\sin 2\pi(k_{1,j}+u_{j})\rvert^{\frac{1}{7}}\lvert\sin 2\pi(k_{1,j}-k_{0,j})\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s\frac{1}{\langle s\rangle^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Big(\langle\ln\zeta\rangle e^{-\zeta\lvert s\rvert}+{\mathds{1}}(\lvert s\rvert\leq 1)\ln\frac{1}{\lvert s\rvert}\Big)\lesssim{}1,

uniformly for
0<\zeta\leq 1
since
d\geq 3
, showing that (
2.28
) holds with
c_{2}=0
.
The case
n=2
is handled in the same way, and for
n=3
, we simply make the change of variables
\tilde{k}_{1}=k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}
, which leads back to the case
n=1
, but with
\Theta(k,\sigma)
replaced by
-\Theta(k,-\sigma)
in the resolvent.
∎

6.
Graph estimates

6.1.
Basic estimates

In this section, we prove estimates on the amplitudes corresponding to graphs that are either higher order (that is, containing clusters that are not pairing), or partially paired (meaning pairing graphs that contain a vertex of degree one).

Lemma 6.1

(Basic graph estimate 1)
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that for
1\leq n\leq N_{0}
and
s>0
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}

\displaystyle\lambda^{2n}\sum_{\bar{\sigma},\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\Delta_{n,\ell^{\prime}}(\bar{k}^{\prime},\bar{\sigma}^{\prime})\prod_{A\in S}\delta_{L}\Big(\sum_{j\in A}K_{j}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big\lvert\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell_{j}^{\prime}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\times\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{2,2n}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j=2}^{2n}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=2}^{2n}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j;J)}\bigg\rvert

(6.1)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C^{1+\tilde{n}_{1}+\tilde{n}_{2}}\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{n}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\frac{(s\lambda^{2})^{\tilde{n}_{0}-{n}^{\prime}_{0}}}{(\tilde{n}_{0}-{n}^{\prime}_{0})!}\lambda^{2+\tilde{n}_{2}+(1-b)\tilde{n}_{1}-\tilde{n}_{0}}N_{0}^{-b_{0}n^{\prime}_{0}}\langle\ln n\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+\tilde{n}_{2}+2\tilde{n}_{1}},

where
\tilde{n}_{j}
is the number of non-amputated interaction vertices of degree
j
and
n^{\prime}_{0}
is the number of degree
0
interaction vertices
v_{l}
with
2<l\leq 2n-N_{0}+1
.
We have used the notations
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\cup\{(n,1)\}
and
K=(k_{0,{}\cdot{}}^{\prime},k_{0,{}\cdot{}})
from
Proposition

3.7
.

Proof.

Note first that the constraints on the parities in the expression above imply that the sums over
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
contain only one non-zero term, so they can be ignored.
We next argue that the
\limsup
can be taken inside the integrals.
First, we resolve the momentum constraints of the
\delta_{L}
-functions using the spanning tree constructed in
Section

4.1
.
This removes all the
\delta_{L}
-functions and the integrals over the ”integrated edges”, and only the integrals over the ”free edges” remain.
Then, we convert the remaining free momentum integrals into Lebesgue integrals of step functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, using (
2.33
), and note that the resulting integrand is uniformly bounded in
L
.
Indeed, since we have removed all the
\delta_{L}
-functions, all that remains of the integrand on the left hand side of (
6.1
) are the interaction terms
\Psi_{1}
and the time integral, which are bounded by
\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}
and
s^{2n-2}
, respectively.
Consequently, by dominated convergence we can move the
L\to\infty
limit inside the integrals.
By reinserting the momentum constraints, it is possible to write the limit as an expression identical to the left hand side of (
6.1
), but with every
\Omega^{\ast}
replaced by
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and every
\delta_{L}
replaced by a continuum
\delta
-function
\delta=\delta_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
.
However, we will refrain from doing this and instead continue working with the resolved momentum constraints in the following.

There are in total
2n
interaction vertices.
We denote
A_{j}
,
j=0,1,2
, to be the set of time slice indices
2\leq l<2n
such that
\mathrm{deg}(v_{l+1})=j
.
It could happen that
A_{j}=\emptyset
for some
j
but we always have

A_{0}\cup A_{1}\cup A_{2}=\{2,3,\dotsc,2n-1\}.

We set
B=\{l\in A_{0}\mid l\leq 2n-N_{0}\}
, which can be an empty set.
For all
l\in B
, it is clear that
\lfloor l/2\rfloor\leq n-N_{0}/2
.
We set
\gamma_{j}=\gamma(j;J)
and find by
Lemma

5.1
,

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{2,2n}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j=2}^{2n}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=2}^{2n}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma_{j}}\bigg\rvert

(6.2)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\oint_{\Gamma_{n}}\frac{\lvert\mathrm{d}z\rvert}{2\pi}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\bigg)\big\lvert e^{-ir_{*}z}\big\rvert\prod_{j\in A_{0}}\big\lvert e^{-ir_{j}\gamma_{j}}\big\rvert\prod_{j\in A}\frac{1}{\lvert z-\gamma_{j}\rvert},

where
\Gamma_{n}
is the contour depicted in Figure
12
, the set
A=\{2n\}\cup A_{1}\cup A_{2}
and
A^{\prime}=\{*\}\cup A_{0}
.

Figure 12.
The integration path
\Gamma_{n}
of (
6.1
).
Here,
c_{n}=2(2n+1)(\lVert\omega\rVert_{\infty}+2\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty})
, and for any momentum graph with
2n
vertices, the phase factors
\gamma(j;J)
always lie in the shaded region.

In the case that
j\in B
, we have
\mathrm{Im}(-\gamma_{j})=\kappa_{n-m}+\kappa_{n-m^{\prime}}
, in which
m+m^{\prime}=2+J_{+}(j-2;J)+J_{-}(j-2;J)=j
.
As a consequence,
\min(m,m^{\prime})\leq\lfloor l/2\rfloor\leq n-N_{0}/2
and then
\mathrm{Im}(-\gamma_{j})\geq\kappa_{n-\min(m,m^{\prime})}=\kappa^{\prime}=\lambda^{2}N_{0}^{b_{0}}\geq 0
.
We then find

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\bigg)\big\lvert e^{-ir_{*}z}\big\rvert\prod_{j\in A_{0}}\big\lvert e^{-ir_{j}\gamma_{j}}\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle e^{s(\mathrm{Im}z)_{+}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{B}}\mathrm{d}r\prod_{j\in B}e^{-\kappa^{\prime}r_{j}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}\setminus B}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j\in B}r_{j}-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}\setminus B}r_{j}\bigg)

(6.3)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle e^{s(\mathrm{Im}z)_{+}}(\kappa^{\prime})^{-\lvert B\rvert}\frac{s^{\tilde{n}}}{\tilde{n}!},

where
(\mathrm{Im}z)_{+}=\max\{\mathrm{Im}z,0\}
, and

\tilde{n}={}\lvert A^{\prime}\setminus B\rvert-1=\lvert A_{0}\setminus B\rvert=\lvert A_{0}\rvert-\lvert B\rvert=\tilde{n}_{0}-n^{\prime}_{0}.

Since
\gamma_{2n}=0
, we now deduce

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{2,2n}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j=2}^{2n}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=2}^{2n}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma_{j}}\bigg\rvert

(6.4)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(\kappa^{\prime})^{-n^{\prime}_{0}}\frac{s^{\tilde{n}_{0}-n^{\prime}_{0}}}{(\tilde{n}_{0}-n^{\prime}_{0})!}\oint_{\Gamma_{n}}\frac{\lvert\mathrm{d}z\rvert}{2\pi}\frac{e^{s(\mathrm{Im}z)_{+}}}{\lvert z\rvert}\prod_{j\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}}\frac{1}{\lvert z-\gamma_{j}\rvert}.

Now, let us estimate
\lvert\Psi_{1}(k_{0;\ell_{1}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}})\Psi_{1}(-k_{0;\ell_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\rvert\leq\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}
to remove the dependence on the two “amputated” interaction vertices at the bottom of the trees.
Supposing that there are any free momenta associated with these two vertices, they will be integrated out next, resulting in a factor of
1
.

Now, let us consider the resolvents
\frac{1}{\lvert z-\gamma_{j}\rvert}
,
j\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}
.
These vertices depend only on the free momenta associated to edges ending on time slices
j^{\prime}\geq j
.
Consider a degree one vertex in the plus tree.
By
Lemma

4.2
, there is a permutation
\pi
of
\{0,1,2\}
such that
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
is free.
Moreover, both
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(3)}
and
k_{0}\coloneq k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(2)}+k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
are independent of
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
.
Then, recalling
Proposition

3.1
and estimating
\lvert\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1;\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\rvert\leq\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}F_{1}(k_{0})
, we may apply
Lemma

5.4
for
k_{0}=k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(2)}+k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
,
\sigma=1
, and
\mathrm{d}k=\mathrm{d}k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
.
For a degree one vertex in the minus tree, the same strategy can be applied with
k_{0}^{\prime}=k_{j-1,\ell_{j}^{\prime}+\pi^{\prime}(2)}^{\prime}+k_{j-1,\ell_{j}^{\prime}+\pi^{\prime}(1)}^{\prime}
,
\sigma=-1
, and
\mathrm{d}k=\mathrm{d}k_{j-1,\ell_{j}^{\prime}+\pi^{\prime}(1)}^{\prime}
, by estimating
\lvert\Psi_{1}(-k_{j-1;\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\rvert\leq\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}F_{1}(-k_{0}^{\prime})
.
Consider a degree two vertex, by
Lemma

4.2
, there is a permutation
\pi
of
\{0,1,2\}
such that
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
,
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(2)}
are free and
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(3)}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}-k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(2)}
is independent of both
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
,
k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(2)}
.
We can then apply
Lemma

5.5
for
k_{0}=k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(3)}
,
\sigma=1
, and
\mathrm{d}k=\mathrm{d}k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(1)}
,
\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}=\mathrm{d}k_{j-1,\ell_{j}+\pi(2)}
.
If this degree two vertex is in the minus tree,
\sigma=-1
.
For degree zero vertices, we simply use the estimate
\lvert\Psi_{1}\rvert\leq\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}
.

By
Lemmas

5.4
and
5.5
, we can iteratively evaluate the free momentum integrals in the time direction, from the bottom to the top of the graph.
At each iteration, there is at most one resolvent factor depending on the corresponding free momenta.
The remaining free momenta only affect the value of the constant
\alpha
in
Lemmas

5.4
and
5.5
.
Using the contour
\Gamma_{n}
, the value for
\beta
is
\lambda^{2}
for the topmost edge of
\Gamma_{n}
.
The remaining
z
makes
\lvert z-\gamma_{j}\rvert\geq 1
and the upper bound remains valid after changing the constant.
Thus, from the momentum integrals we gain a contribution
C\lambda^{-b}\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}
from each degree one vertex, and
C\langle\ln\beta\rangle
from each degree two vertex.

The only integral left is

\oint_{\Gamma_{n}}\frac{\lvert\mathrm{d}z\rvert}{2\pi}\frac{e^{s(\mathrm{Im}z)_{+}}}{\lvert z\rvert},

which is bounded by
Ce^{s\lambda^{2}}\langle\ln(2n)\rangle\langle\ln\beta\rangle
.
Adding up the exponents, the result of the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 6.2

(Basic graph estimate 2)
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that for
n,n^{\prime}\geq 0
and
s>0
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lambda^{n+n^{\prime}}\sum_{\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\sum_{\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}}}{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n^{\prime},1}^{\prime}=-1)\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\int_{(\Omega^{\ast})^{\mathcal{I}_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\Delta_{n^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}(\bar{k}^{\prime},\bar{\sigma}^{\prime})\prod_{A\in S}\delta_{L}\bigg(\sum_{j\in A}K_{j}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big\lvert\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big\rvert\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\big\lvert\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{j-1:\ell_{j}^{\prime}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\times\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n+n^{\prime}}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j=0}^{n+n^{\prime}}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{n+n^{\prime}}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j;J)}\bigg\rvert

(6.5)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C^{1+n_{1}+n_{2}}\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{n+n^{\prime}}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\frac{(s\lambda^{2})^{n_{0}}}{n_{0}!}\lambda^{n_{2}+(1-b)n_{1}-n_{0}}\langle\ln(n+n^{\prime}+1)\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+n_{2}+2n_{1}},

where
n_{j}
is the number of interaction vertices of degree
j
.

Proof.

The proof follows exactly the same lines of arguments with that of
Lemma

6.1
, except there are no amputated vertices, and we instead set
B=\emptyset
.
∎

Lemma 6.3

(Basic graph estimate 3)
.

If we change the term

\Psi_{1}(k_{0:\ell_{1}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}})\Psi_{1}(-k^{\prime}_{0:\ell_{1}^{\prime}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}^{\prime}})

in (
6.1
) to

\Psi_{0}(k_{0:\ell_{1}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}})\Psi_{0}(-k^{\prime}_{0:\ell_{1}^{\prime}},\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}^{\prime}}),

the estimate can be improved by a factor of
C\langle\ln\lambda\rangle\lambda^{bd-2}
or the corresponding
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{2}
is
0
.
If
\tilde{n}_{1}=0
, then the factor can be improved by
C\langle\ln\lambda\rangle\lambda^{b(d-2)}
.
If any of the amputated vertices has degree
2
, then the factor can be improved by
C\langle\ln\lambda\rangle\lambda^{b(d-1)}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the case in which one of the amputated vertices has degree
2
.
If this is a minus vertex, then by
Proposition

3.1
and the fact that
\widehat{V}
is bounded,

(6.6)

\Psi_{0}(-k^{\prime}_{0:\ell_{1}^{\prime}})\lesssim\sum_{e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{1}),e_{1}<e_{2}}{\mathds{1}}\big(d(-(k_{e_{1}}+k_{e_{2}}),M^{\mathrm{sing}})\leq\lambda^{b}\big),

where
v_{1}
is the vertex that all the momenta
k^{\prime}_{0:\ell_{1}^{\prime}}
are attached to.
It follows from
Lemma

4.5
that
-k_{e_{1}}-k_{e_{2}}
depends on the two free momenta of
v_{1},
denoted by
\bar{k},\bar{k}^{\prime}
, in the form of
\bar{k},\bar{k}^{\prime}
, or
-\bar{k}-\bar{k}^{\prime}
.
When we integrate first over
\bar{k}
, then over
\bar{k}^{\prime}
,
Proposition

3.1
can be applied, leading to

(6.7)

\sup_{k_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k{\mathds{1}}\big(d(\pm k+k_{0},M^{{\mathrm{sing}}})<\lambda^{b}\big)\lesssim\lambda^{b(d-1)}.

Now, we can estimate
\Psi_{0}(k_{0:\ell_{1}})\lesssim 1
and the rest of the proof follows the same lines of arguments used in the proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

If the vertex does not have degree two, we can just estimate
\Psi_{0}(-k^{\prime}_{0:\ell_{1}^{\prime}})\lesssim 1
, with the notice that
\widehat{V}
is bounded, and integrate out on any free momenta attached to it.
The extra factor in the amputated plus vertex, denoted by
v_{2}
, can be estimated as

(6.8)

\Psi_{0}(k_{0:\ell_{1}})\lesssim\sum_{e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{2}),e_{1}<e_{2}}{\mathds{1}}\big(d(k_{e_{1}}+k_{e_{2}},M^{\mathrm{sing}})\leq\lambda^{b}\big).

If
v_{2}
has degree two, we gain a factor
\lambda^{b(d-1)}
and the proof follows the same lines of arguments used in the proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

To prove the other claims in the Lemma, we only need to consider the term associated to some fixed pair
e_{1}<e_{2}
with
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{2})
.
If
k_{e_{1}}+k_{e_{2}}
is independent of all free momenta, then by
Lemma

4.8
, we conclude that the two initial time vertices belonging to
e_{1}\cup e_{2}
are isolated from the rest of the initial time vertices, so they must be paired.
It then follows by
Proposition

3.9
that the graph is irrelevant, so we may therefore assume that
k_{e_{1}}+k_{e_{2}}
depends on some free momenta.
Among all of those free edges, we denote by
e_{3}
the one that is added last in the creation algorithm of the momentum graph (i.e. it is the maximum in the edge ordering).
Hence,
e_{3}
is the first of these free edges we will encounter when iterating through the graph from the bottom to the top.
Now, we carry on the same same lines of arguments used in the proof of
Lemma

6.1
, with the following exception: If the fusion vertex that
e_{3}
is attached to is of degree two, we will need the corresponding
\Psi_{1}
factor, which is kept unchanged.
The interaction vertices will be integrated until this fusion vertex is reached.
There are a few possibilities.
If this fusion vertex is either
v_{2}
itself or the top fusion vertex, we use (
6.7
) to gain a factor
\lambda^{b(d-1)}
.
If this vertex is a degree one non-amputated interaction vertex, we can remove the resolvent factor using a trivial
L^{\infty}
estimate and apply (
6.7
).
Since this degree one vertex was previously estimated by a power
\lambda^{-b}\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}
in the proof of
Lemma

6.1
, we thus gain a factor
\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\lambda^{b-2+b(d-1)}=\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\lambda^{bd-2}
compared to that estimate.
In the last case,
e_{3}
is attached to a non-amputated interaction vertex of degree two.
We denote the two free momenta by
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, the third integrated one by
k_{3}
and
k_{0}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
.
Then
k_{e_{1}}+k_{e_{2}}=\pm k_{j}+k^{\prime}_{0}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
k^{\prime}_{0}
is independent of
k_{1},k_{2}
.
We then need to estimate

\displaystyle\iint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}{\mathds{1}}\big(d(\pm k_{j}+k_{0}^{\prime},M^{\mathrm{sing}})<\lambda^{b}\big)

(6.9)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Psi_{1}(\pm(k_{1},k_{2},k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}))}{\lvert\omega^{\lambda}(k_{1})+\sigma^{\prime}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{2})+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})-\alpha-i\beta\rvert}.

If
j=1
, then
\Psi_{1}\lesssim F_{1}(\pm(k_{0}-k_{1}))
.
We then change the integration variable
k_{1}
to
k=\pm k_{1}+k_{0}^{\prime}
.

Lemma

5.4
can be applied to the integral of
k_{2}
and estimate (
6.7
) can be applied to the
k
integral.
This process gives a factor
\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\lambda^{-b+b(d-1)}
.
In case
j=2
or
3
, similar changes of variables can be applied to obtain the same contribution in
\lambda
.
Therefore, we get an improvement of
\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\lambda^{b(d-2)}
in this case.
After this, we can finish estimating the amplitude by following the same lines of arguments used in the proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

If
\tilde{n}_{1}=0
, then there is no non-amputated degree one vertex, so the worst contribution
\lambda^{bd-2}
does not occur.
This finally gives
\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\lambda^{b(d-2)}
, and the claims of the Lemma are proved.
∎

6.2.
Estimating
Q_{3}^{err}
and
Q_{2}^{err}

The graph estimates provided in the previous section suffice to prove that the constructive interference term
Q_{2}^{err}
and the amputated term
Q_{3}^{err}
do not contribute in the weak coupling limit.

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose that
t>0
and
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
and define
N_{0}
and
\kappa
as before.
There are constants
C>0
depending on
f
and
g
, and
c>0
depending on
\omega
and
V
such that

(6.10)

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{3}^{err}[g,f](t)\rvert^{2}\leq{}Ct^{2}e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{N_{0}}N_{0}^{2N_{0}+2}(4N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{4N_{0}+1}\big(\lambda+\lambda^{-2}N_{0}^{-b_{0}N_{0}/4}\big)

when
N_{0}(\lambda)\geq 44
.
In particular, in view of (
5.6
), it follows that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{3}^{err}\rvert

Proof.

We first observe that

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{3}^{err}[g,f](t)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}t^{2}\lambda^{-4}

(6.11)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\lambda^{-2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\text{ interlaces }\\
(N_{0}-1,N_{0}-1)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{N_{0}}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4N_{0}+2})}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert\mathcal{F}^{3}_{N_{0}}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)\rvert,

with
\mathcal{F}^{3}_{n}
as in (
3.41
).
By
Lemma

6.1
, the
\limsup
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{g}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\text{ interlaces }\\
(N_{0}-1,N_{0}-1)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{N_{0}}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4N_{0}+2})}\prod_{A\in S}\sup_{k_{A},\sigma_{A},\Omega}\lvert{C}_{\lvert A\rvert}(k_{A},\sigma_{A},\lambda,\Omega))\rvert

(6.12)

\displaystyle\times e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{N_{0}}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+4N_{0}}\lambda^{2+\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}+(1-b)\tilde{n}_{1}}N_{0}^{-b_{0}n^{\prime}_{0}},

where the notations of
\tilde{n}_{0},\tilde{n}_{1},\tilde{n}_{2},n_{0}^{\prime}
are the same as in
Lemma

6.1
.
We set
r=2N_{0}+1-\lvert S\rvert
and note that
\tilde{n}_{j}=n_{j}-n_{j}(2)\geq 0
,
n_{0}^{\prime}=n_{0}(N_{0}+1)-n_{0}(2)
.
It then follows from
Lemma

4.12
that
\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}=r+n_{0}(2)-n_{2}(2)
.
Using that
n_{0}(2)+n_{1}(2)+n_{2}(2)=2
, we obtain in the case where
r+n_{1}\geq 20
that
\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}+(1-b)\tilde{n}_{1}\geq r+\frac{1}{4}n_{1}-2\geq 3
, and hence
\lambda^{2+\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}+(1-b)\tilde{n}_{1}}N_{0}^{-b_{0}n^{\prime}_{0}}\leq\lambda^{5}
.

On the other hand, if
r+n_{1}\leq 19,
then by
Lemma

4.13
,
n^{\prime}_{0}\geq\frac{N_{0}+1-(n_{1}+r)}{2}-2\geq\frac{N_{0}}{2}-11
, which is at least
\frac{N_{0}}{4}
when
N_{0}\geq 44
.
It follows in this case that
\lambda^{2+\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}+(1-b)\tilde{n}_{1}}N_{0}^{-b_{0}n^{\prime}_{0}}\leq\lambda^{2}N_{0}^{-b_{0}N_{0}/4}
.
By being a bit more careful with estimating the exponents, it is possible to push the bound on
N_{0}
further down, but this is not needed.

Now, we know that the number of terms in the sum over
J
is smaller than
2^{2N_{0}-2}
and the number of terms in the sum over
\ell
is smaller than
(2N_{0})^{N_{0}}
.
Using
Lemma

5.2
, and collecting all the terms, we get the conclusion of the lemma after adjusting the constants
c
and
C
.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose that
t>0
and
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
and define
N_{0}
and
\kappa
as before.
There are constants
C>0
depending on
f
and
g
, and
c>0
depending on
\omega
and
V
such that

(6.13)

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{2}^{err}[g,f](t)\rvert^{2}\leq{}Ct^{2}e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{N_{0}}N_{0}^{2N_{0}+4}(4N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{4N_{0}+2}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}}.

In particular, in view of (
5.6
), it follows that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{2}^{err}\rvert={}0.

Proof.

The same argument used to prove
Proposition

6.4
can be reused to obtain the following estimate for
Q_{2}^{err}
.

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{2}^{err}[g,f](t)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle CN_{0}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}t^{2}\lambda^{-4}

(6.14)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\lambda^{-2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\text{ interlaces }\\
(n-1,n-1)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)\rvert,

with
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}
as in (
3.49
), and then the
\sup
term can be bounded, using
Lemma

6.3
, as

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{g}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\text{ interlaces }\\
(n-1,n-1)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\prod_{A\in S}\sup_{k_{A},\sigma_{A},\Omega}\lvert C_{\lvert A\rvert}(k_{A},\sigma_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\rvert

(6.15)

\displaystyle\times e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{n}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{2+4n}\lambda^{2+\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}+(1-b)\tilde{n}_{1}+db}{\mathds{1}}\big(\mathcal{F}^{2}_{n}\neq 0\big)\mathfrak{G},

where
\mathfrak{G}=\lambda^{-b}
when
n_{2}(2)>0
,
\mathfrak{G}=\lambda^{-2b}
when
n_{2}(2)=0,\tilde{n}_{1}=0
, and
\mathfrak{G}=\lambda^{-2}
otherwise.

By
Lemma

4.13
, we always have
\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}=r-n_{2}(2)+n_{0}(2)\geq 0
.
Hence, if
n_{2}(2)>0
, the power of
\lambda
in the expression above is bounded by
\lambda^{2+b(d-1)}\leq\lambda^{4+\frac{1}{4}}
in dimension
d\geq 4
.

On the other hand, if
n_{2}(2)=0
, then either
n_{0}(2)\geq 1
(in which case
\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}\geq 1
) or
n_{1}(2)=2
.
In the second case, there is exactly one free momentum attached to the bottom interaction vertex, the amputated vertex in the minus tree.
It follows by the iterative cluster scheme that there is a cluster
A\in S
that is connected to exactly two edges of this vertex.
If
A
is a pairing, then the graph is irrelevant by
Proposition

3.9
, so we may assume that
\lvert A\rvert\geq 4
, which in turn implies by
Lemma

4.12
that
r\geq 1
.
We conclude in any case that
\tilde{n}_{2}-\tilde{n}_{0}\geq 1
.

Thus, if
n_{2}(2)=0
and
\tilde{n}_{1}=0
, then the power of
\lambda
in (
6.2
) is bounded by
\lambda^{2+1+bd-2b}\leq\lambda^{4+\frac{1}{2}}\leq\lambda^{4+\frac{1}{4}}
.
In the final case where
n_{2}(2)=0
and
\tilde{n}_{1}\geq 1
, we obtain
\lambda^{2+1+1-b+db-2}=\lambda^{2+b(d-1)}\leq\lambda^{4+\frac{1}{4}}
.
The conclusion of the proposition now follows by estimating the sums in (
6.2
) as in the proof of
Proposition

6.4
.
∎

6.3.
Estimating
Q_{1}^{err}
and
Q^{main}

The following two results show that for the remaining terms in the Duhamel expansion, we can bound the amplitudes for all the graphs that are not fully paired.

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose that
t>0
and
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
and define
N_{0}
and
\kappa
as before.
There are constants
C>0
depending on
f
and
g
, and
c>0
depending on
\omega
,
V
, and
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{1}^{err}[g,f](t)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle Ct^{2}e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{N_{0}}N_{0}^{2N_{0}+5+2b_{0}}(4N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{4N_{0}+2}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}}+Ct^{2}N_{0}^{2+2b_{0}}

(6.16)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\lambda^{-2}\\
N_{0}/2\leq n<N_{0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\text{ interlaces }\\
(n,n)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\lvert\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)\rvert,

where
\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)=0
if the graph created by
S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime}
is not fully paired, otherwise it is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-\lambda^{2})^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum_{\bar{\sigma},\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}^{\prime}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\Delta_{n,\ell^{\prime}}(\bar{k}^{\prime},\bar{\sigma}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime}\Psi_{1}(-k_{j-1:\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime},-\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}^{\prime}}^{\prime})\big]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{A=\{j,l\}\in S}\big[\delta(K_{j}+K_{l}){\mathds{1}}(o_{j}=-o_{l})W^{0}(K_{j},o_{j})\big]

(6.17)

\displaystyle\times{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}^{\prime}=-1)\lvert\hat{g}(k_{n,1})\rvert^{2}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,2n}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j=0}^{2n}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{2n}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j;J)},

where we have reused the notation
K=(k_{0,{}\cdot{}}^{\prime},k_{0,{}\cdot{}})
and
o=(\sigma_{0,{}\cdot{}}^{\prime},\sigma_{0,{}\cdot{}})
from
Proposition

3.7
, as well as the notation from (
2.11
).

W^{0}
is given by (
2.9
).
For the pairings
A=\{j,l\}\in S
, we use the convention
j<l
, so that
o_{j}
denotes the parity of the left leg of the pairing.

Proof.

The same argument used to prove
Proposition

6.4
can be reused to obtain the following estimate for
\bar{Q}_{1}^{err}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert Q_{1}^{err}[g,f](t)\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle CN_{0}^{2}\kappa^{\prime}(\lambda)^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}t^{2}\lambda^{-4}

(6.18)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\lambda^{-2}\\
N_{0}/2\leq n<N_{0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\text{ interlaces }\\
(n,n)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{4n+2})}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\lvert\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)\rvert,

with
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}
as in (
3.47
).
Recall here that by definition (
5.3
),
\kappa^{\prime}(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}N_{0}^{b_{0}}
cancels the negative power of
\lambda
above.
For any graph that is not fully paired, we take the absolute value up to the time-integration and apply
Lemma

6.2
.
For these terms, we can thus bound the
\limsup
in (
6.3
) by

(6.19)

e^{t}\frac{t^{n_{0}}}{n_{0}!}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{2+4n}\lambda^{r+(1-b)n_{1}}C^{2n+1}\lVert\hat{g}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\prod_{A\in S}\sup_{k_{A},\sigma_{A},\Omega}\lvert C_{\lvert A\rvert}(k_{A},\sigma_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\rvert.

If the graph is pairing but not fully paired,
n_{1}\geq 1
.
If the graph is higher order,
r\geq 1
.
For both types, (
6.19
) is bounded by

(6.20)

e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{n}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{2+4n}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\hat{g}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\prod_{A\in S}\sup_{k_{A},\sigma_{A},\Omega}\lvert C_{\lvert A\rvert}(k_{A},\sigma_{A},\lambda,\Omega)\rvert.

Let us now consider a fully paired graph.
In this case, the clusters are pairings with
C_{2}((k^{\prime},k),(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma),\lambda,\Omega)=W_{L}^{\lambda}(k,\sigma^{\prime}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma^{\prime}=-\sigma)
by
Remark

3.3
.
Since by
Lemma

2.3
,

(6.21)

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert W_{L}^{\lambda}([k])-W^{0}(k)\rvert\leq{}2c_{0}^{2}\lambda,\mbox{ and }W^{0}(k)=W^{0}(-k),

for any finite index set
I
it holds true by induction that

(6.22)

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\bigg\lvert\prod_{j\in I}W_{L}^{\lambda}([\pm k_{j}],\sigma_{j})-\prod_{j\in I}W^{0}(k_{j},\sigma_{j})\bigg\rvert\leq{}\lvert I\rvert C^{\lvert I\rvert-1}2c_{0}^{2}\lambda,

with
C=2c_{0}^{2}\lambda_{0}^{\prime}+\lVert W^{0}\rVert_{\infty}<\infty
.
For pairing
S
, we have
\lvert S\rvert=2n+1
by
Lemma

6.2

Hence, we can replace in the definition of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}
all
C_{2}
terms by
W^{0}(K_{j},o_{j}){\mathds{1}}\big(o_{j}=-o_{l}\big)
with an error bounded by

(6.23)

Cn\lVert\hat{g}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{n}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{2+4n}\lambda.

After making this replacement, we resolve all the
\delta_{L}
-functions in the resulting expression and rewrite the integrals over the free momenta as Lebesgue integrals as in (
2.33
).
As explained in the beginning of the proof of
Lemma

6.1
, we can then apply the dominated convergence theorem to take the
L\to\infty
limit and obtain an expression of the form (
6.6
).

Finally, collecting all the terms and estimating the sums over
J,\ell,\ell^{\prime}
, and
S
as before, the conclusion of the proposition holds true.
∎

By exactly the same argument, we can also prove.

Proposition 6.7
.

Suppose that
t>0
and
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
and define
N_{0}
and
\kappa
as before.
There are constants
C>0
depending on
f
and
g
, and
c>0
depending on
\omega
,
V
, and
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{L\to\infty}\big\lvert{Q}^{main}[g,f](t)-{Q}^{main,pair}[g,f](t)\big\rvert^{2}

(6.24)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle Ce^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{N_{0}}N_{0}^{N_{0}+4}(2N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{2N_{0}+2}\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}},

where

(6.25)

Q^{main,pair}[g,f](t)={}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{0}-1}\sum_{\ell\in G_{n}}\sum_{S\in\pi(I_{0,2n+1})}\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t/\varepsilon,\kappa)

in which
\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t/\varepsilon,\kappa)=0
if the graph created by
S,\ell
is not fully paired, otherwise, it is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t/\varepsilon,\kappa)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i\lambda)^{n}\epsilon(S)\sum_{\bar{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\bar{k}\Delta_{n,\ell}(\bar{k},\bar{\sigma})\hat{g}(k_{n,1})^{\ast}\hat{f}(k_{n,1})

\displaystyle\times{\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{n,1}=1){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{0,0}=-1)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}\Psi_{1}(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})\big]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{A=\{j,l\}\in S}\big[\delta(k_{0,l}+k_{0,j}){\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{0,j}=-\sigma_{0,l})W^{0}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})\big]

(6.26)

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,n}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}-\sum_{j=0}^{n}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{n}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma_{j}},

with
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\cup\{(n,1)\}\cup\{(0,0)\}
as in
Proposition

3.5
, and
\gamma_{j}
defined by
(
3.50
)
.
We also use the notation from (
2.11
) with
W^{0}
as in (
2.9
).
For the pairings
A=\{j,l\}\in S
, we use the convention
j<l
, so that
\sigma_{0,j}
denotes the parity of the left leg of the pairing.

6.4.
Crossing graphs

All that remains now is to handle the contributions of fully paired graphs to
Q_{1}^{err}
and
Q^{main}
.
According to the results in
Section

4.3
, these consist exactly of the crossing, nested, and leading graphs.
We treat first the crossing graphs here.

Proposition 6.8

(Crossing graphs)
.

There is a constant
c_{0}
depending on
\omega
and
V
, and a constant
C
depending on
\omega,f,g,
such that the following estimates hold true for all crossing graphs

(6.27)

\displaystyle\big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)\big\rvert\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lambda^{2\gamma}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\langle c_{0}s\lambda^{2}\rangle^{n-1}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{3+c_{2}+2n},

(6.28)

\displaystyle\big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big\rvert\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lambda^{2\gamma}e^{t}\langle c_{0}t\rangle^{n/2-1}\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{3+c_{2}+n},

where the constants
\gamma,c_{2}
appear in
(DR4)
.

Proof.

We consider a general relevant crossing graph with
N=n^{\prime}+n
interaction vertices.
As in
Lemma

4.18
, let
v_{i_{2}}
be the first degree two vertex that does not correspond to an immediate recollision, and let
m^{\prime}=i_{0}-1
be the index of the last propagated crossing slice.
We denote the momenta of the edges in
\mathscr{E}(v_{i_{2}})
by
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
, and
k_{0}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
, with
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
being the free momenta.

We follow the general strategy of
Lemma

6.1
with a few exceptions, by first rewriting the time simplex integral.
We have now
A_{1}=\emptyset
, and we take
A=\{m^{\prime},N\}\cup A_{2}
, that is, we bundle the long time slice
m^{\prime}=i_{0}-1
together with the short time slices in order to get an extra resolvent factor where we can then apply the results of
Lemma

4.18
.
The point is that instead of getting the usual long time slice contribution of
\lambda^{-2}
for the last propagated crossing slice, we can use the crossing estimates of
(DR4)
to get a contribution of
\lambda^{2\gamma-2}
, which gives an improvement of
\lambda^{2\gamma}
over the basic estimates.
Observing that
\lvert A_{2}\rvert=\lvert A_{0}\rvert=N/2
, we find

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,N}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(s-\sum_{j=0}^{N}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{N}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j)}\bigg\rvert

(6.29)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{s^{N/2}-1}{(N/2-1)!}\oint_{\Gamma_{N/2}}\frac{\lvert\mathrm{d}z\rvert}{2\pi}\frac{e^{s(\mathrm{Im}z)_{+}}}{\lvert z\rvert}\prod_{j\in\{m^{\prime}\}\cup A_{2}}\frac{1}{\lvert z-\gamma(j)\rvert}.

By
Lemma

4.18
,
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m^{\prime})=p\Theta_{i_{2}}+\Theta_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{1}+\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})
for a
p\in\{0,1\}
, where
\alpha_{1}
,
\Theta_{i_{0}}
, and
\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})
are independent of all free momenta ending before or at
v_{i_{2}}
.
We can thus follow the basic iteration process until reaching the time slice
i_{2}-1
that ends at the crossing vertex
v_{i_{2}}
.
At this stage, the difficulty is the dependence of the factor
\frac{1}{z-\gamma(m^{\prime})}
on the free momenta.
The trivial estimate
\frac{1}{\lvert z-\gamma(m^{\prime})\rvert}\leq 1
can be applied if
z
does not belong to the top of the integration path, and the usual procedure of
Lemma

6.1
and
Lemma

6.2
can then be reused.
We obtain in this case an additional factor of
\lambda^{2}
in the upper bound of the amplitude, compared to the basic estimates.

We proceed now with the more complicated scenario:
z
belongs to the top of the integration path.
In this case,
z=\alpha+i\beta
for some
\lvert\alpha\rvert\leq 1+c_{N/2}=1+2(N+1)(\lVert\omega\rVert_{\infty}+2\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty})
and
\beta=\lambda^{2}
.
Recalling that
\mathrm{Im}\gamma(m)\leq 0
for all
m
, we can estimate all the remaining resolvent factors by

(6.30)

\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\gamma(m)+i\beta\rvert}\leq{}\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m)+i\beta\rvert}.

At this point, we need to estimate the double loop integral of
v_{i_{2}}
, which now also contains the resolvent factor of
\gamma(m^{\prime})
.
Using (
5.11
) to express the resolvents as oscillating integrals and then applying Fubini shows that

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2}-1)+i\beta\rvert}\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(m^{\prime})+i\beta\rvert}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})-\Theta_{i_{2}}+i\beta\rvert}\frac{1}{\lvert\alpha-\mathrm{Re}\gamma(i_{2})-\alpha_{1}-p\Theta_{i_{2}}-\Theta_{i_{0}}+i\beta\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}sF(r;\beta)F(s;\beta)\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\,e^{-i(r+ps)\Theta_{i_{2}}-is\Theta_{i_{0}}}\bigg\rvert

(6.31)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 4\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\bigg(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\,e^{-\beta\lvert s\rvert}\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\,e^{-i(r+ps)\Theta_{i_{2}}-is\Theta_{i_{0}}}\bigg\rvert\bigg).

Here, we can do the change of variables
r\to t=r+ps
and then estimate the integral using the crossing bounds
(DR4)
, depending on whether
v_{i_{0}}
is a
T
- or the
X
-vertex of the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.

Case 1:

v_{i_{0}}
is a
T_{j}
-vertex.
In this case, we have by
Lemma

4.18
that
\Theta_{i_{0}}=\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u)\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u^{\prime})+\alpha^{\prime}
for some choice of the signs, where
\alpha^{\prime},u^{\prime},u
are independent of
k_{1},k_{2}
, and
u^{\prime}-u
depends on some free momenta ending above
v_{i_{2}}
.
The momentum integral becomes

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\,e^{-it\Theta_{i_{2}}-is\Theta_{i_{0}}}\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\,e^{-it(\pm\omega_{1}^{\lambda}\pm\omega_{2}^{\lambda}\pm\omega_{3}^{\lambda})-is(\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u)\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u^{\prime}))}\bigg\rvert

(6.32)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\lVert p_{\pm t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{3}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};\pm t,\pm s,\pm s,u,u^{\prime})\rVert_{3}={}\lVert p_{t}^{\lambda}\rVert_{3}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\pm s,\pm s,u,u^{\prime})\rVert_{3},

where at the end we have used the time-reversal symmetries of
p_{t}^{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{K}
, and we will show the validity of the inequality in detail at the end of the proof.
Plugging (
6.4
) back into (
6.31
), we may now use the bound (
2.25
) from
(DR4)
to estimate (
6.31
) by

(6.33)

4\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\beta^{\gamma-1}(1+F^{\mathrm{cr}}(u^{\prime}-u,\beta)),

where
u^{\prime}-u
depends on some free momenta, and we have used
1\leq\beta^{\gamma-1}
since
0<\gamma\leq 1
.
We continue iterating the basic estimates of the resolvents for the remaining degree two vertices in (
6.4
) until the first of these momenta appears.
If this momentum is attached to a degree two vertex, we recall from
Lemma

4.5
that the dependence has to be of the form ”
\pm k_{j}
” for a
j\in\{1,2,3\}
.
We can then use (
2.28
) to obtain a factor
\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{1+c_{2}}
.
Otherwise, if
u^{\prime}-u
only depends on the free momentum at the top fusion vertex, we apply (
2.27
), yielding a factor
\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{c_{2}}
.
By comparing with the basic estimates, we have gained an additional factor of

(6.34)

C\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{c_{2}+1}\beta^{\gamma}\frac{1}{\langle s\beta\rangle},

which implies the bounds stated in the Proposition.

Case 2:

v_{i_{0}}
is the
X
-vertex.
Here, we have by
Lemma

4.18
that
\Theta_{i_{0}}=\alpha^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\pm\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j}+u_{j})
for some choice of the signs, where
\alpha^{\prime},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
are independent of
k_{1},k_{2}
, and at least one of the
u_{j}
depends on some free momenta ending above
v_{i_{2}}
.
We find, as for (
6.4
), that the momentum integral in (
6.31
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\,e^{-it\Theta_{i_{2}}-is\Theta_{i_{0}}}\bigg\rvert={}

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\prod_{j=1}^{3}e^{-i(\pm t\omega^{\lambda}(k_{j})\pm s\omega^{\lambda}({k_{j}}+u_{j}))}\bigg\rvert

(6.35)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{3}\lVert\mathcal{K}({}\cdot{};t,\pm s,0,u_{j},0)\rVert_{3}.

This time, we can apply the estimate (
2.26
) from
(DR4)
, and find that (
6.31
) is bounded by

(6.36)

4\langle\ln\beta\rangle^{2}\beta^{\gamma-1}(1+F^{\mathrm{cr}}(u_{j^{\prime}},\beta)),

for some index
j^{\prime}
satisfying that
u_{j^{\prime}}
depends on some free momenta above
v_{i_{2}}
.
From here, we can then follow the same steps used for the
T
-vertex case to get the same improvement (
6.34
) for the amplitude.

Finally, we just need to argue that the bounds in (
6.4
) and (
6.4
) hold.
For this, we note that
p_{t}^{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{K}
are defined as the inverse Fourier transforms of the functions
e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}}
and
e^{-i(t_{0}\omega^{\lambda}+t_{1}\omega^{\lambda}({}\cdot{}+u_{1})+t_{2}\omega^{\lambda}({}\cdot{}+u_{2}))}
, respectively.
Thus, we recognize that the integrals in (
6.4
) and (
6.4
) are both of the form

\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\hat{f}_{1}(k_{1})\hat{f}_{2}(k_{2})\hat{f}_{3}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}),

where the
f_{i}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
can be either
p_{t}^{\lambda}
or
\mathcal{K}
.
Simply expanding the Fourier transforms and then applying Fubini and Hölder, we find

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\hat{f}_{1}(k_{1})\hat{f}_{2}(k_{2})\hat{f}_{3}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-2\pi i(k_{1}\cdot(x_{1}-x_{3})+k_{2}\cdot(x_{2}-x_{3})+k_{0}\cdot x_{3})}f_{1}(x_{1})f_{2}(x_{2})f_{3}(x_{3})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\sum_{x_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-2\pi ik_{0}\cdot x_{3}}f_{1}(x_{3})f_{2}(x_{3})f_{3}(x_{3})\bigg\rvert\leq{}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{3}\lVert f_{2}\rVert_{3}\lVert f_{3}\rVert_{3},

which is exactly the stated bound in both (
6.4
) and (
6.4
).
∎

6.5.
Contributions from attaching leading motives to a graph

Before providing bounds on the amplitudes of the leading and nested graphs, we investigate more closely how the amplitude of a graph is affected when attaching a leading motive to the graph.

Lemma 6.9

(Adding a leading motive to a graph)
.

Consider any momentum graph with a pairing cluster at the bottom, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a leading motive (cf.
Fig.

5
) that can be attached to this pairing.

Let
\sigma
denote the parity of the left leg of the pairing where
\mathcal{M}
is attached,
k_{0}
the momentum of the edge in the initial time slice where
\mathcal{M}
is attached, and
r_{2}
the initial time variable.
Assuming that
\Phi_{1}=1
, adding the motive
\mathcal{M}
to the graph changes a factor
e^{-\kappa_{0}r_{2}}W^{0}(k_{0},\sigma)
to an expression of the form

(6.37)

-\lambda^{2}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\,\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}r\delta(r_{2}-r_{1}-r_{0})\prod_{m=0}^{2}e^{-r_{m}\kappa_{2-m}}F_{\mathcal{M}}(r_{1},k,\sigma),

where
k=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
, and the function
F_{\mathcal{M}}
is explicitly calculable.
For convenience, we have gathered all the possible contributions in
Table

1
.

\mathcal{M}

F_{\mathcal{M}}(r_{1},k,\sigma)

L1^{\tau}

\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}e^{-ir_{1}\tau\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}^{\sigma}W_{2}W_{3}

L2^{\tau}

\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})e^{-ir_{1}\tau\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}^{\sigma}W_{1}W_{3}

L3^{\tau}

\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}e^{-ir_{1}\tau\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}^{\sigma}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}

L4^{\tau}

-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})e^{-ir_{1}\tau\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}^{\sigma}W_{2}W_{3}

L5^{\tau}

-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}e^{-ir_{1}\tau\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}^{\sigma}W_{1}W_{3}

L6^{\tau}

-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})e^{-ir_{1}\tau\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}^{\sigma}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}

G1^{\sigma}

\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})e^{-ir_{1}\sigma\Theta(k,1)}W_{1}^{-\sigma}W_{2}^{\sigma}W_{3}^{\sigma}

G2^{\sigma}

\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})e^{ir_{1}\sigma\Theta(k,1)}W_{1}^{-\sigma}W_{2}^{\sigma}W_{3}^{\sigma}

G3^{\sigma}

-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}e^{-ir_{1}\sigma\Theta(k,1)}W_{1}^{-\sigma}W_{2}^{\sigma}W_{3}^{\sigma}

G4^{\sigma}

-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}e^{ir_{1}\sigma\Theta(k,1)}W_{1}^{-\sigma}W_{2}^{\sigma}W_{3}^{\sigma}

Table 1
.
Contributions to the amplitude from adding a leading motive to a pairing whose left leg has parity
\sigma
.
For the loss motives, we have introduced the parameter
\tau=\pm 1
to write the contribution from a motive and the conjugate motive on the same line.
We use the shorthand notation
W_{j}^{\sigma}\coloneq W^{0}(k_{j},\sigma)
.

Proof.

Consider first a pairing whose left leg has parity
\sigma=-1
, and suppose that we attach the loss motive L1 to this pairing (recall that the leading motives are gathered in
Fig.

5
).
Attaching L1 is exactly the same as attaching the diagram in
Fig.

6
to the original graph at the given pairing.
Recalling by
Lemma

3.10
that the sign
\epsilon(S)
of the clusters behaves multiplicatively when attaching graphs to each other, the resulting contribution has already been calculated in (
3.52
) in
Example

3.14
, except in the present case we have already taken
L\to\infty
.
Comparing with the zeroth order amplitude (which corresponds to the trivial graph consisting of just a single pairing),

\mathcal{F}_{0}^{main}={}\int_{\Omega^{\ast}}\mathrm{d}k_{0}\hat{g}(k_{0})^{\ast}\hat{f}(k_{0})e^{-\kappa_{0}r_{2}}W^{0}(k_{0},-1),

it is apparent that adding L1 to the given pairing gives a contribution equal to (
6.37
) with

F_{L1}(r_{1},k,-1)={}\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}e^{-ir_{1}\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}W_{2}W_{3}.

The contributions from adding any of the motives
L2,\dotsc,L6,G1^{-},G3^{-},G4^{-}
is calculated in exactly the same way, providing all the entries in
Table

1
for
\sigma=-1
and
\tau=1
, except for
G2^{-}
.

The contributions from attaching the remaining loss motives
L1^{-},\dotsc,L6^{-}
are easily obtained by noting that the resulting diagrams are ”conjugated” to the ones used to calculate the contributions from
L1,\dotsc,L6
, that is, the diagrams are obtained by reversing the parities of all edges and inverting the order of all edges in each time slice.
As explained in the proof of
Proposition

3.7
, this affects the corresponding amplitude by simply adding a complex conjugation. (The entire reason for introducing the minus trees in the first place is to handle the conjugated factors in (
3.36
)).
Similarly, we note that the diagram of G
2^{-}
is also obtained by conjugating the diagram of G
1^{-}
, so their amplitudes only differ by a complex conjugation.
This finishes all the entries in
Table

1
for
\sigma=-1
.

If we instead attach a loss motive to a pairing whose left leg has parity
\sigma=1
, it only changes the parity of the cluster corresponding to the initial momentum
k_{0}
.
In other words, instead of seeing
W^{0}(k_{0})
, we see now
\widetilde{W}^{0}(k_{0})
.
Everything else in the calculations of
Example

3.14
would be the same.

For the remaining gain motives, we calculate the contribution from G1 by hand and note that also G2 is obtained from G1 by conjugating the diagram.
finally. we note that the diagrams of G3 and G4 are obtained by conjugating the diagrams of G
4^{-}
and G
3^{-}
, respectively, except all the parities are reversed.
This means that the amplitude of G3 can be obtained from that of G
4^{-}
by adding a complex conjugation and switching any
W^{0}(k_{j},\pm 1)
to
W^{0}(k_{j},\mp 1)
, and the same holds for G4 and G
3^{-}
.
∎

We need to control the time-integrability of factors obtained by iteratively attaching leading motives, so in view of the assertions of
Lemma

6.9
, we consider the function
G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{4}\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, defined for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{4}
by

\displaystyle G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}[f_{0},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}](k_{0})

(6.38)

\displaystyle\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\widetilde{U}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\prod_{i=0}^{3}e^{-is\tau_{i}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{i})}f_{i}(k_{i}),

where
\widetilde{U}
can be either
\pm\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}
,
\pm\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})^{2}
, or
\pm\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})
, depending on the leading motive in question.
We will typically abbreviate
G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}=G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}[f_{0},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]
.
Note that by Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle\lvert G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}(k_{0})\rvert\leq{}

\displaystyle\lvert f_{0}(k_{0})\rvert\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\lvert f_{1}(k_{1})\rvert\lvert f_{2}(k_{2})\rvert\lvert f_{3}(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2})\rvert

(6.39)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\lvert f_{0}(k_{0})\rvert\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\big(\lvert f_{1}\rvert\ast\lvert f_{2}\rvert\ast\lvert f_{3}\rvert\big)(k_{0})\leq{}\lvert f_{0}(k_{0})\rvert\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{\frac{3}{2}},

so
G_{s,\tau}
indeed maps into
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, since
\lVert f\rVert_{3/2}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}
.
An analogous definition of
G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}
is treated in
[
13
]
, and the proofs of the following bounds on
G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}
are essentially the same as in
[
13
]
.
The only differences in our case is the presence of the interaction term
\widetilde{U}
, and the dependence of
\lambda
in the modified dispersion relation in (
6.38
).

Consider also the free evolution semigroup
U_{t}^{\lambda}
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
, that is,

(6.40)

(U_{t}^{\lambda}g)(x)\coloneq{}p_{t}^{\lambda}\ast g(x)={}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}p_{t}^{\lambda}(x-y)g(y),\quad g\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}),

where
p_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}ke^{2\pi ik\cdot x-it\omega^{\lambda}(k)}
is the free propagator as defined in (
2.21
).

Lemma 6.10
.

We have the pointwise bound

(6.41)

\lvert G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}(k_{0})\rvert\leq{}\lvert f_{0}(k_{0})\rvert\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})},

and for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(6.42)

\lVert U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{G}_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\leq{}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\check{f_{0}}\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\lVert p_{t+\tau_{0}s}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f_{i}}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}.

Proof.

Since
p_{t}^{\lambda}
is an inverse Fourier transform,
p_{t}^{\lambda}(x)=(e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}})\check{\mkern 6.0mu}(x)
, we have for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(6.43)

U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{f}={}p_{t}^{\lambda}\ast\check{f}={}(e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}}f)\check{\mkern 6.0mu},

so that

(6.44)

e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}(k)}f(k)={}(U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{f})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}(k)={}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}(U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{f})(x).

The idea is to use the convolution structure, as in (
6.39
), but we cannot immediately take the absolute value inside the integral, so we have to be a bit more careful with the interaction factor.
We write

(6.45)

G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}(k_{0})={}f_{0}(k_{0})e^{-i\tau_{0}s\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0})}F(k_{0}),

with

(6.46)

F(k_{0})=\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\widetilde{U}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\prod_{i=1}^{3}e^{-is\tau_{i}\omega^{\lambda}(k_{i})}f_{i}(k_{i}),

Note that because of the
\delta
-function, we have
\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})=\widehat{V}(k_{0}-k_{3})
and
\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})=\widehat{V}(k_{0}-k_{2})
.
Writing
V_{k_{0}}(x)\coloneq e^{2\pi ik_{0}\cdot x}V(-x)
, so that
\widehat{V}(k_{0}-k_{3})=\widehat{V}_{k_{0}}(k_{3})
, and using (
6.44
), we can express
F
as the Fourier transform of a single function, evaluated at
k_{0}
.
For instance, if
\widetilde{U}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=\widehat{V}(k_{0}-k_{3})^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle F(k_{0})={}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})&(U_{\tau_{1}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{1})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}(k_{1})(U_{\tau_{2}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{2})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}(k_{2})\\
\times&(V_{k_{0}}\ast V_{k_{0}}\ast U_{\tau_{3}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{3})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}(k_{3})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(U_{\tau_{1}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{1})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}\ast(U_{\tau_{2}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{2})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}\ast(V_{k_{0}}\ast V_{k_{0}}\ast U_{\tau_{3}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{3})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}(k_{0})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big((U_{\tau_{1}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{1})(U_{\tau_{2}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{2})(V_{k_{0}}\ast V_{k_{0}}\ast U_{\tau_{3}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{3})\big)\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}(k_{0}),

and similarly for the other possible cases of
\widetilde{U}
.
We conclude that
F
can be written

F(k_{0})={}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-2\pi ik_{0}\cdot x}\prod_{i=1}^{3}h_{i}(x),

where the
h_{i}
can be either
\pm U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{i}
,
\pm V_{k_{0}}\ast(U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{i})
, or
\pm V_{k_{0}}\ast V_{k_{0}}\ast(U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{i})
, depending on the form of
\widetilde{U}
, but with
V_{k_{0}}
appearing twice in total in the expression of
F
.
Now, applying the Hölder and Young inequalities,

(6.47)
