\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq{}\lVert\check{F}\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\leq{}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert h_{i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z})^{d}}\leq{}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})},

since
\lVert V_{k_{0}}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}=\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}
.
This finishes the proof of (
6.41
).

To see that (
6.42
) holds, note first that

\lVert(f_{0}F)\check{\mkern 6.0mu}\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}={}\lVert\check{f}_{0}\ast\check{F}\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\leq{}\lVert\check{f}_{0}\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\tau_{i}s}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}.

Returning to (
6.45
) and using (
6.44
), we have for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
that

(U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{G}_{s,\tau}^{\lambda})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}={}e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}}G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}={}e^{-i(t+\tau_{0}s)\omega^{\lambda}}f_{0}F={}(U_{t+\tau_{0}s}^{\lambda}(f_{0}F)\check{\mkern 6.0mu})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu},

so (
6.42
) now follows by (
6.43
) and Young’s inequality.
∎

We will need to control the time-integrability of iterations of
G_{s,\tau}
, i.e., factors of the form (
6.38
) where the input functions
f_{i}
are themselves of the form (
6.38
).

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
G^{\lambda}
be a factor of the form (
6.38
) obtained by iteration of
M
leading motive integrals, and let
s_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\tau_{m}\in\{\pm 1\}^{4}
be the corresponding time variables and parities.
Then, for each
m=1,\dotsc,M
there are (possibly empty) sets
B_{m,i}\subseteq\{1,\dotsc,m-1\}
,
i=1,2,3
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

(6.48)

\lvert G^{\lambda}(k)\rvert\leq{}\lvert h_{0}(k)\rvert c_{1}^{3M}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2M}\prod_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{\tau_{m,i}s_{m}+\sum_{j\in B_{m,i}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}},

where
h_{0}\in\{1,W^{0},1-W^{0}\}
and
c_{1}=\max\{1,\lVert\check{W}^{0}\rVert_{\ell^{1}},\lVert(1-W^{0})\check{\mkern 6.0mu}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}\}
.
Furthermore, there is a subset
B_{M}\subseteq\{1,\dotsc,M\}
such that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(6.49)

\lVert U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{G}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\leq{}c_{1}^{3M+1}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2M}\big\lVert p_{t+\sum_{j\in B_{M}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\prod_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{\tau_{m,i}s_{m}+\sum_{j\in B_{m,i}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}.

In particular, if the modified dispersion relation
\omega^{\lambda}
satisfies
(DR2)
, then (
6.48
) provides an upper bound
\lvert G^{\lambda}(k)\rvert\leq\widetilde{G}(s)
, uniformly in
k
and
\lambda
, such that
\widetilde{G}
is integrable on
\mathbb{R}^{M}
.

Proof.

In order to prove (
6.48
) and (
6.49
), we will need an extension of (
6.42
).
First, we need to expand
G^{\lambda}
in the first argument
f_{0}
until we see either a factor
1
,
W^{0}
, or
\widetilde{W}^{0}
.
More precisely, we may write
G^{\lambda}=G_{r_{1},\sigma_{1}}^{\lambda}[f_{1,0},f_{1,1},f_{1,2},f_{1,3}]
, where each
f_{1,i}
is either of the form (
6.38
) or one of
1,W^{0},\widetilde{W}^{0}
, and
r_{1}=s_{M}
,
\sigma_{1}=\tau_{M}
.
If
f_{1,0}
is of the form (
6.38
), we keep expanding in the first argument until it is no longer possible, at which point we have for some
1\leq N\leq M
, and some
r_{n}
,
\sigma_{n}
which are a subset of the
s_{m}
,
\tau_{m}
,

(6.50)

G^{\lambda}(k)={}h_{0}(k)\prod_{n=1}^{N}e^{-ir_{n}\sigma_{n,0}\omega^{\lambda}(k)}\prod_{n=1}^{N}F_{n}(k),

where
h_{0}\in\{1,W^{0},\widetilde{W}^{0}\}
, and
F_{n}
is of the form (
6.46
) with some
f_{n,i}
obtained from other iterations, that is,
f_{n,i}
is either of the form (
6.38
), or
f_{n,i}\in\{1,W^{0},\widetilde{W}^{0}\}
.
In particular, as in (
6.47
),

(6.51)

\lVert F_{n}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq{}\lVert\check{F}_{n}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}\leq{}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\sigma_{n,i}r_{n}}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{n,i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}.

Denoting
H\coloneq{}h_{0}\prod_{n=1}^{N}F_{n}
, we then have by (
6.51
) that

\lVert\check{H}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}={}\lVert\check{h}_{0}\ast\check{F}_{1}\ast\cdots\ast\check{F}_{N}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}\leq{}\lVert\check{h}_{0}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2N}\prod_{n=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\sigma_{n,i}r_{n}}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{n,i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}.

Returning to (
6.50
) and using (
6.44
), we have for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
that

(U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{G}^{\lambda})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu}={}e^{-it\omega^{\lambda}}G^{\lambda}={}e^{-i\big(t+\sum_{n=1}^{N}r_{n}\sigma_{n,0}\big)\omega^{\lambda}}H={}(U_{t+\sum_{n=1}^{N}r_{n}\sigma_{n,0}}^{\lambda}\check{H})\hat{\mkern 6.0mu},

so by (
6.43
), we conclude

(6.52)

\lVert U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{G}^{\lambda}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\leq{}\lVert\check{h}_{0}\rVert_{\ell^{1}}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2N}\big\lVert p_{t+\sum_{n=1}^{N}r_{n}\sigma_{n,0}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\prod_{n=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\lVert U_{\sigma_{n,i}r_{n}}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{n,i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}.

We now return to the bounds (
6.48
) and (
6.49
), which we prove simultaneously by induction.
Note that the bounds have already been established for
M=1
by
Lemma

6.10
, and note that both bounds also hold for
M=0
, in the case where
G^{\lambda}=h_{0}
is just a given function.
Thus we only need to do the induction step, so assume that (
6.48
) and (
6.49
) hold for any factor obtained by
K<M
iterations of leading motive integrals.
Write now

G^{\lambda}=G_{s_{M},\tau_{M}}^{\lambda}[G_{0},G_{1},G_{2},G_{3}],

where each
G_{p}
,
p=0,1,2,3
, is an iteration of
K_{p}
applications of
G_{s,\tau}^{\lambda}
, with
K_{p}\in\{0,\dotsc,M-1\}
and
\sum_{p=0}^{3}K_{p}=M-1
.
Note that if a time variable
s_{m}
appears in
G_{p}
, then it does not appear in any of the other factors
G_{q}
,
q\neq p
.
In other words, there are (possibly empty) disjoint subsequences
(m_{n}^{p})_{n=1}^{K_{p}}\subseteq\{1,\dotsc,M-1\}
whose union is
\{0,\dotsc,M-1\}
, such that the time variables in
G_{p}
are given by
s_{m_{n}^{p}}
.
Then, by the induction hypothesis,

\lvert G_{0}(k)\rvert\leq{}\lvert h_{0}(k)\rvert c_{1}^{3K_{0}}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2K_{0}}\prod_{n=1}^{K_{0}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{\tau_{m_{n}^{0},i}s_{m_{n}^{0}}+\sum_{j\in B_{m_{n}^{0},i}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}},

and for
p=1,2,3
,

\lVert U_{t}^{\lambda}\check{G}_{p}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\leq{}c_{1}^{3K_{p}+1}\lVert V\rVert_{\ell^{1}}^{2K_{p}}\big\lVert p_{t+\sum_{j\in B_{K_{p}}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\prod_{n=1}^{K_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{\tau_{m_{n}^{p},i}s_{m_{n}^{p}}+\sum_{j\in B_{m_{n}^{p},i}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}.

Now, combining these with (
6.41
) applied to
G^{\lambda}
, we obtain (
6.48
) by rearranging the factors and setting
B_{M-1,i}\coloneq B_{K_{i}}
.

To prove (
6.49
), the argument is the same, except one starts from (
6.52
) and applies the induction hypothesis to each of the factors
\lVert U_{\sigma_{n,i}r_{n}}^{\lambda}\check{f}_{n,i}\rVert_{\ell^{3}}
.

Finally, to easily see that the right hand side of (
6.48
) is integrable in
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{M}
uniformly in
k
and
\lambda
when
\omega^{\lambda}
satisfies
(DR2)
, we note first that
\lvert h_{0}(k)\rvert\leq 1
for all
k
.
Then we can apply the
\ell^{3}
-dispersivity to get rid of the
\lambda
dependence, and iteratively integrate out the time variables, starting with
s_{M}
.
At the first step, exactly three factors depend on
s_{M}
, the integral of which we can estimate by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s_{M}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{\tau_{M,i}s_{M}+\sum_{j\in B_{M,i}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{\ell^{3}}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s_{M}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\Big\langle\tau_{M,i}s_{M}+\sum_{j\in B_{M,i}}\tau_{j,i}s_{j}\Big\rangle^{-\frac{1+\delta}{3}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}s_{M}\langle s_{M}\rangle^{-1-\delta},

by use of Hölder’s inequality.
Now, only three of the remaining factors in (
6.48
) depend on
s_{M-1}
, so we can repeat this procedure
M-1
more times to integrate out all the time variables.
∎

6.6.
Leading and nested graphs

Here we provide the final bounds that we need on the leading and nested graphs in order to finish the proof of the main theorem in the next section.
The contributions from the presence of leading motives in the graphs need to be estimated carefully, and we need to remove the momentum cut-offs
\Phi_{1}
in order to use the structure of the
G_{s,\tau}
-factors from
Section

6.5
.

Lemma 6.12

(Removing the cut-off)
.

There is a constant
c>0
depending on
\omega
, and a
C>0
depending on
\omega,f,g,
such that the following estimates hold true for all relevant fully paired graphs,

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)-\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert

(6.53)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{2n}n\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\langle cs\lambda^{2}\rangle^{n}\langle\ln n\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+n},

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)-\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert

(6.54)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{n}n\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{n/2}\langle\ln n\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+n/2},

with
\gamma^{\prime}
as in (
5.1
).

Proof.

Consider a relevant fully paired graph with
N
interaction vertices.
The idea is to iteratively expand the cut-off functions
\Phi_{1}=1-\Phi_{0}
at each interaction vertex
v_{m}
, going from the bottom of the graph to the top.
At each step we obtain two terms, one that corresponds to replacing
\Phi_{1}
with
1
at the vertex
v_{m}
, which we use to continue the iteration, and one that corresponds to replacing
\Phi_{1}
with
\Phi_{0}
at
v_{m}
, which we need to estimate.

In the amplitude corresponding to the term with
\Phi_{0}
, we take the absolute value inside all the momentum integrals, and then estimate any remaining
\Phi_{1}
factors and all
W^{0}
factors by one, and all interaction factors by
\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}
.
The resulting amplitude looks almost identical to the one in (
6.2
), except there is now a single
\Phi_{0}
factor at
v_{m}
instead of all the usual interaction factors
\Phi_{1}
.
Then, we estimate the time integral as in the basic scheme and obtain an expression with resolvents
\lvert z-\gamma(j)\rvert^{-1}
for the degree two vertices, of which there are
N/2
since the graph is fully paired.
Now, we estimate the
\Phi_{0}
factor using
Proposition

3.1
,

\Phi_{0}(\pm k)\leq\sum\limits_{e_{i}<e_{j}}{\mathds{1}}\big(d(\pm(k_{e_{i}}+k_{e_{j}}),M^{\mathrm{sing}})<\lambda^{b}\big),

where the sum runs over pairs of edges in
\mathscr{E}_{-}(v_{m})
.

Fix now any pair
e_{i}<e_{j}
and note that since the graph is relevant, we must have
k_{e_{i}}+k_{e_{j}}\neq 0
(because of the initial presence of the
\Phi_{1}
factors), so by
Lemma

4.8
,
k_{e_{i}}+k_{e_{j}}
must depend on some free momenta.
We now iterate through the momentum integrals of the degree two vertices, from the bottom to the top of the graph, until we reach a vertex where
k_{e_{i}}+k_{e_{j}}
depends on the free momenta.
If it only depends on the free momentum at the top fusion vertex, then we obtain an additional factor
\lambda^{b(d-1)}
from integrating the characteristic function, by
Proposition

3.1
.
Otherwise,
k_{e_{i}}+k_{e_{j}}
depends double loop of some degree two vertex
v_{m^{\prime}}
, and we have to estimate

\iint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\frac{{\mathds{1}}\big(d(\pm(k_{e_{i}}+k_{e_{j}}),M^{\mathrm{sing}})<\lambda^{b}\big)}{\lvert z-\gamma(m^{\prime})\rvert},

where
k_{1},k_{2}
are the free momenta of
v_{m^{\prime}}
.
However, trivially estimating the resolvent
\lvert z-\gamma(m^{\prime})\rvert^{-1}\leq\lambda^{-2}
and then integrating the characteristic functions yields a bound
\lambda^{b(d-1)-2}\leq\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}
for this vertex.
After this, the iteration scheme of the basic estimate can be continued, yielding a contribution of
\langle\ln\lambda\rangle
from any other degree two vertex.
In conclusion, we find that the amplitude with the extra characteristic function is bounded by

C\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{N}\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\langle cs\lambda^{2}\rangle^{N}\langle\ln N\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+N},

which has to be multiplied by
N
to give an upper bound on the difference in (
6.53
), (
6.54
), since we remove
\Phi_{1}
in
N
steps.
∎

Remark 6.13

(Dimension)
.

Lemma

6.12
is one of two places where we use the assumption that
d\geq 4
, the other place being in
Lemma

6.3
.
However, it might be worth noting that for the specific case of the nearest neighbour dispersion relation (with corresponding
V
) as in
Section

5.3
, it is possible to combine the bound (
5.21
) with the representation (
5.11
) to obtain a different estimate of the degree one vertex integrals in
Lemma

5.4
, of the form

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\frac{F_{1}(\sigma^{\prime}k)}{\lvert\omega^{\lambda}(k)+\sigma\omega^{\lambda}(k_{0}-k)-\alpha+i\beta\rvert}\lesssim{}\langle\ln\beta\rangle\prod_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{d_{\mathbb{T}}\big(k_{0,j},\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}\big)^{1/2}}.

Here, the right hand side is not uniform in
k_{0}
, but it has a better dependence on
\lambda
.
Then, combining with Hölder’s inequality, one may avoid using the trivial estimate
\lambda^{-2}
on the resolvent factor in the proof above.
This allows for removal of the cut-off functions also in three dimensions, in this specific case.

Proposition 6.14

(Leading graphs)
.

There is a constant
c_{0}>0
depending on
\omega
,
V
, and a
C>0
depending on
\omega,V,f,g,
such that the following estimates hold true for all leading graphs,

(6.55)

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert\leq{}\frac{C}{n!}(c_{0}\lambda^{2}s)^{n},

(6.56)

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert\leq{}\frac{C}{(n/2)!}(c_{0}t)^{n/2}.

Proof.

The argument is the same for the two bounds, so we write it in detail only for
\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}
.
Recall that any leading graph with
n=2m
interaction vertices is built up from exactly
m
leading motives.
We start from (
6.7
) and consider first the phase factors
\gamma_{j}
given by (
3.50
),

\gamma_{j}=\sum_{l=j+1}^{n}\Theta_{l-1:\ell_{l}}(k,\sigma)-i\kappa_{n-j}.

Since the leading motives preserve the phase, meaning that
\Theta_{l-1:\ell_{l}}=-\Theta_{l:\ell_{l+1}}
for all odd
l
, as discussed in
Remark

3.13
, the terms in the sum above cancel pairwise, implying for all
j
that

\mathrm{Re}\,\gamma_{j}={}\begin{cases}0,&j\text{ even},\\
\Theta_{j-1:\ell_{j}},&j\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

Now, since there are two free edges for every leading motive in the graph, we can resolve the momentum constraints as usual and write, by abbreviating
U_{j}\coloneq U(k_{j-1:\ell_{j}},\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}})
from (
3.11
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{2m}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i)^{m}\lambda^{2m}\epsilon(S)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k_{2m,1}\hat{g}(k_{2m,1})^{\ast}\hat{f}(k_{2m,1})

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{d}k_{j,1}\mathrm{d}{k_{j,2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2m}\big[\sigma_{j,\ell_{j}}U_{j}\big]\prod_{A=\{j,l\}\in S}W^{0}(k_{0,j},\sigma_{0,j})

\displaystyle\times\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,2m}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(\frac{t}{\lambda^{2}}-\sum_{j=0}^{2m}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{2m}e^{-r_{j}\kappa_{2m-j}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}e^{-ir_{2j-1}\Theta_{2j}}.

Then, changing the time variables from
r
to
(\tilde{s},s)
with
\tilde{s}_{i}=\lambda^{2}r_{2i}
for
i=0,1,\dotsc,m
, and
s_{i}=r_{2i-1}
for
i=1,\dotsc,m
, we can rewrite the time integral,

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,2m}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\bigg(\frac{t}{\lambda^{2}}-\sum_{j=0}^{2m}r_{j}\bigg)\prod_{j=0}^{2m}e^{-r_{j}\kappa_{2m-j}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}e^{-ir_{2j-1}\Theta_{2j}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\lambda^{-2m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}}\mathrm{d}s\prod_{j=1}^{m}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{2m-(2j-1)}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}e^{-is_{j}\Theta_{2j}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m+1}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}\delta\Big(t-\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\tilde{s}_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=0}^{m}e^{-\lambda^{-2}\tilde{s}_{j}\kappa_{2m-2j}}.

Now, evaluating the momentum integrals for each motive leads to iterations of
G_{s_{i},\tau_{i}}
as in (
6.38
), yielding a function
\widetilde{G}^{\lambda}(k_{2m,1},s;S,\ell)
, which by
Proposition

6.11
has an
s
-integrable upper bound
G(s;S,\ell)
, independent of
k_{2m,1}
and
\lambda
.
That is, we can rewrite
\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{2m}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{2m}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-i)^{m}\epsilon(S)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}}\mathrm{d}s\prod_{j=1}^{m}e^{-s_{j}\kappa_{2m-(2j-1)}}\widetilde{G}^{\lambda}(k,s;S,\ell)

(6.57)

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m+1}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}\delta\Big(t-\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\tilde{s}_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=0}^{m}e^{-\lambda^{-2}\tilde{s}_{j}\kappa_{2m-2j}}.

Finally, taking absolute values inside the integrals and simply estimating the
\kappa
-dependent factors by
1
, we obtain (
6.56
) after noting that

(6.58)

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m+1}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}\delta\Big(t-\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\tilde{s}_{i}\Big)={}\frac{1}{m!}\Big(t-\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\Big)^{m}{\mathds{1}}\Big(0\leq t-\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\Big),

which is easily shown by induction.
The constant
c_{0}
comes from the integral of the upper bound on
G^{\lambda}
from (
6.48
).
∎

Proposition 6.15

(Nested graphs)
.

There is a constant
c>0
, depending on
\omega,V
, and a
C>0
depending on
\omega,f,g,
such that the following estimates hold true for all relevant nested graphs,

(6.59)

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{err,pair}_{n}(S,J,\ell,\ell^{\prime},s,\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\langle cs\lambda^{2}\rangle^{n}\langle\ln n\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+n},

(6.60)

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert\leq{}

\displaystyle C\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}e^{t}\langle ct\rangle^{n/2}\langle\ln n\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+n/2}.

Proof.

Let
i_{2}
and
j_{0}
be as in
Lemma

4.17
, that is,
v_{i_{2}}
is the first degree two vertex from the bottom that does not belong to an immediate recollision, and
j_{0}
is the index of the first time slice that is nested inside the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
.
Consider the contribution of the first
i_{2}
time slices to the total phase in the time integral, i.e.
e^{-i\sum_{j=0}^{i_{2}-1}r_{j}\gamma(j)}
.
Recalling that the imaginary part of
\gamma(j)
is always non-positive just a sum of entries of
\kappa
, we can re-write

(6.61)

\sum_{i=0}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i}\gamma(i)={}\zeta(r)+\sum_{i=0}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i}\sum_{j=i+1}^{i_{2}}\Theta_{j},

where
\mathrm{Im}\zeta(r)\leq 0
does not depend on any free momenta ending before (or at)
v_{i_{2}}
.
Denote by

B_{j}\coloneq\{1\leq i<i_{2}\mid\deg v_{i}=j\},\qquad j=0,2,

the indices of the vertices below
v_{i_{2}}
with degree
j
.
Then by choice of
v_{i_{2}}
, every
v_{i}
with
i\in B_{2}
ends and immediate recollision, implying that
i-1\in B_{0}
and
\Theta_{i-1}=-\Theta_{i}
.
The remaining indices in
B_{0}
that do not belong to an immediate recollision are

B_{0}^{\prime}\coloneq\{j\in B_{0}\mid j+1\notin B_{2}\}.

Since both of the time slices
j_{0}-1
and
j_{0}
are long, we must have that
j_{0}\in B_{0}^{\prime}
.
Continuing to rewrite the phase factor from before, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i}\sum_{j=i+1}^{i_{2}}\Theta_{j}=\sum_{j\in B_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{j_{0}\}}\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}r_{i}\Theta_{j}+(\Theta_{i_{2}}+\Theta_{j_{0}})\sum_{i=0}^{j_{0}-1}r_{i}+\sum_{j\in B_{2}}\Theta_{j}r_{j-1}+\Theta_{i_{2}}\sum_{i=j_{0}}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i},

where by
Lemma

4.17
, the terms in the first two sums do not depend on any free momenta ending before or at
v_{i_{2}}
.
We conclude that the phase factor (
6.61
) can be written

(6.62)

\sum_{i=0}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i}\gamma(i)=\tilde{\zeta}(r)+\sum_{j\in B_{2}}\Theta_{j}r_{j-1}+\Theta_{i_{2}}\sum_{i=j_{0}}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i},

where
\tilde{\zeta}(r)
is independent of all free momenta ending before or at
v_{i_{2}}
and
\mathrm{Im}\tilde{\zeta}(r)\leq 0
.

We can now follow the basic iterative scheme with a few modifications.
The idea is to integrate out the free momenta of the vertices below
v_{i_{2}}
before taking absolute values inside the remaining integrals.
Recall from earlier the sets of time slice indices

A_{j}\coloneq\{0\leq i<N\mid\deg v_{i+1}=j\}

for
j=0,2
, where now
\lvert A_{j}\rvert=N/2
since the graph is fully paired.
To decompose the time integral, we now take

A\coloneq\{N\}\cup\{i_{2}\leq i<N\mid\deg v_{i+1}=2\},

and

A^{\prime}\coloneq\{\ast\}\cup I_{0,i_{2}-1}\cup A_{0},

and write, using
Lemma

5.1
,

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{0,N}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j=0}^{N}r_{j}\Big)\prod_{j=0}^{N}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\oint_{\Gamma_{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi}\prod_{j\in A}\frac{i}{z-\gamma(j)}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\Big)e^{-ir_{\ast}z}\prod_{j\in I_{0,i_{2}-1}\cup A_{0}}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\oint_{\Gamma_{N}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi}\frac{i}{z}\prod_{i_{2}\leq j\in A_{2}}\frac{i}{z-\gamma(j)}\begin{aligned} &\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\Big)\\
&\times e^{-ir_{\ast}z}e^{-i\sum_{j=0}^{i_{2}-1}r_{j}\gamma(j)}\prod_{i_{2}\leq j\in A_{0}}e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j)}.\end{aligned}

Now, inserting (
6.62
) into this expression, we may integrate out all the double loop momenta of the immediate recollision vertices below
v_{i_{2}}
.
Each of these double loop integrals corresponds to a
G_{r,\tau}^{\lambda}
-factor as in (
6.38
), which in total yields a factor
G_{1}^{\lambda}(k,r)
that depends only on the recollision time variables
r_{j}
with
j\in\{i\mid i+1\in B_{2}\}
, and only on the free momenta of the vertices
v_{i_{2}}
and above.
Note that
G_{1}^{\lambda}
is a product of factors of the form as in
Proposition

6.11
, and not all of them have to depend on the free momenta of
v_{i_{2}}
.
Taking absolute values inside all remaining integrals except for the double loop integral of
v_{i_{2}}
, and estimating
\lvert e^{-ir_{\ast}z}\rvert\leq e^{s\lambda^{2}}
,
\lvert e^{-i\tilde{\zeta}(r)}\rvert\leq 1
, and
\lvert e^{-ir_{j}\gamma(j)}\rvert\leq 1
for all remaining
j\in A_{0}
above
v_{i_{2}}
, we arrive for instance at an expression of the form

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{F}_{n}^{err,pairs}\rvert\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\lambda^{N}\lVert\widehat{V}\rVert_{\infty}^{N-2(\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1)}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k_{N}\lvert\hat{g}(k_{N})\rvert^{2}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N-2(\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1)}}\prod_{i_{2}<j\in A_{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{j,1}\mathrm{d}k_{j,2}

\displaystyle\times\oint_{\Gamma_{N}}\mathrm{d}z\frac{e^{\lambda^{2}s}}{\lvert z\rvert}\prod_{i_{2}\leq j\in A_{2}}\frac{1}{\lvert z-\gamma(j)\rvert}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\Big)

(6.63)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\lvert\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}e^{-i\Theta_{i_{2}}\sum_{i=j_{0}}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i}}U_{j_{0}}U_{i_{2}}\Big[\prod W^{0}\Big]G_{1}^{\lambda}(k,r)\bigg\rvert,

where
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
denote the free momenta of
v_{i_{2}}
,
\prod W^{0}
contains the remaining factors of
W^{0}
that might depend on
k_{1},k_{2}
, and
U_{j_{0}}
,
U_{i_{2}}
are the interaction factors of
v_{j_{0}}
and
v_{i_{2}}
.
Since the double loop of
v_{i_{2}}
corresponds to that of a leading motive by
Lemma

4.17
, the interaction factor
U_{j_{0}}U_{i_{2}}
is of the same form as the allowed factors in (
6.38
), and hence the integral over
k_{1},k_{2}
also gives rise to a
G_{r,\tau}^{\lambda}
-factor, except it depends on a sum of time variables
\sum_{i=j_{0}}^{i_{2}-1}r_{i}
.
The resulting factor, which we denote by
G_{2}^{\lambda}(k,r)
, depends on the time variables corresponding to the set
B\coloneq I_{j_{0},i_{2}-1}\cup\{i\mid i+1\in B_{2}\}
, but we note that
only
the last application of
G_{r,\tau}^{\lambda}
, corresponding to the double loop of the nest, depends on any of the long time slices.

We will estimate the integral of
G_{2}^{\lambda}
using
Proposition

6.11
by first integrating out the time slice
i_{2}-1
along with all the long time slices in the nest of
v_{i_{2}}
.
Since the time slice
j_{0}
is long, we have
M\coloneq\lvert\{i_{2}-1\}\cup(A_{0}\cap I_{j_{0},i_{2}-1})\rvert\geq 2
time variables to integrate in this step, which allows us to use the dispersive estimate of
(DR2)
to obtain the extra decay in
\lambda
that we need for the nested graphs.
Afterwards, we iterate through the short time slice integrals going from the top of the nest and downwards.
Applying (
6.48
), we thus have to estimate

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\Big)\big\lvert G_{2}^{\lambda}(k,r)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}\setminus B}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}\setminus B}r_{j}\Big)\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{B}}\mathrm{d}r\big\lvert G_{2}^{\lambda}(k,r)\big\rvert{\mathds{1}}\Big(\sum_{j\in B}r_{j}\leq s\Big)

(6.64)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{s^{\tilde{n}}}{\tilde{n}!}C^{\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1}\lVert V\rVert_{1}^{2(\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1)}\begin{aligned} &\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{B}}\mathrm{d}r{\mathds{1}}\Big(\sum_{j\in B}r_{j}\leq s\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{i_{2}-1}r_{j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{j\in A_{2}}\\
\scriptscriptstyle{j<i_{2}-1}\end{subarray}}\tau_{i_{2}-1,i,j}r_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{3}\\
&\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in A_{2}\\
m<i_{2}-1\end{subarray}}\bigg({\mathds{1}}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in A_{2}\\
j<m\end{subarray}}r_{j}\leq s\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{r_{m}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{j\in A_{2}}\\
\scriptscriptstyle{j<m}\end{subarray}}\tau_{m,i,j}r_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{3}\bigg),\end{aligned}

with
\tilde{n}=\lvert A^{\prime}\setminus B\rvert-1=\lvert A_{0}\setminus I_{j_{0},i_{2}-1}\rvert
and
\tau_{m,i,j}\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
Using Hölder’s inequality and
(DR2)
, the integral over
r_{i_{2}-1}
and the long time slices is bounded by an integral of the type

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}}\mathrm{d}r{\mathds{1}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{M}r_{i}\leq s\Big)\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{M}r_{i}+\alpha\Big\rangle^{-1-\delta}={}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\frac{\big\lvert\{\sum_{i=1}^{M}r_{i}=t\}\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}\big\rvert}{\langle t+\alpha\rangle^{1+\delta}}

\displaystyle={}\frac{\sqrt{M}}{(M-1)!}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\frac{t^{M-1}}{\langle t+\alpha\rangle^{1+\delta}}\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{M}s^{M-2}}{(M-1)!}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\frac{t}{\langle t+\alpha\rangle^{1+\delta}},

where

-s\leq-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in A_{2}\\
j<i_{2}-1\end{subarray}}r_{j}\leq\alpha\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in A_{2}\\
j_{0}<j<i_{2}-1\end{subarray}}r_{j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in A_{2}\\
j<i_{2}-1\end{subarray}}r_{j}\leq 2s.

Using
2\delta\geq\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}<1
, we have

(6.65)

\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\frac{t}{\langle t+\alpha\rangle^{1+\delta}}\leq{}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\frac{1}{\langle t+\alpha\rangle^{\delta}}+\alpha_{-}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}t\frac{1}{\langle t+\alpha\rangle^{1+\delta}}\leq{}C(s^{1-\gamma^{\prime}/2}+\alpha_{-}),

which we now plug back into (
6.64
).
For the term involving
s^{1-\gamma^{\prime}/2}
, we simply use that the remaining time integrals in (
6.64
) are bounded by
Proposition

6.11
.
For the term involving
\alpha_{-}\leq\sum_{j\in A_{2};j<i_{2}-1}r_{j}
, we have to iterate through the time integrals of each of the remaining degree two vertices, from the top of the nest and down.
If
m
is the short time slice corresponding to the next vertex, there are now exactly three factors left in (
6.64
) that depend on
r_{m}
, as well as a contribution from
\alpha_{-}
.
We save all other time variables appearing in
\alpha_{-}
for later iterations.
We estimate the
r_{m}
-integral by Hölder,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}r_{m}\,r_{m}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\big\lVert p_{r_{m}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{j\in A_{2}}\\
\scriptscriptstyle{j<m}\end{subarray}}\tau_{m,i,j}r_{j}}^{\lambda}\big\rVert_{3}\leq{}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\bigg(\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}r_{m}\,r_{m}\Big\langle r_{m}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in A_{2}\\
j<m\end{subarray}}\tau_{m,i,j}r_{j}\Big\rangle^{-1-\delta}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}},

where each of the factors is of the same form as in (
6.65
) for some
\alpha_{i}
with
\lvert\alpha_{i}\rvert\leq\sum_{j\in A_{2};j<m}r_{j}\leq s
, allowing us to continue the iteration in the next step.
Noting that each iteration may add additional terms that depend on the time slices below (through the
\alpha_{i}
), and that there are at most
\lvert B_{2}+1\rvert\leq\lvert A_{2}\rvert=\lvert A_{0}\rvert
steps to be carried out, we find that (
6.64
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{A^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}r\delta\Big(s-\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}r_{j}\Big)\big\lvert G_{2}^{\lambda}(k,r)\big\rvert\lesssim{}

\displaystyle C^{\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1}\lVert V\rVert_{1}^{2(\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1)}\frac{\sqrt{M}\lvert A_{0}\rvert^{2}}{\tilde{n}!(M-1)!}s^{\tilde{n}+M-1-\gamma^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2C^{\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1}\lVert V\rVert_{1}^{2(\lvert B_{2}\rvert+1)}s^{N/2-\gamma^{\prime}/2},

since
\tilde{n}+M-1=\lvert A_{0}\setminus I_{j_{0},i_{2}-1}\rvert+\lvert A_{0}\cap I_{j_{0},i_{2}-1}\rvert=\lvert A_{0}\rvert=N/2
.

Finally, going back to (
6.63
), we may now iterate through the remaining degree two vertices above
v_{i_{2}}
as in the scheme of the basic estimates.
We find that the amplitude is bounded by

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{F}_{n}^{err,pairs}\rvert\leq{}

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}C^{N/2}\lVert V\rVert_{1}^{N}\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}/2}e^{s\lambda^{2}}(s\lambda^{2})^{N/2-\gamma^{\prime}/2}\langle\ln N\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+N}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}/2}e^{s\lambda^{2}}\langle cs\lambda^{2}\rangle^{N/2}\langle\ln N\rangle\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{1+N},

concluding the proof.
∎

7.
Completion of the proof of the main theorem

We are now able to finish the proof of
Theorem

2.2
.
We need the following lemma providing a bound on the number of leading graphs, but we refer to
[
13
, Lemma 10.1]
for the proof.

Lemma 7.1
.

Consider a momentum graph with
n^{\prime}
interaction vertices in the minus tree and
n
interaction vertices in the plus tree.
If
n+n^{\prime}
is odd, the graph is not leading.
If
n+n^{\prime}
is even, there are at most

(7.1)

4^{n+n^{\prime}}(n+n^{\prime}-1)!!\leq{}8^{n+n^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{n+n^{\prime}}{2}\Big)!

leading graphs, where we define
(-1)!!=1
.

Note also that the total number of pairing graphs with
n
interaction vertices is bounded by
5n(2n)!
.
This follows easily from the facts that there are
(2n)!!=2^{n}n!
ways of distributing the interaction vertices across the plus and minus trees, and there are
\frac{(2n+2)!}{2^{n+1}(n+1)!}
ways of dividing the
2n+2
initial vertices into sets of
n+1
pairs.

Letting
c_{0}
denote the constant from
Proposition

6.14
, we choose
t_{0}=(2^{6}c_{0})^{-1}
.
Then, for any
0<t<t_{0}
and
0<s\leq t\lambda^{-2}
, the series
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(2^{6}c_{0}\lambda^{2}s)^{m}
is convergent.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
t_{0}
be the constant specified above, and assume
0<t<t_{0}
, it follows that

(7.2)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\limsup_{L\to\infty}\big\lvert Q^{\lambda}[g,f](t)-Q^{main,pair}[g,f](t)\big\rvert={}0,

Where
Q^{main,pair}[g,f](t)
is given by (
6.25
).
Moreover, we also have

(7.3)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\bigg\lvert Q^{main,pair}[g,f](t)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=0,\\
n\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{N_{0}-1}\sum_{\text{leading graphs}}\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\bigg\rvert={}0.

Proof.

Returning to (
3.25
), we have

\displaystyle\big\lvert Q^{\lambda}-Q^{main,pair}\big\rvert\leq{}\lvert Q^{main}-Q^{main,pair}\rvert+\lvert Q^{err}_{1}\rvert+\lvert Q^{err}_{2}\rvert+\lvert Q^{err}_{3}\rvert,

where the first, third, and fourth terms vanish in the limit, by
Proposition

6.7
,
Proposition

6.5
, and
Proposition

6.4
, respectively.
It also follows from
Proposition

6.6
that we only need to consider fully paired graphs in order to bound
\lvert Q^{err}_{1}\rvert
.
Now, considering for instance the nested graphs (of which there are at most
5N_{0}(2N_{0})!
), we get by combining
Lemma

6.12
and
Proposition

6.15
that the total contribution from these is bounded by

Ct^{2}\langle ct\rangle^{N_{0}}\lambda^{\gamma^{\prime}}N_{0}^{4+2b_{0}+1}(2N_{0})!\langle\ln\lambda\rangle^{2+N_{0}},

which vanishes as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
, in view of (
5.6
).
Similarly, applying
Proposition

6.8
shows that the contribution from the crossing graphs also vanishes in the limit.
For the remaining graphs, which are leading, we use
Proposition

6.14
combined with
Lemma

6.12
(to remove the cut-off
\Phi_{1}
), as well as the fact that there are at most
2^{6n}n!
such graphs, by
Lemma

7.1
.
The contribution coming from removing the cut-off is handled in the same way as the contributions from the nested and crossing graphs.
The final term that we need to bound is thus

N_{0}^{2+2b_{0}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\lambda^{-2}\\
N_{0}/2\leq n<N_{0}\end{subarray}}2^{6n}(c_{0}\lambda^{2}s)^{n}\leq{}N_{0}^{2+2b_{0}}\sup_{N_{0}/2\leq n<N_{0}}\Big(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\Big)^{n}\leq{}N_{0}^{2+2b_{0}}\Big(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\Big)^{\frac{N_{0}}{2}},

which tends to zero as
\lambda\to 0^{+}
.
This shows that (
7.2
) holds.

The statement (
7.3
) about
Q^{main,pair}
is proved in the same way, starting from
Proposition

6.7
, and then using
Proposition

6.8
and
Proposition

6.15
to bound the contributions from the crossing and nested graphs.
Finally, applying
Lemma

6.12
allows us to remove the momentum cut-off
\Phi_{1}
for the remaining leading graphs.
∎

Finally, the proof of
Theorem

2.2
will be completed after computing the sum of the contributions from the leading diagrams in the weak-coupling limit.
Note that combining
Lemma

7.1
with
Proposition

6.14
shows that for each even
n
,

\sum_{\text{leading graphs}}\Big\lvert\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}\Big\rvert\leq c\Big(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\Big)^{\frac{n}{2}},

implying for
t<t_{0}
that we can move the
\lambda\to 0
limit inside the sum over
n
in (
7.3
).
After combining with the lemma below, this finishes the proof of
Theorem

2.2
.

Lemma 7.3

(Convergence of leading terms)
.

For any even integer
n=2m
, and with
\nu
as in (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sum_{\text{leading graphs}}\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{n}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}

(7.4)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)W^{0}(k)\nu(k)^{n/2}\frac{(-t)^{n/2}}{(n/2)!}.

Proof.

Take first
\lambda
small enough so that
N_{0}(\lambda)>4m
.
This ensures that
\kappa_{j}=0
for all
j
appearing in the expression of
\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{2m}
, by the choice of parameters (
5.4
).
Proceeding as in the proof of (
6.56
), we combine (
6.57
) and (
6.58
) to immediately obtain by dominated convergence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{2m}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}

\displaystyle={}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m!}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}}\mathrm{d}s\widetilde{G}^{\lambda}(k,s;S,\ell)\Big(t-\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\Big)^{m}{\mathds{1}}\Big(\lambda^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\leq t\Big)

(7.5)

\displaystyle\to{}\frac{(-t)^{m}}{m!}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}}\mathrm{d}s\widetilde{G}^{0}(k,s;S,\ell),

as
\lambda\to 0
.
Recall here that
\widetilde{G}^{\lambda}(k,s;S,\ell)
is an
m
-fold iteration of
G_{s_{i},\tau_{i}}
-factors as in (
6.38
), and that
\widetilde{G}^{\lambda}
has an
s
-integrable upper bound which is independent of
k
and
\lambda
, by
Proposition

6.11
.

We will argue by induction that (
7.4
) holds, or, more specifically, that

(7.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m-\text{motive}\\
\text{leading graphs}\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}}\mathrm{d}s\widetilde{G}^{0}(k,s;S,\ell)={}W^{0}(k)\nu(k)^{m}.

For
m=0
, we have the amplitude for the trivial graph consisting of a single loop,

\mathcal{F}_{0}^{main,pair}(t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)={}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)W^{0}(k)e^{-\frac{t}{\lambda^{2}}\kappa_{0}}={}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)W^{0}(k),

since in this case
\gamma_{0}=-i\kappa_{0}=0
by (
3.50
) and our choice of
\kappa
(
5.4
).

Assume now that (
7.4
) holds for
m-1
, and note that the set of leading graphs with
m
motives is obtained by adding a single leading motive to a graph with
m-1
motives in such a way that the resulting graph has no interaction vertices in the minus tree, and each
m
-motive leading graph can be obtained like this in exactly one way.
This means that we can split the sum in (
7.4
) into a double sum, where we first fix a graph with
m-1
motives and sum over all the ways of adding a leading motive to this fixed graph, and then afterwards sum over all graphs with
m-1
motives.

Thus we fix a graph with
m-1
motives and consider all the ways of adding a leading motive to its pairing clusters.
Since the resulting graph should contain no interaction vertices in the minus tree, when adding a motive to the special pairing that connects the minus tree to the plus tree (whose left leg has parity -1), we can only use six of the loss motives, L1,…,L6 (recall the leading motives in
Fig.

5
).

For the remaining pairing clusters there are no restrictions, and we may use any of the
16
leading motives suitable for any given cluster (recall that only half of the gain motives may be attached to any given pairing).
We consider first the special pairing connecting the plus and minus trees and show that summing over the ways of adding a motive to this pairing results in the right hand side of (
7.4
).
Finally, we will argue that the contributions from adding motives to all the other pairings exactly cancel each other.

Thus, denoting by
k_{0}
the momentum corresponding to the right leg of the pairing connecting to the minus tree, we compute the contribution from adding the admissible loss motives using
Lemma

6.9
, which changes the factor
W^{0}(k_{0})
to

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\sum_{j=1}^{6}F_{Lj}(s_{1},k,-1)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})e^{-is_{1}\Theta(k,1)}W_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\begin{aligned} \times\Big(&\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}W_{2}W_{3}+\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})W_{1}W_{3}\\
&+\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})W_{2}W_{3}\\
&-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}W_{1}W_{3}-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\Big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-W_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})e^{-is_{1}\Theta(k,1)}

(7.7)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\big(\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})\big)\big(W_{2}W_{3}-W_{1}W_{3}+W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-W^{0}(k_{0})\nu(k_{0})

where we have used that the change of variables
k_{2}\leftrightarrow k_{3}
leaves the phase
\Theta(k,1)
invariant.
Now summing over all graphs with
m-1
leading motives, we obtain from (
7.5
) and the induction hypothesis the total contribution

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sum_{\mathcal{G}_{m}}\mathcal{F}^{main,pair}_{2m}(S,\ell,t\lambda^{-2},\kappa)\Big|_{\Phi_{1}\to 1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{(-t)^{m}}{m!}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)\nu(k)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m-1)-\text{motive}\\
\text{leading graphs}\end{subarray}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I_{m-1}}}\mathrm{d}s\widetilde{G}(k,s;S,\ell)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}k\hat{g}(k)^{\ast}\hat{f}(k)W^{0}(k)\nu(k)^{m}\frac{(-t)^{m}}{m!},

where
\mathcal{G}_{m}
is the subset of the
m
-motive leading graphs obtained by attaching one of the six admissible loss motives to an
m-1
-motive graph at the pairing connecting to the minus tree.

As mentioned above, we still need to show that the contributions from all other leading graphs cancel out, so fix a graph consisting of
m-1
leading motives, as well as any pairing at the bottom of the graph which does not connect to the minus tree.
Denote by
\sigma
the parity of the left leg of the pairing, and by
k_{0}
the momentum corresponding to this leg.
As above, we want to sum up all the possible contributions from attaching a leading motive to this pairing, but this time there are
16
possibilities.
Assuming for simplicity that
\sigma=-1
, we collect first the terms from
Lemma

6.9
with the same phase and continue from (
7.7
),

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{6}F_{Lj}(r,k,-1)+F_{G2^{-}}(r,k,-1)+F_{G4^{-}}(r,k,-1)\bigg)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})e^{-ir\Theta(k,1)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} &\times\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})\big(\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})\big)\\
&\times\big(\underbrace{W_{0}\big(W_{2}W_{3}-W_{1}W_{3}+W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\big)-\widetilde{W}_{1}W_{2}W_{3}}_{\mathrlap{\!=\widetilde{W}_{0}\widetilde{W}_{1}W_{2}W_{3}-W_{0}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\widetilde{W}_{3}}}\big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})e^{-ir\Theta(k,1)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})\big)^{2}\big(\widetilde{W}_{0}\widetilde{W}_{1}W_{2}W_{3}-W_{0}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\widetilde{W}_{3}\big),

where we in the last equality do a change of variables
k_{2}\leftrightarrow k_{3}
to complete the square.
Similarly, we get the same expression for the sum of the remaining terms, but with a flipped sign in the phase of the exponential factor, giving the total contribution

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\big(e^{-ir\Theta(k,1)}+e^{ir\Theta(k,1)}\big)

(7.8)

\displaystyle\times\big(\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})\big)^{2}\big(\widetilde{W}_{0}\widetilde{W}_{1}W_{2}W_{3}-W_{0}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\widetilde{W}_{3}\big).

We recognise, at least formally, the Boltzmann-Nordheim collision operator (
2.14
), so since
W^{0}
is a stationary solution to the Boltzmann-Nordheim equation, this should vanish.
Now, rewriting and using the explicit expression (
2.9
) of
W^{0}
with
\beta=1/T
denoting the inverse temperature,

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{0}\widetilde{W}_{1}W_{2}W_{3}-W_{0}W_{1}\widetilde{W}_{2}\widetilde{W}_{3}={}

\displaystyle W_{0}W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\Big(\frac{1}{W_{0}}-1\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{W_{1}}-1\Big)-\Big(\frac{1}{W_{2}}-1\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{W_{3}}-1\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle W_{0}W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}\big(e^{\beta(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1})}-e^{\beta(\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})}\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle W_{0}W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}e^{\beta(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1})}(1-e^{\beta\Theta(k,1)}),

so doing the time integral in (
7.8
) from
0
to some
M>0
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\frac{i\beta}{2}\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}}\mathrm{d}k\delta(k_{0}-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3})\big(e^{-iM\Theta(k,1)}-e^{iM\Theta(k,1)}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big(\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{2})-\widehat{V}(k_{1}+k_{3})\big)^{2}W_{0}W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}e^{\beta(\omega_{0}+\omega_{1})}\frac{1-e^{\beta\Theta(k,1)}}{\beta\Theta(k,1)}.

With our assumptions on
V
and
\omega
, the integrand (the expression on the second line above) is smooth and bounded on
(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{3}
, so it is the Fourier transform of a function in
\ell^{1}((\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{3})
.
We conclude by
Proposition

5.3
that the limit above is zero, so the contributions coming from attaching a leading motive to our fixed pairing (assuming that
\sigma=-1
) cancel each other.
The argument when
\sigma=1
is exactly the same, so we conclude that (
7.6
) holds, and combining with (
7.5
) finishes the proof of (
7.4
), and hence also of
Theorem

2.2
.
∎
